# Glossary

Terms taught in weeks 1–36, listed by subject. Each entry links to the lesson that teaches it, where the Frayer card (definition, origin when it is known, example, near-miss, and a connection) is given. Use this page to look a word up; use the week-end vocabulary reviews to retrieve it from memory.

Pronunciations are on the lesson page (Hear, in English). Spanish and Latin *words* still have their own Hear-word buttons in those lessons.

## Mathematics

- **[a downward-opening quadratic inequality](days/d53.html#term-downward-opening-quadratic-inequality)** — Day 53, Mathematics · Algebra XVIII: a downward-opening parabola, and a square. When the x² coefficient is negative, the already-taught parabola opens downward, so the regions swap: y > 0 between the roots, and y < 0 outside them.
- **[a function not continuous at a point where it is not defined](days/d167.html#term-a-function-not-continuous-at-a-point-where-it-is-not-defined)** — Day 167, Mathematics · A function not continuous at a point where it is not defined. If a function is not defined at a, it is not continuous at a, even when the limit as x approaches a exists.
- **[a height found by the Pythagorean theorem](days/d72.html#term-height-by-pythagoras)** — Day 72, Mathematics · Geometry XXXI: a height from Pythagoras, then the area. When a perpendicular height of a triangle is not given, but it forms a right triangle with known sides, that height can be found from a² + b² = c², and then the area is ½bh.
- **[a limit as a positive integer grows large](days/d158.html#term-a-limit-as-a-positive-integer-grows-large)** — Day 158, Mathematics · Algebra / analysis: a limit as a positive integer grows large, a first look. A limit may also describe what happens as a positive integer n grows large.
- **[a limit where the function is not defined at the point](days/d157.html#term-a-limit-where-the-function-is-not-defined-at-the-point)** — Day 157, Mathematics · Algebra / analysis: a limit where the function is not defined at the point, a first look. A limit may exist at a number a even when f(a) itself is not defined.
- **[a limit, a first look](days/d156.html#term-a-limit-a-first-look)** — Day 156, Mathematics · Algebra / analysis: a limit, a first look. A limit, in this first look, is a value that a quantity approaches as an input approaches a chosen number.
- **[a line and a parabola, as a system](days/d48.html#term-line-and-parabola-as-a-system)** — Day 48, Mathematics · Algebra XVI: a line and a parabola, as a system. Two equations together, one the already-taught parabola y = ax² + bx + c (often y = x² in a first look) and one a line, solved by substitution.
- **[a line and a plane in space](days/d121.html#term-a-line-and-a-plane-in-space)** — Day 121, Mathematics · Geometry LXVII: a line and a plane in space, a first look. In space, a line and a plane have three possible relations: the line may lie in the plane, meet the plane at exactly one point, or be parallel to the plane and never meet it.
- **[a line perpendicular to a plane](days/d122.html#term-a-line-perpendicular-to-a-plane)** — Day 122, Mathematics · Geometry LXVIII: a line perpendicular to a plane, a first look. A line is perpendicular to a plane if it meets the plane at a point P, the foot, and is perpendicular to every line in the plane that passes through P.
- **[a missing side in a right triangle](days/d68.html#term-missing-side-right-triangle)** — Day 68, Mathematics · Geometry XXXI: a missing side, from a² + b² = c². A leg or the hypotenuse of a right triangle, found from the Pythagorean theorem when the other two sides are known.
- **[a perfect-square inequality](days/d53.html#term-perfect-square-inequality)** — Day 53, Mathematics · Algebra XVIII: a downward-opening parabola, and a square. A quadratic that is a square, such as (x − 3)², is never negative.
- **[a short mix of this week’s algebra and geometry](days/d54.html#term-short-mix-algebra-geometry-w11)** — Day 54, Mathematics · Geometry XXIV: a short mix — a tangent-secant, and an inequality. A sitting that uses Monday’s and Wednesday’s quadratic inequalities and Tuesday’s tangent-secant theorem, each in its own picture, without mixing the theorems into one invented hybrid.
- **[AA similarity](days/d62.html#term-aa-similarity)** — Day 62, Mathematics · Geometry XXVI: AA similarity. If two angles of one triangle are equal to two angles of another triangle, the third angles are equal as well (because the angle sum is 180°), and the triangles are similar.
- **[alternate segment theorem (III.32)](days/d36.html#term-alternate-segment-iii32)** — Day 36, Mathematics · Geometry XVII: the tangent-chord angle (Euclid III.32). The angle between a tangent and a chord, at the point of contact, equals the inscribed angle in the alternate segment of the circle (the segment on the other side of the chord).
- **[altitude to the hypotenuse](days/d67.html#term-altitude-to-the-hypotenuse)** — Day 67, Mathematics · Geometry XXX: a similar-triangle proof of the Pythagorean theorem. The perpendicular from the right angle to the hypotenuse in a right triangle.
- **[AM–GM inequality](days/d07.html#term-am-gm)** — Day 7, Mathematics · Algebra III: inequalities and the mean. For a, b ≥ 0, (a + b)/2 ≥ √(ab), with equality only when a = b.
- **[angle bisector](days/d12.html#term-angle-bisector)** — Day 12, Mathematics · Geometry VI: the angle bisector and a parallel through a point. The ray that divides an angle into two equal angles.
- **[angle in a semicircle](days/d22.html#term-angle-in-a-semicircle)** — Day 22, Mathematics · Geometry XI: the angle in a semicircle, and angles in the same segment. An angle inscribed in a semicircle — the angle at the point C on the circle when AB is a diameter — is a right angle.
- **[angles in the same segment](days/d22.html#term-angles-same-segment)** — Day 22, Mathematics · Geometry XI: the angle in a semicircle, and angles in the same segment. Inscribed angles that stand on the same arc (and so lie in the same segment of the circle) are equal.
- **[annulus](days/d84.html#term-annulus)** — Day 84, Mathematics · Geometry XLI: mixed circle measure, and a ring. An annulus, or ring, is the region between two circles that share a center.
- **[apothem](days/d79.html#term-apothem)** — Day 79, Mathematics · Geometry XXXVII: a regular polygon’s area, and the apothem. The apothem of a regular polygon is the perpendicular from the center to the middle of a side.
- **[area of a circle](days/d82.html#term-area-of-a-circle)** — Day 82, Mathematics · Geometry XXXIX: the area of a circle. The area of a circle of radius r is A = πr², with the same π taught yesterday as C / d.
- **[area of a circular segment](days/d154.html#term-area-of-a-circular-segment)** — Day 154, Mathematics · Geometry XCIV: the area of a circular segment, a first look. A circular segment, in this lesson, is the region between an arc and the chord that joins the arc’s ends.
- **[area of a parallelogram](days/d76.html#term-area-of-a-parallelogram)** — Day 76, Mathematics · Geometry XXXIV: the area of a parallelogram. The area of a parallelogram is the base times the corresponding perpendicular height: A = b × h.
- **[area of a regular polygon](days/d79.html#term-area-of-a-regular-polygon)** — Day 79, Mathematics · Geometry XXXVII: a regular polygon’s area, and the apothem. A regular polygon divides into n congruent isosceles triangles from the center.
- **[area of a right triangle](days/d73.html#term-area-of-a-right-triangle)** — Day 73, Mathematics · Geometry XXXII: the area of a right triangle. In a right triangle the two legs are perpendicular, so either leg may be taken as the base and the other as the height.
- **[area of a sector in radians](days/d151.html#term-area-of-a-sector-in-radians)** — Day 151, Mathematics · Geometry XCI: the area of a sector in radians, a first look. The area of a sector of a circle of radius r, with central angle θ measured in radians, is A = (1/2)r²θ.
- **[area of a trapezoid](days/d78.html#term-area-of-a-trapezoid)** — Day 78, Mathematics · Geometry XXXVI: the area of a trapezoid. The area of a trapezoid is the average of the two parallel bases times the perpendicular height: A = ½(a + b) × h.
- **[area of a triangle](days/d71.html#term-area-of-a-triangle)** — Day 71, Mathematics · Geometry XXX: the area of a triangle. The number of unit squares a triangle covers.
- **[area of a triangle as (1/2)ab sin C](days/d132.html#term-area-of-a-triangle-as-one-half-ab-sin-c)** — Day 132, Mathematics · Geometry LXXVI: the area of a triangle as (1/2)ab sin C, a first look. The area of a triangle is (1/2)ab sin C, where C is the included angle between sides a and b.
- **[arithmetic progression](days/d20.html#term-arithmetic-progression)** — Day 20, Mathematics · Two-week problem set: constructions to tangents, and a first look at progressions. A sequence in which each term after the first is obtained by adding the same fixed number, the common difference d.
- **[axiom](days/d02.html#term-axiom)** — Day 2, Mathematics · Geometry I: definitions, axioms and the first proofs. A starting statement accepted without proof, so that theorems can be proved from it.
- **[bar model](days/d01.html#term-bar-model)** — Day 1, Mathematics · Modeling: from bar model to equation. A drawing of quantities as bars of equal units, so the relationship is visible before any equation is written.
- **[central angle](days/d21.html#term-central-angle)** — Day 21, Mathematics · Geometry X: central and inscribed angles. An angle whose vertex is the center of a circle, and whose sides pass through two points of the circle.
- **[chord](days/d18.html#term-chord)** — Day 18, Mathematics · Geometry IX: the circle, chords. A segment joining two points of a circle.
- **[circle through three points (review)](days/d29.html#term-circle-through-three-points)** — Day 29, Mathematics · Geometry XV: the circumcircle, revisited with these two weeks’ theorems. A unique circle passes through three points that are not on a single line (the circumcircle of the triangle they form).
- **[circumference of a circle](days/d81.html#term-circumference-of-a-circle)** — Day 81, Mathematics · Geometry XXXVIII: the circumference of a circle. The circumference of a circle is the length of the circle itself, the distance once around.
- **[complementary tangents](days/d113.html#term-complementary-tangents)** — Day 113, Mathematics · Geometry LXV: tangent of complementary angles. If A and B are the two acute angles of a right triangle, already known to add to 90°, then tan A = 1 / tan B, because the side opposite A is adjacent to B, and the side adjacent to A is opposite B.
- **[completing the square](days/d05.html#term-completing-the-square)** — Day 5, Mathematics · Algebra II: quadratic equations. Rewriting x² + px as a square plus or minus a constant, so a quadratic can be solved or a parabola’s vertex read off.
- **[composition of functions](days/d41.html#term-composition-of-functions)** — Day 41, Mathematics · Algebra XIII: composition of functions. The function (f ∘ g) that sends x to f(g(x)): first apply g, then apply f to that result.
- **[congruence △ABC ≅ △DEF](days/d04.html#term-congruence)** — Day 4, Mathematics · Geometry II: congruent triangles and the isosceles triangle. A matching of two figures so that corresponding sides and corresponding angles are equal; one can be placed exactly on the other.
- **[construction](days/d11.html#term-construction)** — Day 11, Mathematics · Geometry V: constructions with compass and straightedge. A drawing made with only an unmarked straightedge and a compass, justified by axioms and earlier theorems, not by measuring with a ruler.
- **[continuous at a point, a first look](days/d166.html#term-continuous-at-a-point-a-first-look)** — Day 166, Mathematics · Continuous at a point, a first look. A function f is continuous at a number a, in this first look, when f is defined at a and the limit of f(x) as x approaches a equals f(a).
- **[converse of the Pythagorean theorem](days/d69.html#term-converse-pythagorean-theorem)** — Day 69, Mathematics · Geometry XXXII: the converse, and more than one integer triple. If the sides a, b, and c of a triangle satisfy a² + b² = c², then the triangle is right-angled at the angle opposite c.
- **[cosine of an acute angle](days/d107.html#term-cosine-of-an-acute-angle)** — Day 107, Mathematics · Geometry LX: cosine of an acute angle, a first look. In a right triangle, the cosine of an acute angle A, written cos A, is the length of the side adjacent to A, but not the hypotenuse, divided by the length of the hypotenuse.
- **[cyclic quadrilateral](days/d23.html#term-cyclic-quadrilateral)** — Day 23, Mathematics · Geometry XII: cyclic quadrilaterals. A quadrilateral whose four vertices all lie on a single circle.
- **[diagonal](days/d14.html#term-diagonal)** — Day 14, Mathematics · Geometry VII: the parallelogram. A segment joining two vertices of a polygon that are not already joined by a side.
- **[discriminant D = b² − 4ac](days/d05.html#term-discriminant)** — Day 5, Mathematics · Algebra II: quadratic equations. The quantity that decides how many real roots a quadratic has: two if D > 0, one if D = 0, none if D < 0.
- **[distance from the center](days/d18.html#term-central-distance)** — Day 18, Mathematics · Geometry IX: the circle, chords. The perpendicular distance from the center of a circle to a chord.
- **[equation of a circle](days/d42.html#term-equation-of-a-circle)** — Day 42, Mathematics · Geometry XIX: the equation of a circle. The coordinate form of the locus of points at a fixed distance r from a center (h, k): (x − h)² + (y − k)² = r².
- **[exponent an](days/d16.html#term-exponent)** — Day 16, Mathematics · Algebra V: the laws of exponents. The small number that says how many times a base is used as a factor: in aⁿ, a is the base and n is the exponent.
- **[exterior angle](days/d06.html#term-exterior-angle)** — Day 6, Mathematics · Geometry III: parallel lines and the angle sum. An angle formed by one side of a triangle and the extension of an adjacent side.
- **[f(x)](days/d31.html#term-f-of-x)** — Day 31, Mathematics · Algebra VIII: functions and function notation. Function notation: the output of f when the input is x.
- **[factor](days/d03.html#term-factor)** — Day 3, Mathematics · Algebra I: identities and factoring. To write an expression as a product; or one of those pieces.
- **[Friday mix (composition and a circle)](days/d45.html#term-friday-mix-composition-circle)** — Day 45, Mathematics · Friday mix: composition, inverses, and a circle. A Friday mix of this week’s mathematics: composition of functions, composition with an inverse, the equation of a circle, and a line-and-circle system.
- **[Friday mix: functions and loci](days/d35.html#term-functions-loci-mix)** — Day 35, Mathematics · Friday mix: functions, graphs, and a locus. A Friday mix, not a new topic: evaluate f(x), read a linear or quadratic graph, and name one of the two loci taught yesterday.
- **[frustums and similar solids, mixed](days/d104.html#term-frustums-and-similar-solids-mixed)** — Day 104, Mathematics · Geometry LVIII: frustums and similar solids, mixed. A short mixed practice of this week’s methods: a frustum as a large cone minus a similar small cone, V = (1/3)πh(R² + Rr + r²), lateral area π(R + r)l, total surface with both bases, and similar solids with areas scaling by k² and volumes by k³.
- **[function y = f(x)](days/d09.html#term-function)** — Day 9, Mathematics · Functions and their graphs. A rule that assigns to each allowed input exactly one output.
- **[geometric progression](days/d20.html#term-geometric-progression)** — Day 20, Mathematics · Two-week problem set: constructions to tangents, and a first look at progressions. A sequence in which each term after the first is obtained by multiplying by the same fixed number, the common ratio r.
- **[height of an isosceles triangle](days/d74.html#term-height-of-an-isosceles-triangle)** — Day 74, Mathematics · Geometry XXXIII: an isosceles triangle, its height, and its area. In an isosceles triangle the altitude to the base meets the base at its midpoint and is perpendicular to it.
- **[heuristic](days/d10.html#term-heuristic)** — Day 10, Mathematics · Two-week problem set. A deliberate method of attack, not a guess: for example, draw a bar, try a smaller case, or write a short proof plan before computing.
- **[hypotenuse](days/d66.html#term-hypotenuse)** — Day 66, Mathematics · Geometry XXIX: the Pythagorean theorem. The side opposite the right angle in a right triangle.
- **[identity](days/d03.html#term-identity)** — Day 3, Mathematics · Algebra I: identities and factoring. An equality that is true for every allowed value of its letters, and that must be proved by expanding or by using a known identity.
- **[identity function](days/d43.html#term-identity-function)** — Day 43, Mathematics · Algebra XIV: composition and the inverse. The function that sends every allowed x to x itself, often written id(x) = x.
- **[incenter](days/d49.html#term-incenter)** — Day 49, Mathematics · Geometry XXII: the incenter, and A = r s. The center of a triangle’s incircle: the point where the angle bisectors meet, equally far from all three sides.
- **[incircle](days/d47.html#term-incircle)** — Day 47, Mathematics · Geometry XXI: the incircle, from equal tangents. The unique circle that sits inside a triangle and is tangent to all three sides.
- **[inequality](days/d07.html#term-inequality)** — Day 7, Mathematics · Algebra III: inequalities and the mean. A statement that one quantity is greater or less than another.
- **[inradius](days/d49.html#term-inradius)** — Day 49, Mathematics · Geometry XXII: the incenter, and A = r s. The radius of the incircle: the perpendicular distance from the incenter to any side.
- **[inscribed angle](days/d21.html#term-inscribed-angle)** — Day 21, Mathematics · Geometry X: central and inscribed angles. An angle whose vertex lies on the circle, and whose sides are chords of the circle.
- **[intersecting chords](days/d24.html#term-intersecting-chords)** — Day 24, Mathematics · Geometry XIII: intersecting chords. If two chords of a circle meet at a point E inside the circle, then the products of the segments of each chord are equal: AE · EC = BE · ED.
- **[intersection of a line and a circle](days/d44.html#term-intersection-line-circle)** — Day 44, Mathematics · Geometry XX: a line and a circle, as a system. The point or points that lie on both a line and a circle.
- **[inverse of a linear function](days/d39.html#term-inverse-linear-function)** — Day 39, Mathematics · Algebra XII: the inverse of a linear function. The function that undoes a given linear function.
- **[isosceles](days/d04.html#term-isosceles)** — Day 4, Mathematics · Geometry II: congruent triangles and the isosceles triangle. A triangle with two sides equal.
- **[iteration of a function](days/d46.html#term-iteration-of-a-function)** — Day 46, Mathematics · Algebra XV: iterating a function. Applying the same function more than once: f(f(x)) is f applied to the result of f(x).
- **[linear function](days/d32.html#term-linear-function)** — Day 32, Mathematics · Algebra IX: graphs of linear functions. A function whose graph is a straight line.
- **[locus](days/d08.html#term-locus)** — Day 8, Mathematics · Geometry IV: inequalities in triangles. The set of all points that satisfy a given condition.
- **[mixed circle problems](days/d28.html#term-mixed-circle-problems)** — Day 28, Mathematics · Geometry XIV: mixed circle problems. Problems that use more than one circle fact from these two weeks — inscribed and central angles, the angle in a semicircle, cyclic quadrilaterals, or intersecting chords — in a single figure, with the reason named at each step.
- **[mixed progressions](days/d27.html#term-mixed-progressions)** — Day 27, Mathematics · Algebra IX: choosing an AP or a GP, and a first applied sum. Problems that ask you to decide whether a sequence is arithmetic, geometric, or neither, and then to use the matching formula for a term or a sum — not to apply one formula to every list of numbers.
- **[opposite angles supplementary](days/d23.html#term-opposite-angles-supplementary)** — Day 23, Mathematics · Geometry XII: cyclic quadrilaterals. In a cyclic quadrilateral, each pair of opposite angles sums to 180°.
- **[order of composition](days/d41.html#term-order-of-composition)** — Day 41, Mathematics · Algebra XIII: composition of functions. Which function is applied first.
- **[parabola](days/d33.html#term-parabola)** — Day 33, Mathematics · Algebra X: graphs of quadratic functions. The graph of a quadratic function, y = ax² + bx + c with a ≠ 0.
- **[parallel through a point](days/d12.html#term-parallel-construction)** — Day 12, Mathematics · Geometry VI: the angle bisector and a parallel through a point. The unique line through a given point P, not on a line ℓ, that is parallel to ℓ.
- **[parallelogram](days/d14.html#term-parallelogram)** — Day 14, Mathematics · Geometry VII: the parallelogram. A quadrilateral whose opposite sides are parallel (both pairs).
- **[perpendicular bisector](days/d11.html#term-perpendicular-bisector)** — Day 11, Mathematics · Geometry V: constructions with compass and straightedge. The line that cuts a segment in half and meets it at a right angle.
- **[perpendicular bisector as a locus](days/d34.html#term-perp-bisector-locus)** — Day 34, Mathematics · Geometry XVI: loci — a circle, and the perpendicular bisector. The locus of points equidistant from two fixed points A and B is the perpendicular bisector of the segment AB.
- **[perpendicular height](days/d71.html#term-perpendicular-height)** — Day 71, Mathematics · Geometry XXX: the area of a triangle. The distance from a vertex of a triangle to the line of the opposite side, measured along a line that meets that side at a right angle.
- **[power](days/d16.html#term-power)** — Day 16, Mathematics · Algebra V: the laws of exponents. The result of raising a base to an exponent, or the expression aⁿ itself.
- **[Pythagorean theorem](days/d66.html#term-pythagorean-theorem)** — Day 66, Mathematics · Geometry XXIX: the Pythagorean theorem. In a right triangle, the square on the side opposite the right angle equals the sum of the squares on the other two sides.
- **[quadratic inequality](days/d51.html#term-quadratic-inequality)** — Day 51, Mathematics · Algebra XVII: a quadratic inequality, from the graph. An inequality whose sides, once arranged, give a quadratic on one side, such as (x − 2)(x − 6) < 0.
- **[radical](days/d17.html#term-radical)** — Day 17, Mathematics · Algebra VI: radicals. An expression that uses a root symbol, such as √a or ³√a.
- **[ratio](days/d01.html#term-ratio)** — Day 1, Mathematics · Modeling: from bar model to equation. A comparison of two quantities by units of the same size, written a : b.
- **[rectangle](days/d15.html#term-rectangle)** — Day 15, Mathematics · Geometry VIII: rectangle, rhombus and square. A parallelogram with a right angle.
- **[regular polygon](days/d37.html#term-regular-polygon)** — Day 37, Mathematics · Geometry XVIII: regular polygons, and the interior angle. A polygon with all sides equal and all interior angles equal.
- **[related angles on the unit circle](days/d131.html#term-related-angles-on-the-unit-circle)** — Day 131, Mathematics · Geometry LXXV: related angles on the unit circle, a first look. On the unit circle already taught, an angle and 180° minus that angle have the same sine and opposite cosines: sin(180° − θ) = sin θ, and cos(180° − θ) = −cos θ.
- **[remainder](days/d01.html#term-remainder)** — Day 1, Mathematics · Modeling: from bar model to equation. What is left of a quantity after a part of it has been taken.
- **[rhombus](days/d15.html#term-rhombus)** — Day 15, Mathematics · Geometry VIII: rectangle, rhombus and square. A parallelogram with all four sides equal.
- **[scale factor (similarity)](days/d63.html#term-scale-factor-similarity)** — Day 63, Mathematics · Geometry XXVII: a missing side, from a scale factor. The constant ratio of corresponding sides of two similar triangles.
- **[secant](days/d52.html#term-secant)** — Day 52, Mathematics · Geometry XXIII: a tangent and a secant (Euclid III.36). A line that cuts a circle at two points.
- **[sector area from the arc, A = (1/2)r s](days/d153.html#term-sector-area-from-the-arc)** — Day 153, Mathematics · Geometry XCIII: sector area from the arc, A = (1/2)r s. Because the already-taught arc length is s = rθ with θ in radians, the sector area A = (1/2)r²θ can be rewritten A = (1/2)r s.
- **[sector of a circle](days/d83.html#term-sector-of-a-circle)** — Day 83, Mathematics · Geometry XL: a sector of a circle. A sector of a circle is the region bounded by two radii and the included arc.
- **[signs of sine and cosine in the four quadrants](days/d129.html#term-signs-of-sine-and-cosine-in-the-four-quadrants)** — Day 129, Mathematics · Geometry LXXIV: signs of sine and cosine in the four quadrants, a first look. On the unit circle, cosine is the x-coordinate and sine is the y-coordinate, so their signs follow the signs of x and y.
- **[similar solids](days/d103.html#term-similar-solids)** — Day 103, Mathematics · Geometry LVII: similar solids, and the scale factors k, k², and k³. Two solids are similar when one is a uniform enlargement or reduction of the other: corresponding lengths are in the same ratio k, already taught as a scale factor for similar triangles.
- **[similar triangles](days/d61.html#term-similar-triangles)** — Day 61, Mathematics · Geometry XXV: similar triangles. Two triangles are similar when their corresponding angles are equal and their corresponding sides are proportional — they are the same shape, not necessarily the same size.
- **[sine and cosine at the axis angles](days/d127.html#term-sine-and-cosine-at-the-axis-angles)** — Day 127, Mathematics · Geometry LXXII: sine and cosine at 180° and 270°, a first look. On the unit circle, the four axis angles in degrees have these points: 0°, (1, 0); 90°, (0, 1); 180°, (−1, 0); 270°, (0, −1).
- **[sine and cosine of 30°, 45°, and 60° on the unit circle](days/d128.html#term-sine-and-cosine-of-30-45-and-60-on-the-unit-circle)** — Day 128, Mathematics · Geometry LXXIII: 30°, 45°, and 60° on the unit circle, a first look. The already-taught 45° isosceles right triangle, and the 30°-60° triangle from half an equilateral triangle, are scaled so that the hypotenuse is 1, and then placed on the unit circle.
- **[sine and cosine of complementary angles](days/d108.html#term-sine-and-cosine-of-complementary-angles)** — Day 108, Mathematics · Geometry LXI: sine and cosine of complementary angles. The two acute angles in a right triangle are complementary: they add to 90°.
- **[sine and cosine, mixed](days/d109.html#term-sine-and-cosine-mixed)** — Day 109, Mathematics · Geometry LXII: sine and cosine, mixed. A short mixed practice of this week’s methods: sin A is opposite over hypotenuse; cos A is adjacent over hypotenuse; and sin(90° − A) = cos A, because the two acute angles of a right triangle add to 90°.
- **[sine of an acute angle](days/d106.html#term-sine-of-an-acute-angle)** — Day 106, Mathematics · Geometry LIX: sine of an acute angle, a first look. In a right triangle, the sine of an acute angle A, written sin A, is the length of the side opposite A divided by the length of the hypotenuse.
- **[sine, cosine, and tangent, mixed](days/d114.html#term-sine-cosine-and-tangent-mixed)** — Day 114, Mathematics · Geometry LXVI: sine, cosine, and tangent, mixed. A mixed practice of the three already-taught right-triangle ratios, together with tan A = sin A / cos A and tan(90° − A) = 1 / tan A.
- **[skew lines](days/d124.html#term-skew-lines)** — Day 124, Mathematics · Geometry LXX: skew lines, a first look. Two lines in space are skew if they are not parallel, do not meet, and do not lie in one and the same plane.
- **[slant height of a pyramid face](days/d96.html#term-slant-height-of-a-pyramid-face)** — Day 96, Mathematics · Geometry LI: the surface area of a right pyramid. The slant height l of a face of a right square pyramid is the altitude of that triangular face, from the apex to the midpoint of a base edge.
- **[slant height of a right cone](days/d97.html#term-slant-height-of-a-right-cone)** — Day 97, Mathematics · Geometry LII: the surface area of a right cone. The slant height l of a right cone is the distance from the apex to a point on the base circle, along the side.
- **[slope](days/d32.html#term-slope)** — Day 32, Mathematics · Algebra IX: graphs of linear functions. The constant rate of change of a linear function: rise over run, (y₂ − y₁)/(x₂ − x₁), between any two points on the line.
- **[square root √a](days/d17.html#term-square-root)** — Day 17, Mathematics · Algebra VI: radicals. For a ≥ 0, the square root √a is the unique number ≥ 0 whose square is a.
- **[substitution](days/d13.html#term-substitution)** — Day 13, Mathematics · Algebra IV: systems of linear equations. A method for solving a system: use one equation to express one unknown in terms of the other, and put that expression into the second equation.
- **[sum of a finite geometric progression](days/d26.html#term-sum-of-a-finite-gp)** — Day 26, Mathematics · Algebra VIII: the sum of a finite geometric progression. The sum of the first n terms of a geometric progression with first term a and common ratio r is Sn = a(rn − 1)/(r − 1) when r ≠ 1.
- **[sum of an arithmetic progression](days/d25.html#term-sum-of-an-arithmetic-progression)** — Day 25, Mathematics · Algebra VII: the sum of an arithmetic progression. The sum of the first n terms of an arithmetic progression with first term a and last term ℓ is Sn = n/2 · (a + ℓ).
- **[surface area of a composite solid](days/d99.html#term-surface-area-of-a-composite-solid)** — Day 99, Mathematics · Geometry LIV: the surface area of a composite solid. The surface area of a composite solid is the sum of the areas that can still be seen or touched.
- **[surface area of a frustum of a right cone](days/d102.html#term-surface-area-of-a-frustum-of-a-right-cone)** — Day 102, Mathematics · Geometry LVI: the surface area of a frustum of a right cone. The lateral surface of a frustum of a right cone is what remains when the small cone’s lateral surface is taken from the large cone’s, both already taught as πrL.
- **[surface area of a right cone](days/d97.html#term-surface-area-of-a-right-cone)** — Day 97, Mathematics · Geometry LII: the surface area of a right cone. The lateral surface of a right cone unrolls to a sector of a circle whose radius is the slant height l and whose arc is the base circumference 2πr, already taught.
- **[surface area of a right pyramid](days/d96.html#term-surface-area-of-a-right-pyramid)** — Day 96, Mathematics · Geometry LI: the surface area of a right pyramid. The surface area of a right pyramid is the area of the base plus the areas of the triangular faces.
- **[surface area of a sphere](days/d94.html#term-surface-area-of-a-sphere)** — Day 94, Mathematics · Geometry XLIX: the surface area of a sphere, and mixed solids. The surface area of a sphere of radius r is A = 4πr², in square units.
- **[system of equations](days/d13.html#term-system-of-equations)** — Day 13, Mathematics · Algebra IV: systems of linear equations. Two or more equations that must be true at the same time, about the same unknowns.
- **[tangent (origin recalled)](days/d36.html#term-tangent-origin)** — Day 36, Mathematics · Geometry XVII: the tangent-chord angle (Euclid III.32). A line that meets a circle at exactly one point.
- **[tangent as sine over cosine](days/d112.html#term-tangent-as-sine-over-cosine)** — Day 112, Mathematics · Geometry LXIV: tangent as sine over cosine. From the already-taught definitions, tan A = sin A / cos A, because (opposite over hypotenuse) divided by (adjacent over hypotenuse) is opposite over adjacent.
- **[tangent of an acute angle](days/d111.html#term-tangent-of-an-acute-angle)** — Day 111, Mathematics · Geometry LXIII: tangent of an acute angle, a first look. In a right triangle, the tangent of an acute angle A, written tan A, is the opposite side over the adjacent side.
- **[tangent to a circle](days/d19.html#term-tangent-circle)** — Day 19, Mathematics · Geometry X: tangents to a circle. A line that meets a circle at exactly one point, called the point of contact.
- **[the ambiguous case (SSA)](days/d146.html#term-the-ambiguous-case-ssa)** — Day 146, Mathematics · Geometry LXXXVII: the ambiguous case (SSA), a first look. The ambiguous case is the SSA arrangement: two sides and a non-included angle, the angle opposite one of those sides.
- **[the derivative at a point, a first look](days/d172.html#term-the-derivative-at-a-point-a-first-look)** — Day 172, Mathematics · The derivative at a point, a first look. The derivative of a function f at a number a, written f'(a), is, in this first look, the limit of the already-taught difference quotient as h approaches 0, when that limit exists: f'(a) = lim h→0 [f(a + h) − f(a)] / h.
- **[the derivative of invented f(x) = x²](days/d173.html#term-the-derivative-of-invented-f-x-x-squared)** — Day 173, Mathematics · The derivative of invented f(x) = x². On the invented function f(x) = x², the difference quotient at a general x is [ (x + h)² − x² ] / h = (x² + 2xh + h² − x²) / h = 2x + h, for h ≠ 0.
- **[the difference quotient, a first look](days/d171.html#term-the-difference-quotient-a-first-look)** — Day 171, Mathematics · The difference quotient, a first look. The difference quotient of a function f, at a number a, in this first look, is the ratio [f(a + h) − f(a)] / h, for a increment h not equal to 0.
- **[the law of cosines](days/d136.html#term-the-law-of-cosines)** — Day 136, Mathematics · Geometry LXXIX: the law of cosines, a first look. The law of cosines says that in any triangle, c² = a² + b² − 2ab cos C, where c is the side opposite angle C, and a and b are the other two sides.
- **[the law of sines](days/d133.html#term-the-law-of-sines)** — Day 133, Mathematics · Geometry LXXVII: the law of sines, a first look. The law of sines says that in any triangle, a/sin A = b/sin B = c/sin C, where side a is opposite angle A, side b opposite B, and side c opposite C.
- **[the limit of a product, a first look](days/d162.html#term-the-limit-of-a-product-a-first-look)** — Day 162, Mathematics · Algebra / analysis: the limit of a product, a first look. If two quantities have limits, the limit of their product is the product of those limits.
- **[the limit of a quotient, a first look](days/d163.html#term-the-limit-of-a-quotient-a-first-look)** — Day 163, Mathematics · Algebra / analysis: the limit of a quotient, a first look. If two quantities have limits, and if the limit of the denominator is not zero, the limit of their quotient is the quotient of those limits.
- **[the limit of a sum, a first look](days/d161.html#term-the-limit-of-a-sum-a-first-look)** — Day 161, Mathematics · Algebra / analysis: the limit of a sum, a first look. If two quantities have limits, the limit of their sum is the sum of those limits.
- **[the radian](days/d141.html#term-the-radian)** — Day 141, Mathematics · Geometry LXXXIII: the radian, a first look. A radian is a measure of a central angle: the angle for which the arc it cuts off has the same length as the radius.
- **[the tangent-secant theorem](days/d52.html#term-tangent-secant-theorem)** — Day 52, Mathematics · Geometry XXIII: a tangent and a secant (Euclid III.36). If from a point P outside a circle a tangent touches the circle at T and a secant cuts the circle at A (nearer) and B (farther), then PT² = PA × PB.
- **[theorem](days/d02.html#term-theorem)** — Day 2, Mathematics · Geometry I: definitions, axioms and the first proofs. A statement that is proved from definitions, axioms, and earlier theorems.
- **[transversal](days/d06.html#term-transversal)** — Day 6, Mathematics · Geometry III: parallel lines and the angle sum. A line that crosses two or more other lines, forming corresponding, alternate interior, and same-side interior angles.
- **[trapezoid](days/d78.html#term-trapezoid)** — Day 78, Mathematics · Geometry XXXVI: the area of a trapezoid. In the American usage used on this site, a trapezoid is a quadrilateral with exactly one pair of parallel sides, called the bases.
- **[triangle as half a parallelogram](days/d77.html#term-triangle-as-half-parallelogram)** — Day 77, Mathematics · Geometry XXXV: a triangle as half a parallelogram. A diagonal of a parallelogram splits it into two triangles of equal area.
- **[triangle inequality](days/d08.html#term-triangle-inequality)** — Day 8, Mathematics · Geometry IV: inequalities in triangles. In a triangle, each side is less than the sum of the other two (and greater than their difference).
- **[two planes in space](days/d123.html#term-two-planes-in-space)** — Day 123, Mathematics · Geometry LXIX: two planes in space, a first look. In space, two distinct planes either intersect in a line, or they are parallel and do not meet.
- **[two tangents from a point](days/d19.html#term-two-tangents)** — Day 19, Mathematics · Geometry X: tangents to a circle. From a point outside a circle, the two tangent segments to the points of contact are equal.
- **[two-week mathematics check](days/d30.html#term-two-week-math-check)** — Day 30, Mathematics · Two-week problem set: circles and progressions. A mixed problem set on these two weeks’ skills: central and inscribed angles, the angle in a semicircle, cyclic quadrilaterals, intersecting chords, and the sums of arithmetic and geometric progressions, with the reason named each time.
- **[two-week mix](days/d40.html#term-two-week-math-mix)** — Day 40, Mathematics · Friday mix: two weeks of functions, graphs, and circle geometry. A Friday mix, not a new chapter: functions and f(x); linear and quadratic graphs; two loci; the tangent-chord theorem (III.32); a regular polygon’s interior angle; Vieta’s formulas; the inverse of a linear function.
- **[unit (in a bar or ratio)](days/d10.html#term-unit-bar)** — Day 10, Mathematics · Two-week problem set. One equal part in a bar model or a ratio.
- **[unit circle](days/d126.html#term-unit-circle)** — Day 126, Mathematics · Geometry LXXI: the unit circle, a first look. The unit circle is the circle of radius 1 centered at the origin of the coordinate plane.
- **[vertex](days/d09.html#term-vertex)** — Day 9, Mathematics · Functions and their graphs. The turning point of a parabola.
- **[vertex of a parabola](days/d33.html#term-vertex-parabola)** — Day 33, Mathematics · Algebra X: graphs of quadratic functions. The turning point of the graph of a quadratic.
- **[vertical angles ∠AOB and ∠COD](days/d02.html#term-vertical-angles)** — Day 2, Mathematics · Geometry I: definitions, axioms and the first proofs. A pair of angles whose sides are the extensions of each other’s sides: the opposite angles formed when two lines meet.
- **[Vieta's formulas r + s = −p, rs = q](days/d05.html#term-vietas-formulas)** — Day 5, Mathematics · Algebra II: quadratic equations. For the roots r and s of x² + px + q = 0, the sum of the roots is −p and the product is q.
- **[Vieta’s formulas (quadratic)](days/d38.html#term-vieta-quadratic)** — Day 38, Mathematics · Algebra XI: Vieta’s formulas for a quadratic. For a quadratic equation, the sum and the product of the roots can be read from the coefficients.
- **[volume of a composite solid](days/d98.html#term-volume-of-a-composite-solid)** — Day 98, Mathematics · Geometry LIII: the volume of a composite solid. A composite solid is made by joining or cutting already-taught solids, such as a cube and a pyramid, or a cone and a hemisphere.
- **[volume of a frustum of a right cone](days/d101.html#term-volume-of-a-frustum-of-a-right-cone)** — Day 101, Mathematics · Geometry LV: the volume of a frustum of a right cone. A frustum of a right cone is the solid that remains when a smaller cone, similar to the original, is cut away by a plane parallel to the base.
- **[volume of a right cone](days/d92.html#term-volume-of-a-right-cone)** — Day 92, Mathematics · Geometry XLVII: the volume of a right cone. A right cone has a circular base and its apex directly above the center of that base.
- **[volume of a right cylinder](days/d88.html#term-volume-of-a-right-cylinder)** — Day 88, Mathematics · Geometry XLIV: the volume of a right cylinder. A right cylinder has two parallel circular bases and a height perpendicular to those bases.
- **[volume of a right prism](days/d87.html#term-volume-of-a-right-prism)** — Day 87, Mathematics · Geometry XLIII: a right prism, volume as base area times height. A right prism has two parallel, congruent bases, and its side edges are perpendicular to the bases.
- **[volume of a right pyramid](days/d91.html#term-volume-of-a-right-pyramid)** — Day 91, Mathematics · Geometry XLVI: the volume of a right pyramid. A right pyramid has its apex directly above the center of the base, and its volume is V = (1/3) × B × h, where B is the area of the base and h is the perpendicular height from the apex to the base.
- **[volume of a right rectangular prism](days/d86.html#term-volume-of-a-right-rectangular-prism)** — Day 86, Mathematics · Geometry XLII: the volume of a right rectangular prism. A right rectangular prism, or classroom box, has three edges that meet at right angles: a length ℓ, a width w, and a height h.
- **[volume of a solid](days/d86.html#term-volume-of-a-solid)** — Day 86, Mathematics · Geometry XLII: the volume of a right rectangular prism. The volume of a solid is the amount of space it occupies, measured in cubic units.
- **[volume of a sphere](days/d93.html#term-volume-of-a-sphere)** — Day 93, Mathematics · Geometry XLVIII: the volume of a sphere. The volume of a sphere of radius r is V = (4/3)πr³, in cubic units.
- **[week’s mathematics mix](days/d50.html#term-weeks-mathematics-mix-w10)** — Day 50, Mathematics · Friday mix: iteration, an incircle, and a line with a parabola. A Friday mix of this week’s skills only: iteration f(f(x)); the incircle from equal tangents; a line and a parabola as a system; and the incenter with A = r s.
- **[week’s mathematics mix](days/d55.html#term-weeks-mathematics-mix-w11)** — Day 55, Mathematics · Friday mix: quadratic inequalities, and a tangent with a secant. A Friday mix of this week’s quadratic inequalities (upward, downward, and a square) and the tangent-secant theorem (Euclid III.36).
- **[π, as C divided by d](days/d81.html#term-pi-as-c-divided-by-d)** — Day 81, Mathematics · Geometry XXXVIII: the circumference of a circle. π is the ratio of a circle’s circumference to its diameter: C / d = π, the same for every circle.
## Physics

- **[a sonic boom](days/d145.html#term-a-sonic-boom)** — Day 145, Physics · Friday mix: the Doppler effect, Example 17.4, and a first look at a sonic boom. A sonic boom, in OpenStax 17.4, is a constructive interference of sound created by an object moving faster than sound, a shock wave along a cone.
- **[acceleration aavg = Δv / Δt](days/d03.html#term-acceleration)** — Day 3, Physics · Acceleration and velocity–time graphs. The rate of change of velocity: how quickly velocity increases, decreases, or changes direction.
- **[action–reaction pair](days/d10.html#term-action-reaction-pair)** — Day 10, Physics · Newton's third law and the two-week check. The two forces named by the third law: they are equal, opposite, simultaneous, of the same type, and they act on two different bodies.
- **[angle of incidence](days/d153.html#term-angle-of-incidence)** — Day 153, Physics · The law of reflection, a first look, from OpenStax 25.2. The angle of incidence, θᵢ, is the angle between the incoming ray and the perpendicular (the normal) to the surface at the point where the ray strikes.
- **[antinode](days/d123.html#term-antinode)** — Day 123, Physics · Standing waves, nodes, and the fundamental, a first look. An antinode is a location of maximum amplitude in a standing wave.
- **[base of support](days/d68.html#term-base-of-support)** — Day 68, Physics · Stability, and the base of support. The area on the ground (or other supporting surface) within which a vertical line from the center of mass must fall, if the object is not to tip.
- **[beat frequency](days/d125.html#term-beat-frequency)** — Day 125, Physics · Friday mix: interference, standing waves, and beat frequency, a first look at beats. The beat frequency, in OpenStax 16.10, is f_B = |f1 − f2|, the frequency at which two waves of similar but not identical frequencies fluctuate in amplitude, from constructive to destructive interference.
- **[center of mass](days/d66.html#term-center-of-mass)** — Day 66, Physics · The center of mass. The balance point of a system of masses, treated as the single point at which the system’s mass may be taken to act for the purpose of translation, or of balance under gravity (when that point is also called the center of gravity, for a uniform gravitational field).
- **[centripetal](days/d41.html#term-centripetal)** — Day 41, Physics · A first look at circular motion, and a center-seeking force. Center-seeking: a force (or an acceleration) directed toward the center of the circle, needed if the motion is to stay on that circle.
- **[centripetal acceleration a = v²/r](days/d16.html#term-centripetal-acceleration)** — Day 16, Physics · Uniform circular motion and centripetal force. The inward, center-seeking acceleration of a body that moves at constant speed on a circle, of size a = v²/r (or a = 4π²r/T², where T is the period).
- **[centripetal force F = mv²/r](days/d16.html#term-centripetal-force)** — Day 16, Physics · Uniform circular motion and centripetal force. The net force toward the center that produces the centripetal acceleration: F_net, inward = m v²/r.
- **[circular motion](days/d41.html#term-circular-motion)** — Day 41, Physics · A first look at circular motion, and a center-seeking force. Motion along a circular path.
- **[component vx, vy](days/d13.html#term-component)** — Day 13, Physics · Vectors and components. The projection of a vector along a chosen axis.
- **[conservation (mechanical, first model)](days/d28.html#term-conservation-mechanical)** — Day 28, Physics · Mechanical energy, conserved when friction does no work. The statement that KE + PE is constant when only gravity (in the PE = m g h model) and forces that do no work (such as a normal force perpendicular to the motion) act.
- **[conservation of momentum](days/d18.html#term-conservation-of-momentum)** — Day 18, Physics · Momentum. If two (or more) bodies interact and the net external force on the system is zero (or is negligible for a short collision), the total momentum of the system does not change.
- **[constructive interference](days/d121.html#term-constructive-interference)** — Day 121, Physics · Superposition, and constructive and destructive interference, a first look. Pure constructive interference, in OpenStax 16.10, is the superposition of two identical waves that arrive exactly in phase, crest aligned with crest and trough with trough.
- **[conversion of elastic potential energy](days/d88.html#term-conversion-of-elastic-potential-energy)** — Day 88, Physics · From elastic potential energy to kinetic energy. If friction and other non-conservative losses are neglected, the elastic potential energy stored in a Hooke’s-law spring can be converted into kinetic energy: ½kx² = ½mv².
- **[damped harmonic motion](days/d101.html#term-damped-harmonic-motion)** — Day 101, Physics · Damped harmonic motion: underdamped, critical, and overdamped. OpenStax, College Physics 2e, section 16.7, treats damped harmonic motion: a non-conservative damping force removes mechanical energy, usually as thermal energy.
- **[damping and the resonance peak](days/d108.html#term-damping-and-the-resonance-peak)** — Day 108, Physics · Damping and the shape of a resonance peak, from OpenStax 16.8. OpenStax 16.8, with its graph of amplitude against driving frequency, shows that resonance still occurs when the driving frequency equals the natural frequency, for each amount of damping shown.
- **[destructive interference](days/d121.html#term-destructive-interference)** — Day 121, Physics · Superposition, and constructive and destructive interference, a first look. Pure destructive interference, in OpenStax 16.10, is the superposition of two identical waves that arrive exactly out of phase, crest aligned with trough.
- **[displacement Δx = xfinal − xinitial](days/d01.html#term-displacement)** — Day 1, Physics · Motion: position, displacement, velocity. The change in position: final minus initial.
- **[elastic potential energy](days/d86.html#term-elastic-potential-energy)** — Day 86, Physics · Elastic potential energy, PE_el = ½kx². Elastic potential energy is the energy stored in a system that obeys Hooke’s law when it is stretched or compressed from equilibrium.
- **[energy in simple harmonic motion](days/d91.html#term-energy-in-simple-harmonic-motion)** — Day 91, Physics · Energy in simple harmonic motion: ½mv² + ½kx² = ½kX². In undamped simple harmonic motion, OpenStax 16.5 writes the conservation of energy as ½mv² + ½kx² = constant.
- **[fiber optics, a first look from official OpenStax 25.4](days/d173.html#term-fiber-optics-a-first-look-from-official-openstax-25-4)** — Day 173, Physics · Fiber optics, a first look from official OpenStax 25.4. OpenStax 25.4, after the official critical angles, describes fiber optics qualitatively: light can be guided along a transparent fiber by already-taught total internal reflection, remaining inside the fiber so long as it strikes the boundary at an angle greater than the critical angle.
- **[force constant](days/d81.html#term-force-constant)** — Day 81, Physics · Hooke’s law, and the force constant. The force constant k (also called the spring constant) measures how stiff the system is.
- **[forced oscillation and resonance](days/d106.html#term-forced-oscillation-and-resonance)** — Day 106, Physics · Forced oscillations and resonance, a first look. A forced oscillation, in OpenStax 16.8, is an oscillation driven by a periodic driving force.
- **[free fall g ≈ 9.8 m/s², downward](days/d05.html#term-free-fall)** — Day 5, Physics · Free fall. Motion under gravity alone, with air resistance neglected.
- **[friction f = μN](days/d11.html#term-friction)** — Day 11, Physics · Friction and the normal force. A force between surfaces in contact that resists their relative sliding.
- **[Friday mix (circular motion)](days/d45.html#term-friday-mix-circular-motion)** — Day 45, Physics · Friday mix: circular motion, and a center-seeking force. A Friday mix of this week’s first look: circular motion, tangent velocity, a centripetal (center-seeking) force, and what happens if that force stops — still with no invented speed.
- **[Friday mix: power and torque](days/d35.html#term-power-torque-mix)** — Day 35, Physics · Friday mix: power and a first look at torque. A Friday mix, not a new topic: compute a power in watts, and a torque in newton-meters, keeping the units distinct.
- **[fulcrum](days/d63.html#term-fulcrum)** — Day 63, Physics · The lever, as a case of rotational equilibrium. The support about which a lever turns.
- **[g from G M / R²](days/d46.html#term-g-from-gm-over-r-squared)** — Day 46, Physics · g from G M / R², with sourced Earth figures. Near a sphere, the gravitational field g is G M / R², because F = G M m / R² and F = m g, so the m cancels.
- **[g on Mars, from the same formula](days/d51.html#term-g-on-mars-from-the-same-formula)** — Day 51, Physics · g on Mars, from the same formula and sourced figures. The already-taught formula g = G M / R², applied to NASA fact-sheet figures for Mars, giving about 3.7 m/s², which matches the fact sheet’s own listed gravity 3.7 m/s².
- **[g on the Moon, a first sourced look](days/d48.html#term-g-on-the-moon)** — Day 48, Physics · g on the Moon, from the same formula and sourced figures. The same formula g = G M / R², now used with sourced Moon figures, compared with a NASA table value and with a NASA Science qualitative comparison.
- **[geometric optics](days/d151.html#term-geometric-optics)** — Day 151, Physics · The ray aspect of light, and geometric optics, a first look, from OpenStax 25.1. In OpenStax 25.1, “The part of optics dealing with the ray aspect of light is called geometric optics.” Because light “moves in straight lines, changing directions when it interacts with materials, it is described by geometry and simple trigonometry.” The law of refraction remains reserved.
- **[gravitational potential energy (near Earth)](days/d26.html#term-gravitational-pe)** — Day 26, Physics · Gravitational potential energy near the Earth. In this course’s first model, the gravitational potential energy of an object near the Earth, treated as a particle, is PE = m g h, where h is the height above a chosen reference level, g is 9.8 N/kg (or 9.8 m/s²), and PE is in joules.
- **[gravity g](days/d05.html#term-gravity)** — Day 5, Physics · Free fall. The attraction that gives a free-falling object its downward acceleration g near Earth.
- **[Hooke’s law](days/d81.html#term-hookes-law)** — Day 81, Physics · Hooke’s law, and the force constant. Hooke’s law says that, for a system that obeys it, the restoring force is proportional to the displacement from equilibrium and is opposite in direction: F = −kx.
- **[impulse J = Δp](days/d20.html#term-impulse)** — Day 20, Physics · Two-week check: friction to momentum. The product of a net force and the time it acts, equal to the change in momentum: J = F_net Δt = Δp, when the force is constant (or using an average force).
- **[independence of components](days/d20.html#term-independence-of-components)** — Day 20, Physics · Two-week check: friction to momentum. The rule, used for projectiles, that the horizontal motion (constant v_x, if air is neglected) and the vertical motion (acceleration g) can be solved separately, and that a change in one component does not by itself change the other.
- **[index of refraction](days/d156.html#term-index-of-refraction)** — Day 156, Physics · Refraction and the index of refraction, a first look, from OpenStax 25.3. In OpenStax 25.3, the index of refraction of a material is n = c / v, where v is the observed speed of light in the material and c is the speed of light in a vacuum.
- **[inertia](days/d06.html#term-inertia)** — Day 6, Physics · Newton's first law: inertia. The tendency of a body to keep its velocity — to stay at rest, or to keep moving in a straight line at constant speed — unless a net force acts on it.
- **[intensity of a wave](days/d126.html#term-intensity-of-a-wave)** — Day 126, Physics · Intensity of a wave, a first look, from OpenStax 16.11. In OpenStax 16.11, intensity I is power per unit area, I = P/A, where P is the power carried by the wave through area A.
- **[intensity under constructive interference](days/d128.html#term-intensity-under-constructive-interference)** — Day 128, Physics · Intensity under constructive interference, from OpenStax Example 16.10. In OpenStax Example 16.10, intensity is proportional to amplitude squared.
- **[inverse-square (gravitation)](days/d36.html#term-inverse-square-gravitation)** — Day 36, Physics · A first look at gravitation: an inverse-square force. A pattern in which a quantity is proportional to 1/r²: if r is multiplied by 2, the quantity is multiplied by 1/4; if r is multiplied by 3, the quantity is multiplied by 1/9.
- **[joule](days/d21.html#term-joule)** — Day 21, Physics · Work done by a constant force. The SI unit of work (and of energy): one joule (J) is one newton-meter, the work done by a one-newton force along one meter of displacement in its own direction.
- **[kinetic energy](days/d23.html#term-kinetic-energy)** — Day 23, Physics · Kinetic energy, and the work–energy theorem. The energy an object has because it is moving.
- **[law of reflection](days/d153.html#term-law-of-reflection)** — Day 153, Physics · The law of reflection, a first look, from OpenStax 25.2. In OpenStax, College Physics 2e, 25.2, “The law of reflection states that the angle of reflection equals the angle of incidence—θᵣ = θᵢ.” The angles “are measured relative to the perpendicular to the surface at the point where the ray strikes the surface.” That perpendicular is the normal.
- **[law of refraction (Snell’s law)](days/d160.html#term-law-of-refraction-snells-law)** — Day 160, Physics · The law of refraction, Snell’s law, and official Example 25.2, from OpenStax 25.3. In OpenStax, College Physics 2e, 25.3, the law of refraction, also called Snell’s law, is n₁ sin θ₁ = n₂ sin θ₂.
- **[locating a center of mass by suspension](days/d73.html#term-center-of-mass-by-suspension)** — Day 73, Physics · Finding a center of mass by two suspensions. A method for finding the center of mass of a flat object: hang it from one point, mark the vertical plumb line through that point; hang it from a second point, mark that second plumb line; the center of mass lies where the two lines meet.
- **[mass (mechanics)](days/d38.html#term-mass-mechanics)** — Day 38, Physics · Mass and weight. A measure of the amount of matter, and of how strongly the body resists a change of motion (inertia), in this first look.
- **[mass m](days/d08.html#term-mass)** — Day 8, Physics · Newton's second law. A measure of how much a body resists acceleration (inertial mass).
- **[maximum speed in SHM](days/d93.html#term-maximum-speed-in-shm)** — Day 93, Physics · The maximum speed in simple harmonic motion, vmax = X√(k/m). In undamped simple harmonic motion, the maximum speed occurs at x = 0, and OpenStax 16.5 gives vmax = X√(k/m).
- **[mechanical energy (no friction)](days/d28.html#term-mechanical-energy)** — Day 28, Physics · Mechanical energy, conserved when friction does no work. In this course’s first conservation model, mechanical energy is the sum KE + PE, with PE = m g h near the Earth.
- **[momentum p = mv](days/d18.html#term-momentum)** — Day 18, Physics · Momentum. The product of mass and velocity, p = m v, a vector in the same direction as the velocity.
- **[net force Fnet](days/d06.html#term-net-force)** — Day 6, Physics · Newton's first law: inertia. The vector sum of all forces on a body.
- **[net work](days/d25.html#term-net-work)** — Day 25, Physics · Friday mix: work, friction, and kinetic energy. The sum of the work done by every force acting on the object along the path, equal (in this course) to the work done by the net force along the path, and equal to the change in kinetic energy.
- **[neutral equilibrium](days/d71.html#term-neutral-equilibrium)** — Day 71, Physics · Stable, unstable, and neutral equilibrium. A rest position from which a small displacement leads to a new rest nearby, without a tendency to return or to run away.
- **[Newton's second law Fnet = m a](days/d08.html#term-newtons-second-law)** — Day 8, Physics · Newton's second law. The net force on a body equals its mass times its acceleration.
- **[Newton's third law](days/d10.html#term-newtons-third-law)** — Day 10, Physics · Newton's third law and the two-week check. If body A exerts a force on body B, then B exerts a force on A that is equal in size, opposite in direction, and of the same type.
- **[Newton’s law of gravitation (first look)](days/d36.html#term-newton-gravitation)** — Day 36, Physics · A first look at gravitation: an inverse-square force. Every two masses attract each other with a force along the line joining them.
- **[node (of a standing wave)](days/d123.html#term-node-of-a-standing-wave)** — Day 123, Physics · Standing waves, nodes, and the fundamental, a first look. A node is a point in a standing wave where the disturbance is zero, and the string does not move.
- **[normal force N](days/d11.html#term-normal-force)** — Day 11, Physics · Friction and the normal force. The force a surface exerts on an object, perpendicular to the surface.
- **[official discussion angles: water to air, and diamond to air](days/d171.html#term-official-discussion-angles-water-to-air-and-diamond-to-air)** — Day 171, Physics · Official discussion angles: water to air, and diamond to air. OpenStax 25.4, after official Example 25.4, states as official discussion results that “the same calculation as made here shows that the critical angle for a ray going from water to air is 48.6°, while that from diamond to air is 24.4°, and that from flint glass to crown glass is 66.3°.” Those three figures are official book discussion results, not invented classroom numbers, and not a new official calculation made here from invented official indices.
- **[official Example 25.1, the speed of light in zircon](days/d158.html#term-official-example-25-1-the-speed-of-light-in-zircon)** — Day 158, Physics · Official Example 25.1, the speed of light in zircon, from OpenStax 25.3. OpenStax, College Physics 2e, official Example 25.1, takes the official Table 25.1 index of zircon, n = 1.923, and the official three-digit c = 3.00 × 10⁸ m/s, and finds the speed of light in zircon from v = c / n.
- **[official Example 25.3, a ray from air into diamond](days/d161.html#term-official-example-25-3-a-ray-from-air-into-diamond)** — Day 161, Physics · Official Example 25.3, a ray from air into diamond, from OpenStax 25.3. OpenStax, College Physics 2e, official Example 25.3, takes a ray from air into diamond.
- **[official Example 25.4, the critical angle for polystyrene to air](days/d168.html#term-official-example-25-4-the-critical-angle-for-polystyrene-to-air)** — Day 168, Physics · Official Example 25.4, the critical angle for polystyrene to air. OpenStax official Example 25.4 asks for the critical angle for light traveling in a polystyrene pipe surrounded by air.
- **[OpenStax Example 16.7](days/d103.html#term-openstax-example-16-7)** — Day 103, Physics · OpenStax Example 16.7: friction on a mass-spring, and a total distance 1.59 m. OpenStax Example 16.7, in section 16.7, places a 0.200 kg object on a spring of k = 50.0 N/m, released from rest at X = 0.100 m from equilibrium, with simple friction, μk = 0.0800, on a horizontal surface.
- **[orbit (first look)](days/d40.html#term-orbit-first-look)** — Day 40, Physics · Friday mix: gravitation, and a first look at orbits. A path in which a body keeps falling toward a larger body and yet does not arrive, because it also has motion to the side.
- **[oscillation](days/d76.html#term-oscillation)** — Day 76, Physics · Oscillation, period, and frequency: a first look. An oscillation is a repeating back-and-forth motion about a central position, such as a hanging bob swinging to and fro, or a mass on a spring moving in and out, once the motion repeats.
- **[period (oscillation)](days/d76.html#term-period-oscillation)** — Day 76, Physics · Oscillation, period, and frequency: a first look. The period, T, is the time for one complete cycle of an oscillation — for example, the time from a leftmost position out and back to that same leftmost position.
- **[period of a mass-spring](days/d83.html#term-period-of-a-mass-spring)** — Day 83, Physics · The period of a mass on a spring. For a mass m on a spring that obeys Hooke’s law, and with no damping, the period is T = 2π√(m/k).
- **[position x](days/d01.html#term-position)** — Day 1, Physics · Motion: position, displacement, velocity. Where something is, measured from a chosen origin along a line, with a sign for direction.
- **[power (mechanics)](days/d31.html#term-power-mechanics)** — Day 31, Physics · Power: work over time. The rate at which work is done: power is work divided by the time in which that work is done.
- **[projectile vx constant; ay = g](days/d15.html#term-projectile)** — Day 15, Physics · Projectile motion. A body in free flight under gravity alone (air resistance neglected in this course), with a constant horizontal velocity and a vertical acceleration of g downward.
- **[projection of uniform circular motion](days/d96.html#term-projection-of-uniform-circular-motion)** — Day 96, Physics · A projection of uniform circular motion is SHM. OpenStax, College Physics 2e, section 16.6, shows that if a point P moves at constant speed around a circle of radius X, its projection on a fixed axis undergoes simple harmonic motion of amplitude X.
- **[range (of a projectile)](days/d15.html#term-range)** — Day 15, Physics · Projectile motion. The horizontal distance traveled from the launch point to the landing point, equal to vx times the time of flight, when the landing is at the same vertical level or at a stated lower level.
- **[ray (optics)](days/d151.html#term-ray-optics)** — Day 151, Physics · The ray aspect of light, and geometric optics, a first look, from OpenStax 25.1. In OpenStax, College Physics 2e, 25.1, light is modeled as traveling in straight lines called rays.
- **[reference level](days/d26.html#term-reference-level)** — Day 26, Physics · Gravitational potential energy near the Earth. The height you choose as h = 0 when you write PE = m g h.
- **[refraction](days/d156.html#term-refraction)** — Day 156, Physics · Refraction and the index of refraction, a first look, from OpenStax 25.3. In OpenStax, College Physics 2e, 25.3, “The changing of a light ray’s direction (loosely called bending) when it passes through variations in matter is called refraction.” The book says this happens because “light changes speed when going from one material to another.” The index of refraction of a material is defined as n = c / v, where v is the speed of light in the material and c is the speed of light in a vacuum.
- **[resonance in a closed air column](days/d146.html#term-resonance-in-a-closed-air-column)** — Day 146, Physics · Resonance in a closed air column, a first look, from OpenStax 17.5. In OpenStax, College Physics 2e, 17.5, a tube closed at one end has a node of air displacement at the closed end and an antinode at the open end.
- **[resonance in an open air column](days/d150.html#term-resonance-in-an-open-air-column)** — Day 150, Physics · Friday mix: closed and open air columns, and official Example 17.5. In OpenStax 17.5, a tube open at both ends has antinodes of air displacement at both ends.
- **[rotational equilibrium](days/d61.html#term-rotational-equilibrium)** — Day 61, Physics · Rotational equilibrium of a rigid body. A rigid body is in rotational equilibrium about a point or an axis when the sum of the torques about that point is zero (Στ = 0): the clockwise torques and the counterclockwise torques balance, so there is no net tendency to start turning.
- **[simple harmonic motion](days/d83.html#term-simple-harmonic-motion)** — Day 83, Physics · The period of a mass on a spring. Simple harmonic motion, in OpenStax’s wording, is oscillatory motion for a system whose net force can be described by Hooke’s law.
- **[simple pendulum](days/d78.html#term-simple-pendulum)** — Day 78, Physics · The simple pendulum, for a small swing. A simple pendulum, in the school model used here, is a bob on a light string or rod of length L that swings through a small angle.
- **[sound](days/d131.html#term-sound)** — Day 131, Physics · Sound, a first look, from OpenStax 17.1. In OpenStax 17.1, sound is a disturbance of matter that is transmitted from its source outward.
- **[sound intensity level (the decibel)](days/d136.html#term-sound-intensity-level-the-decibel)** — Day 136, Physics · Sound intensity level, and the decibel, a first look, from OpenStax 17.3. The sound intensity level β, in decibels, is defined in OpenStax 17.3 by β = 10 log10(I/I0), where I is the sound intensity in watts per square meter and I0 = 10^−12 W/m² is the reference intensity, the threshold of hearing at 1000 Hz.
- **[speed of an SHM projection](days/d98.html#term-speed-of-an-shm-projection)** — Day 98, Physics · The speed of the projection, from similar triangles. OpenStax 16.6 compares two similar right triangles in the circular picture, one of displacements and one of velocities, and obtains v = vmax √(1 − x²/X²) for the speed of the projection.
- **[speed of sound](days/d133.html#term-speed-of-sound)** — Day 133, Physics · The speed of sound, frequency, and wavelength, from OpenStax 17.2. In OpenStax 17.2, the speed of sound, frequency, and wavelength are related by the already-taught wave equation vw = fλ.
- **[stability (against tipping)](days/d68.html#term-stability-against-tipping)** — Day 68, Physics · Stability, and the base of support. An object is stable against tipping while a vertical line from its center of mass falls inside its base of support.
- **[stable equilibrium](days/d71.html#term-stable-equilibrium)** — Day 71, Physics · Stable, unstable, and neutral equilibrium. A rest position from which a small displacement leads to a restoring effect: the object tends to return to that position.
- **[standing wave](days/d123.html#term-standing-wave)** — Day 123, Physics · Standing waves, nodes, and the fundamental, a first look. A standing wave, in OpenStax 16.10, is the result of two identical waves moving in opposite directions, superposed so that the oscillations stay at fixed locations.
- **[superposition](days/d121.html#term-superposition)** — Day 121, Physics · Superposition, and constructive and destructive interference, a first look. When two or more waves arrive at the same point, their disturbances are superimposed: the resulting disturbance is the sum of the individual disturbances.
- **[tangent velocity, kept](days/d43.html#term-tangent-velocity-kept)** — Day 43, Physics · If the inward pull stops: the tangent direction. The direction the motion already has at a point of the circle: along the tangent.
- **[the Doppler effect](days/d141.html#term-the-doppler-effect)** — Day 141, Physics · The Doppler effect, a first look, from OpenStax 17.4. The Doppler effect, in OpenStax 17.4, is an alteration in the observed frequency of a sound due to motion of either the source or the observer.
- **[the lever rule](days/d63.html#term-lever-rule)** — Day 63, Physics · The lever, as a case of rotational equilibrium. For a light lever in rotational equilibrium about its fulcrum, the effort times its perpendicular distance from the fulcrum equals the load times its perpendicular distance from the fulcrum (opposite senses).
- **[torque (first look)](days/d33.html#term-torque-first-look)** — Day 33, Physics · A first look at torque. A measure of how effectively a force tends to turn an object about a point or an axis: the product of the force and the perpendicular distance from the point (or axis) to the line of the force.
- **[total internal reflection, and the critical angle, a first look](days/d166.html#term-total-internal-reflection-and-the-critical-angle-a-first-look)** — Day 166, Physics · Total internal reflection, and the critical angle, a first look. Total internal reflection, in this first look from OpenStax 25.4, occurs when a ray in a medium of index n₁ strikes a boundary with a medium of smaller index n₂, n₁ > n₂, at an incident angle greater than the critical angle.
- **[transverse and longitudinal waves](days/d113.html#term-transverse-and-longitudinal-waves)** — Day 113, Physics · Transverse and longitudinal waves, from OpenStax 16.9. In OpenStax 16.9, a transverse wave, or shear wave, has its disturbance perpendicular to the direction of propagation, as in a wave on a cord, a guitar string, or visible light.
- **[two-week physics check](days/d30.html#term-two-week-physics-check)** — Day 30, Physics · Two-week check: work, energy, and a first look at conservation. A mixed check of work, kinetic energy, gravitational potential energy, and mechanical energy, including one problem in which friction does work so that KE + PE is not constant.
- **[underdamped, critically damped, and overdamped](days/d101.html#term-underdamped-critically-damped-and-overdamped)** — Day 101, Physics · Damped harmonic motion: underdamped, critical, and overdamped. OpenStax 16.7 distinguishes three amounts of damping.
- **[unstable equilibrium](days/d71.html#term-unstable-equilibrium)** — Day 71, Physics · Stable, unstable, and neutral equilibrium. A rest position from which a small displacement leads to a further move away: the object does not return, and it tends to leave that position.
- **[vector v](days/d13.html#term-vector)** — Day 13, Physics · Vectors and components. A quantity that has both a size (magnitude) and a direction, such as displacement, velocity, acceleration, or force.
- **[velocity vavg = Δx / Δt](days/d01.html#term-velocity)** — Day 1, Physics · Motion: position, displacement, velocity. Displacement divided by the time taken (average velocity).
- **[velocity–time graph](days/d03.html#term-velocity-time-graph)** — Day 3, Physics · Acceleration and velocity–time graphs. A graph with time on the horizontal axis and velocity on the vertical axis.
- **[watt](days/d31.html#term-watt)** — Day 31, Physics · Power: work over time. The SI unit of power: one watt is one joule per second.
- **[wave, wavelength, and wave velocity](days/d111.html#term-wave-wavelength-and-wave-velocity)** — Day 111, Physics · Waves, wavelength, and wave velocity, a first look. In OpenStax College Physics 2e, section 16.9, a wave is a disturbance that propagates, or moves from the place it was created.
- **[week's physics mix](days/d50.html#term-weeks-physics-mix-w10)** — Day 50, Physics · Friday mix: g = G M / R² on the Earth and on the Moon. A Friday mix of this week's gravitation work: g = G M / R², the sourced Earth computation (about 9.79 m/s²), the course's working 9.8 N/kg, and the sourced Moon value about 1.6 m/s² (about one-sixth).
- **[week’s physics mix](days/d55.html#term-weeks-physics-mix-w11)** — Day 55, Physics · Friday mix: g on the Earth, the Moon, and Mars, and a weight. A Friday mix of g = G M / R² on Earth, the Moon, and Mars, and of W = m g for a labeled invented mass.
- **[weight](days/d38.html#term-weight-force)** — Day 38, Physics · Mass and weight. The gravitational force on a body.
- **[weight on Mars, for a labeled invented mass](days/d53.html#term-weight-on-mars-for-a-labeled-invented-mass)** — Day 53, Physics · Weight on Mars, for a labeled invented mass. Weight as the already-taught W = m g, using today’s Mars g ≈ 3.7 m/s² and an invented classroom mass that stays labeled invented.
- **[work](days/d21.html#term-work-physics)** — Day 21, Physics · Work done by a constant force. In this course, a force does work on an object when the object is displaced and the force has a component along that displacement.
- **[work–energy theorem](days/d23.html#term-work-energy-theorem)** — Day 23, Physics · Kinetic energy, and the work–energy theorem. The net work done on an object equals the change in its kinetic energy: Wnet = ΔKE = KEfinal − KEinitial.
## Science

- **[a close of How we see](days/d54.html#term-a-close-of-how-we-see)** — Day 54, Science · A close of How we see: tools, and a limit. A short close of the Term 1 seeing module: the eye as a kind of camera, accommodation, refractive errors from the NEI, and corrective lenses as a tool.
- **[a simple circuit](days/d144.html#term-a-simple-circuit)** — Day 144, Science · A simple circuit, a first look, from OpenStax 20.1. A simple circuit, in OpenStax 20.1, is a closed path for current, supplied by conducting wires connecting a load to the terminals of a voltage source, such as a battery.
- **[accommodation (eye)](days/d37.html#term-accommodation-eye)** — Day 37, Science · The lens changes shape: accommodation. The change of shape of the eye’s lens that allows a near object, or a far object, to be focused on the retina.
- **[afterimage](days/d44.html#term-afterimage)** — Day 44, Science · Afterimages: a careful observation, without an invented mechanism. A color or shape that may still appear after the gaze has moved from a bright color or a bright form to a plain surface.
- **[alternating current and direct current, a first look](days/d162.html#term-alternating-current-and-direct-current-a-first-look)** — Day 162, Science · Alternating current and direct current, and official household 120 V, from OpenStax 20.5. In OpenStax, College Physics 2e, 20.5, direct current, DC, “is the flow of electric charge in only one direction.
- **[anthocyanin](days/d29.html#term-anthocyanin)** — Day 29, Science · Autumn color: unmasking, and a caution about new reds. A class of red and purple pigments that, in many autumn leaves, are produced in the season itself, rather than merely unmasked.
- **[astigmatism](days/d49.html#term-astigmatism)** — Day 49, Science · Refractive errors continued: astigmatism and presbyopia. A refractive error that, in the NEI's wording, can make far-away and nearby objects look blurry or distorted.
- **[autoxidation](days/d14.html#term-autoxidation)** — Day 14, Science · Autoxidation and the oil film. A reaction in which a substance combines with oxygen in the air, without being set on fire.
- **[autumn color (careful account)](days/d29.html#term-autumn-color)** — Day 29, Science · Autumn color: unmasking, and a caution about new reds. A first account of why many deciduous leaves change color: chlorophyll often breaks down in autumn, so yellow and orange carotenoids already present can become visible.
- **[azurite](days/d132.html#term-azurite)** — Day 132, Science · Azurite, a blue copper carbonate, a first look. Azurite is a blue copper carbonate mineral.
- **[binder](days/d17.html#term-binder)** — Day 17, Science · Pigment and binder. The substance that holds the pigment particles in a film and attaches the film to the support.
- **[blind spot](days/d34.html#term-blind-spot)** — Day 34, Science · From the retina to a signal. A place on the retina where the optic nerve leaves the eye, and where there are no light-sensitive receptors, so no image is received there.
- **[boiling point (at 1 atm)](days/d62.html#term-boiling-point-1-atm)** — Day 62, Science · Water: melting and boiling at one atmosphere. The temperature at which a liquid boils at a stated pressure, here one atmosphere.
- **[capillary rise](days/d69.html#term-capillary-rise)** — Day 69, Science · Water: capillary rise. The rise (or, in some liquids, the fall) of a liquid in a narrow tube, caused by the liquid’s surface tension together with the way the liquid meets the wall.
- **[carbon dioxide](days/d24.html#term-carbon-dioxide-photo)** — Day 24, Science · Photosynthesis as a net process. A gas, CO2, that is one of the carbon sources for the sugars made in photosynthesis.
- **[carotenoid (leaf)](days/d27.html#term-carotenoid-leaf)** — Day 27, Science · Paper chromatography of leaf pigments. A class of yellow and orange leaf pigments, already present in many green leaves, usually masked by chlorophyll in midsummer views of the leaf.
- **[chlorophyll](days/d22.html#term-chlorophyll)** — Day 22, Science · Living color: why many leaves look green. The green pigment in leaves (and in other photosynthetic parts) that absorbs some of the light the plant uses.
- **[cinnabar](days/d137.html#term-cinnabar)** — Day 137, Science · Cinnabar, a red mercury sulfide, a first look. Cinnabar, in USGS Spectral Library sample HS133, Clark et al.
- **[color vision deficiency](days/d42.html#term-color-vision-deficiency)** — Day 42, Science · Photoreceptors, and a first look at color vision. A difference in how colors are seen, compared with most people.
- **[combustion](days/d04.html#term-combustion)** — Day 4, Science · Combustion and gases: the quantitative model. The chemical burning of a fuel with oxygen, producing new gases (and often water) and releasing heat.
- **[concentrated solution](days/d74.html#term-concentrated-solution)** — Day 74, Science · Water: dilute and concentrated solutions. In this week’s school language, a solution with relatively much solute in a given amount of solution, compared with a more dilute one.
