- **[conduction, convection, and radiation](days/d107.html#term-conduction-convection-and-radiation)** — Day 107, Science · Three methods of heat transfer, a first look. OpenStax 14.4 teaches that whenever there is a temperature difference, heat transfer occurs, and that every such process takes place by only three methods.
- **[cornea](days/d32.html#term-cornea)** — Day 32, Science · How we see: the eye as a kind of camera. The clear front surface of the eye, which begins the bending of incoming light.
- **[corrective lenses, as a tool](days/d52.html#term-corrective-lenses-as-a-tool)** — Day 52, Science · Corrective lenses, as a tool (not a classroom diagnosis). Spectacles or contact lenses can change how light is focused on the retina, so a refractive error already taught (myopia, hyperopia, astigmatism) may be compensated.
- **[density](days/d82.html#term-density)** — Day 82, Science · Water: density, and why ice floats. Density is mass per unit volume: ρ = m / V.
- **[dilute solution](days/d74.html#term-dilute-solution)** — Day 74, Science · Water: dilute and concentrated solutions. A solution in which the sum of the mole fractions of the solutes is small compared with unity, in the IUPAC Gold Book’s wording; in school language, a solution with relatively little solute in a given amount of solution.
- **[dissolution](days/d72.html#term-dissolution)** — Day 72, Science · Water: solutions, solute, and solvent. The mixing of two phases with the formation of one new homogeneous phase, the solution.
- **[drying oil](days/d12.html#term-drying-oil)** — Day 12, Science · Phenomenon: why oil paint dries. A plant oil, such as linseed oil (from flax seed), that can form a solid film by reacting with oxygen in the air, rather than by the evaporation of water.
- **[electric current](days/d142.html#term-electric-current)** — Day 142, Science · Electric current, a first look, from OpenStax 20.1. Electric current, in OpenStax 20.1, is the rate at which charge flows: I = ΔQ / Δt.
- **[electric power](days/d157.html#term-electric-power)** — Day 157, Science · Electric power, a first look, from OpenStax 20.4. In OpenStax, College Physics 2e, 20.4, electric power is the rate of energy use or energy conversion in an electric circuit.
- **[enthalpy of fusion of ice](days/d87.html#term-enthalpy-of-fusion-of-ice)** — Day 87, Science · The enthalpy of fusion of ice, from the Harvey/NIST table. The enthalpy of fusion of ice, written ΔfusH in the Harvey/NIST table, is the energy needed to melt ice at the transition, per gram, without a change of temperature.
- **[enthalpy of sublimation of ice](days/d92.html#term-enthalpy-of-sublimation-of-ice)** — Day 92, Science · The enthalpy of sublimation of ice, from the Harvey/NIST table. The enthalpy of sublimation of ice, written ΔsublH in the Harvey/NIST table, is the energy needed to turn ice directly into vapor at the transition, per gram, at the stated temperature.
- **[evaporation](days/d12.html#term-evaporation)** — Day 12, Science · Phenomenon: why oil paint dries. The change in which a liquid becomes a gas at its surface.
- **[frequency f = c / λ](days/d09.html#term-frequency)** — Day 9, Science · Light: polarization and the quantitative model. The number of wave cycles per second, measured in hertz (Hz).
- **[hematite](days/d124.html#term-hematite)** — Day 124, Science · Hematite, and a red from a yellow ochre, a first look. Hematite is the anhydrous iron oxide Fe2O3.
- **[hyperopia](days/d47.html#term-hyperopia)** — Day 47, Science · Refractive errors: myopia and hyperopia. Farsightedness: a refractive error that, in the NEI's wording, makes nearby objects look blurry.
- **[lapis lazuli](days/d127.html#term-lapis-lazuli)** — Day 127, Science · Lapis lazuli, a rock whose blue is lazurite, a first look. Lapis lazuli is a rock, not a single mineral.
- **[latent heat of vaporization of water (OpenStax)](days/d97.html#term-latent-heat-of-vaporization-of-water-openstax)** — Day 97, Science · The latent heat of vaporization of water, from OpenStax. OpenStax, College Physics 2e, Table 14.2, lists the latent heat of vaporization of water as 2256 kJ/kg (540 kcal/kg) at the normal boiling point, 100.0 °C.
- **[latent heat of vaporization of water at 37.0 °C (OpenStax)](days/d102.html#term-latent-heat-of-vaporization-of-water-at-37-c-openstax)** — Day 102, Science · Evaporation below the boiling point, and Lv at 37.0 °C from OpenStax. OpenStax, College Physics 2e, section 14.3, notes that water can evaporate at temperatures below the boiling point, and that more energy is required than at the boiling point.
- **[malachite](days/d134.html#term-malachite)** — Day 134, Science · Malachite, a green copper carbonate, a first look. Malachite is a green copper carbonate mineral, related to Tuesday’s azurite but not the same mineral.
- **[mass check (of a film)](days/d19.html#term-mass-check)** — Day 19, Science · The oil-paint module: a quantitative check and a short synthesis. A measurement that can distinguish evaporation from autoxidation: a film that is mainly losing a liquid to the air should lose mass; a drying-oil film can gain mass as it takes up oxygen (before any later, slower losses).
- **[maximum density of liquid water near 4 °C](days/d84.html#term-maximum-density-of-liquid-water-near-4-c)** — Day 84, Science · Water: the liquid is denser near 4 °C than at 0 °C. Liquid water is denser near 4 °C than at 0 °C.
- **[melting point (at 1 atm)](days/d62.html#term-melting-point-1-atm)** — Day 62, Science · Water: melting and boiling at one atmosphere. The temperature at which a solid and its liquid are in equilibrium at a stated pressure, here one atmosphere.
- **[model (scientific)](days/d02.html#term-scientific-model)** — Day 2, Science · Phenomenon: the candle under the jar. A reasoned picture of what is happening that makes a testable prediction.
- **[model of oil-paint drying](days/d19.html#term-oil-paint-model)** — Day 19, Science · The oil-paint module: a quantitative check and a short synthesis. The short account this module has built: a drying-oil binder, open to the air, undergoes autoxidation and then polymerization, forming a solid film that holds the pigment; a solvent, if used, may evaporate, but that is not what makes the oil itself into a film; watercolor is a different system, with water leaving mainly by evaporation.
- **[myopia](days/d47.html#term-myopia)** — Day 47, Science · Refractive errors: myopia and hyperopia. Nearsightedness: a refractive error that, in the NEI's wording, makes far-away objects look blurry.
- **[ochre](days/d122.html#term-ochre)** — Day 122, Science · Ochre, from rock to pigment, a first look. Ochre, as the term is used in USGS mineral sources, is an earthy, often clayey form of an iron-oxide mineral used as a pigment after milling and only minimal processing.
- **[official Example 20.5, a headlight filament](days/d154.html#term-official-example-20-5)** — Day 154, Science · Official Example 20.5, a headlight filament, from OpenStax 20.3. OpenStax, College Physics 2e, official Example 20.5, takes a tungsten car-headlight filament with a cold resistance of 0.350 Ω and a length of 4.00 cm, and asks for the diameter.
- **[official Example 20.7, hot and cold power of a headlight](days/d159.html#term-official-example-20-7-hot-and-cold-power-of-a-headlight)** — Day 159, Science · Official Example 20.7, hot and cold power of a headlight, from OpenStax 20.4. OpenStax, College Physics 2e, official Example 20.7, uses the already-taught official headlight figures.
- **[official Example 20.8, the book’s example cost of a 60-W bulb](days/d172.html#term-official-example-20-8-the-books-example-cost-of-a-60-w-bulb)** — Day 172, Science · Official Example 20.8, the book’s example cost of a 60-W bulb. OpenStax official Example 20.8 uses a book example rate of 12 cents per kWh, not a named utility rate, and asks for the total cost of using a 60-W incandescent bulb for 1000 hours if the bulb cost 25 cents.
- **[official Example 20.8, the book’s example cost of a compact fluorescent light](days/d174.html#term-official-example-20-8-the-books-example-cost-of-a-compact-fluorescent-light)** — Day 174, Science · Official Example 20.8, the book’s example cost of a compact fluorescent light. OpenStax official Example 20.8, part (b), replaces the 60-W bulb with a compact fluorescent light that provides the same light output at one-quarter the wattage, so 15 W, costs $1.50, and lasts 10 times longer.
- **[official Example 20.9, peak voltage and peak power for AC](days/d167.html#term-official-example-20-9-peak-voltage-and-peak-power-for-ac)** — Day 167, Science · Official Example 20.9, peak voltage and peak power for AC. OpenStax official Example 20.9 asks for the peak voltage for 120-V AC power, and for the peak power consumption rate of a 60.0-W AC light bulb.
- **[Ohm’s law](days/d147.html#term-ohms-law)** — Day 147, Science · Ohm’s law, a first look, from OpenStax 20.2. In OpenStax, College Physics 2e, 20.2, Ohm’s law is the observed relation that the current through most substances is directly proportional to the voltage applied: I ∝ V.
- **[OpenStax Example 14.5, the ice box](days/d114.html#term-openstax-example-14-5-the-ice-box)** — Day 114, Science · OpenStax Example 14.5: conduction through an ice box. OpenStax Example 14.5 applies Q/t = kA(T2 − T1)/d to a Styrofoam ice box, then uses the already-taught heat of fusion to find how much ice melts in one day.
- **[optic nerve](days/d34.html#term-optic-nerve)** — Day 34, Science · From the retina to a signal. The nerve that carries a signal from the retina toward the brain.
- **[paper chromatography](days/d27.html#term-paper-chromatography)** — Day 27, Science · Paper chromatography of leaf pigments. A separation method in which a solvent moves up a piece of paper by capillary action and carries dissolved substances at different rates, so they form bands.
- **[phenomenon](days/d02.html#term-phenomenon)** — Day 2, Science · Phenomenon: the candle under the jar. A real event or appearance that you can observe, and that you then try to explain with a model and a test.
- **[photoreceptor](days/d42.html#term-photoreceptor)** — Day 42, Science · Photoreceptors, and a first look at color vision. A cell in the retina that turns light into an electrical signal.
- **[photosynthesis](days/d24.html#term-photosynthesis)** — Day 24, Science · Photosynthesis as a net process. The process by which green plants (and some other organisms) use light to build sugars from carbon dioxide and water, and release oxygen as a product.
- **[pigment](days/d17.html#term-pigment)** — Day 17, Science · Pigment and binder. A finely divided solid that gives a paint its color (and often its opacity) and that remains as particles in the film.
- **[pigment](days/d22.html#term-leaf-pigment)** — Day 22, Science · Living color: why many leaves look green. A substance that absorbs some wavelengths of light more than others, and so gives a material a color when white light falls on it.
- **[polarization](days/d09.html#term-polarization)** — Day 9, Science · Light: polarization and the quantitative model. The preferring of one direction of vibration in a light wave.
- **[polymerization](days/d14.html#term-polymerization)** — Day 14, Science · Autoxidation and the oil film. A process in which small molecules join into much larger ones (polymers).
- **[presbyopia](days/d49.html#term-presbyopia)** — Day 49, Science · Refractive errors continued: astigmatism and presbyopia. A refractive error that, in the NEI's wording, makes it hard for middle-aged and older adults to see things up close.
- **[quantitative](days/d04.html#term-quantitative)** — Day 4, Science · Combustion and gases: the quantitative model. Described with measured numbers and a relation, not only with words such as ‘more’ or ‘less’.
- **[rate of conductive heat transfer](days/d112.html#term-rate-of-conductive-heat-transfer)** — Day 112, Science · The rate of conductive heat transfer, a first look. In OpenStax College Physics 2e, section 14.5, the rate of conductive heat transfer through a slab is Q/t = kA(T2 − T1)/d, where Q/t is in watts (joules per second), k is the thermal conductivity of the material, A is the surface area, d is the thickness, and (T2 − T1) is the temperature difference across the slab.
- **[Rayleigh scattering intensity ∝ 1/λ⁴](days/d07.html#term-rayleigh-scattering)** — Day 7, Science · Phenomenon: why is the sky blue and the sunset red?. The scattering of light by particles much smaller than the wavelength.
- **[refractive error](days/d47.html#term-refractive-error)** — Day 47, Science · Refractive errors: myopia and hyperopia. A vision problem in which the shape of the eye keeps light from focusing correctly on the retina, so it is hard to see clearly.
- **[resistivity](days/d152.html#term-resistivity)** — Day 152, Science · Resistivity, a first look, from OpenStax 20.3. In OpenStax, College Physics 2e, 20.3, resistivity ρ is “an intrinsic property of a material, independent of its shape or size.” For a uniform cylinder of length L and cross-sectional area A, made of a material with resistivity ρ, the resistance is R = ρL / A.
- **[retina](days/d32.html#term-retina)** — Day 32, Science · How we see: the eye as a kind of camera. The light-sensitive layer at the back of the eye, where an image is formed and a signal begins.
- **[saturated solution](days/d77.html#term-saturated-solution)** — Day 77, Science · Water: a saturated solution. In the IUPAC Gold Book (S05471), a saturated solution is a solution which has the same concentration of a solute as one that is in equilibrium with undissolved solute at specified values of the temperature and pressure.
- **[solubility](days/d79.html#term-solubility)** — Day 79, Science · Water: solubility, at a specified temperature. In the IUPAC Gold Book (S05740), the analytical composition of a saturated solution, expressed as the proportion of a designated solute in a designated solvent, is the solubility of that solute.
- **[solute](days/d72.html#term-solute)** — Day 72, Science · Water: solutions, solute, and solvent. The minor component of a solution, regarded as having been dissolved by the solvent.
- **[solution](days/d72.html#term-solution)** — Day 72, Science · Water: solutions, solute, and solvent. A liquid or solid phase containing more than one substance, when for convenience one (or more) substance, called the solvent, is treated differently from the other substances, called solutes.
- **[solvent](days/d72.html#term-solvent)** — Day 72, Science · Water: solutions, solute, and solvent. The substance in a solution that is treated differently from the solutes; often, and in this week’s water work, the major component regarded as having dissolved the solute.
- **[specific heat of ice](days/d89.html#term-specific-heat-of-ice)** — Day 89, Science · The specific heat of ice, and a comparison with melting. The specific heat of ice in this lesson is the Harvey/NIST figure for ice Ih at 0 °C and 101.325 kPa: cp = 2.10 J g⁻¹ K⁻¹.
- **[surface tension](days/d67.html#term-surface-tension)** — Day 67, Science · Water: surface tension. A tension in a liquid surface, arising because the surface behaves as if it were a stretched skin: it tends to contract.
- **[three methods in one fireplace, OpenStax 14.4](days/d109.html#term-three-methods-in-one-fireplace-openstax-14-4)** — Day 109, Science · All three methods together, from OpenStax’s fireplace. OpenStax 14.4’s fireplace is a single sourced illustration in which all three already-taught methods occur.
- **[three sourced phase-change figures for water](days/d99.html#term-three-sourced-phase-change-figures-for-water)** — Day 99, Science · Fusion, sublimation, and vaporization, with the sources kept distinct. Three already-taught figures, kept with their sources: Harvey/NIST, ΔfusH(0 °C) = 333.4 J/g and ΔsublH(0 °C) = 2834 J/g, at 101.325 kPa; OpenStax Table 14.2, Lv = 2256 kJ/kg at 100.0 °C.
- **[triple point](days/d64.html#term-triple-point)** — Day 64, Science · Water: the triple point, and the ice point at one atmosphere. The unique pressure and temperature at which the solid, liquid, and vapor of a substance all coexist in equilibrium.
- **[two OpenStax vaporization figures for water](days/d104.html#term-two-openstax-vaporization-figures-for-water)** — Day 104, Science · Two OpenStax vaporization figures, 100.0 °C and 37.0 °C, kept distinct from Harvey. Two already-taught OpenStax figures, kept with their temperatures: Lv = 2256 kJ/kg at 100.0 °C, from Table 14.2, and Lv = 2430 kJ/kg at 37.0 °C, from footnotes 4 and 5 to the same table.
- **[two views (binocular, first look)](days/d39.html#term-two-views-binocular)** — Day 39, Science · Two eyes, and a first look at judging distance. The two eyes, set a little apart, receive slightly different views of a near scene.
- **[ultramarine (from lapis lazuli)](days/d129.html#term-ultramarine-from-lapis-lazuli)** — Day 129, Science · Ultramarine, the blue from lapis lazuli, a first look. Ultramarine, in this first look, is the blue pigment prepared from lapis lazuli by separating the blue lazurite from the other minerals in the rock, after the rock has been ground.
- **[wavelength λ](days/d07.html#term-wavelength)** — Day 7, Science · Phenomenon: why is the sky blue and the sunset red?. The distance from one crest of a wave to the next.
- **[why ice floats](days/d82.html#term-why-ice-floats)** — Day 82, Science · Water: density, and why ice floats. Ice floats on liquid water because the ice is less dense than the liquid.
## History

- **[476 as a conventional year](days/d63.html#term-year-476-conventional)** — Day 63, History · 476 CE: a conventional marker, and the East continues. The year later historians often use as a marker for the end of the western imperial line in Italy, when Romulus Augustulus was deposed by Odoacer.
- **[70 CE, the destruction of the house in Jerusalem](days/d46.html#term-70-ce-destruction-of-the-house)** — Day 46, History · 70 CE: Josephus on the house in Jerusalem, and a patronage caution. The year, in the conventional narrative, of the Roman destruction of the Temple in Jerusalem, which Josephus dates to the second year of Vespasian.
- **[79 CE, as a conventional event-year](days/d51.html#term-79-ce-as-a-conventional-event-year)** — Day 51, History · 79 CE: a later letter to Tacitus, and a conventional year. The usual historical year for the eruption of Vesuvius described in Pliny the Younger’s letter to Tacitus.
- **[a clove-scented leaf, two hours, and a fall of water](days/d165.html#term-a-clove-scented-leaf-two-hours-and-a-fall-of-water)** — Day 165, History · A clove-scented leaf, two hours, and a fall of water, from the second-voyage letter. In Major’s 1870 wording, confirmed from the page, the island “was filled with an astonishingly thick growth of wood.” “We found there a tree whose leaf had the finest smell of cloves that I have ever met with; it was like a laurel leaf, but not so large: but I think it was a species of laurel.” “We found no signs of any people in this island, and concluded it was uninhabited; we remained only two hours, for it was very late when we landed, and on the following morning we left for another very large island, situated below this at the distance of seven or eight leagues.” “At three leagues distance, we could see an immense fall of water, which looked of the breadth of an ox, and discharged itself from such a height that it appeared to fall from the sky.” Men on board laid wagers, “some maintaining that it was but a series of white rocks, and others that it was water.” Nearer, “it was the most beautiful thing in the world to see from how great a height and from what a small space so large a fall of water was discharged.” Those claims are the letter’s.
- **[Alexander of Macedon](days/d16.html#term-alexander)** — Day 16, History · Alexander and the end of the Achaemenid empire. The Macedonian king who, from 336 BCE (the death of his father, Philip II) until his own death at Babylon in 323 BCE, conquered the Achaemenid Persian empire.
- **[Anthemius of Tralles](days/d71.html#term-anthemius-of-tralles)** — Day 71, History · Anthemius of Tralles and Isidorus of Miletus. The master-builder named by Procopius in Buildings I.1.24, in Dewing’s translation: “the most learned man in the skilled craft which is known as the art of building,” who regulated the artisans’ tasks and prepared designs of the future construction.
- **[apology](days/d08.html#term-apology)** — Day 8, History · The trial of Socrates. A defense speech. Plato’s Apology is not an admission of fault; the Greek apologia means a defense.
- **[Arrian](days/d16.html#term-arrian)** — Day 16, History · Alexander and the end of the Achaemenid empire. A Greek historian (2nd century CE) whose Anabasis of Alexander is a main surviving narrative of the campaign.
- **[Caesar as a participant source](days/d28.html#term-caesar-participant)** — Day 28, History · Late Republic sources: Caesar as a participant, and Cicero’s letters. Julius Caesar’s Commentaries (including the Gallic War) are a primary source in the strict sense that the author was a commander writing about campaigns he led — and they are also advocacy: the writer has a stake in how he is seen in Rome.
- **[citizenship](days/d06.html#term-citizenship)** — Day 6, History · Athens: Pericles' Funeral Oration. Membership in the political community, with its rights and duties.
- **[consul](days/d23.html#term-consul)** — Day 23, History · The Republic: consuls, Senate, and Polybius as a source. One of the two chief annual magistrates of the Roman Republic.
- **[corroboration](days/d01.html#term-corroboration)** — Day 1, History · How historians work. Checking whether an independent source agrees, in part or in whole, with the claim you are testing.
- **[democracy](days/d06.html#term-democracy)** — Day 6, History · Athens: Pericles' Funeral Oration. In Pericles’ usage (as Thucydides reports it), a constitution whose administration ‘favors the many instead of the few’.
- **[Dewing’s close of Buildings I.2](days/d113.html#term-dewings-close-of-buildings-i-2)** — Day 113, History · Dewing, Buildings I.2.18–19: the close of the chapter. Dewing I.2.18–19 closes the chapter: the other shrines Justinian “dedicated to Christ are so numerous and so great in size, that it is impossible to write about them in detail,” for “neither the power of language, nor the whole span of eternity, would suffice us to make a catalogue and by name descant upon each one of these.
- **[diplomatic copy](days/d15.html#term-diplomatic-copy)** — Day 15, History · The Egyptian–Hittite treaty of year 21 of Ramesses II. A version of an agreement written for one court, often in a lingua franca (here Akkadian on the tablet from Ḫattuša) or in the court’s own script (Egyptian at Karnak), after the terms have been negotiated.
- **[dome that seems suspended (Dewing)](days/d83.html#term-dome-that-seems-suspended-dewing)** — Day 83, History · The four triangles and the dome that seems suspended (Dewing I.1.42–49). In Dewing I.1.45–46, “upon this circle rests the huge spherical dome (sphairoeidês tholos) which makes the structure exceptionally beautiful.
- **[early Rome (tradition and evidence)](days/d21.html#term-early-rome-sources)** — Day 21, History · Sources for early Rome: Livy, tradition, and archaeology. The period of the Roman kings and the first generations of the Republic, for which later literary tradition (Livy and others) must be read beside archaeology and inscriptions.
- **[edict](days/d31.html#term-edict)** — Day 31, History · From republic to principate: 31 and 27 BCE. A formal public command or proclamation by a person in authority.
- **[eruption, as a later name](days/d53.html#term-eruption-as-a-later-name)** — Day 53, History · The pine-tree cloud, and what the set extract does not yet give. A later English name for the event the letter describes.
- **[Flavian](days/d43.html#term-flavian)** — Day 43, History · A later name: the Flavian line. A later name for the line of Vespasian and his sons Titus and Domitian, who follow the civil war of 69 CE.
- **[forty thousand pounds’ weight of silver (Dewing)](days/d96.html#term-forty-thousand-pounds-weight-of-silver-dewing)** — Day 96, History · The treasures, and forty thousand pounds’ weight of silver (Dewing I.1.64–66). Dewing’s figure in I.1.65 is “forty thousand pounds’ weight of silver,” in the Inner Sanctuary.
- **[four piers (Dewing)](days/d81.html#term-four-piers-dewing)** — Day 81, History · The west entrances, the four piers, and the four arches (Dewing I.1.36–41). In Dewing I.1.37–38, four man-made eminences (lophoi), “which they call piers (pessoi),” stand in the center of the church, two on the north and two on the south, “opposite and equal to each other, each pair having between them just four columns.” They are “composed of huge st…
- **[four triangles under the circle (Dewing)](days/d83.html#term-four-triangles-under-the-circle-dewing)** — Day 83, History · The four triangles and the dome that seems suspended (Dewing I.1.42–49). In Dewing I.1.43–45, because the arches form a four-cornered plan, “the stonework between the arches produces four triangles (trigôna).” Each triangle is contracted to a point below, “by the coming together of each pair of arches,” and “ends in the segment of a circle which it supports.” Upon that circle rests the dome.
- **[four-cornered courses (Dewing)](days/d86.html#term-four-cornered-courses-dewing)** — Day 86, History · The one device: four-cornered courses and lead in the joints (Dewing I.1.50–53). In Dewing I.1.51–52, the piers “are not constructed in the same way as other structures.” “The courses of stone were laid down so as to form a four-cornered shape, the stones being rough by nature but worked smooth; and they were cut to the angles when they were destined to fo…
- **[Friday mix (69 CE and a later name)](days/d45.html#term-friday-mix-69-flavian)** — Day 45, History · Friday mix: 69 CE, Tacitus’s opening, and a Flavian later name. A Friday mix of this week’s history: 69 CE as a civil-war year after 68, the later name “Year of the Four Emperors,” Tacitus’s opening and career line, and Flavian as a later name for Vespasian, Titus, and Domitian — still with no fall day of Rome.
- **[Friday mix: the principate](days/d35.html#term-principate-mix)** — Day 35, History · Friday mix: principate, sources, and no single fall day. A Friday mix: the conventional dates (31 and 27 BCE), the meaning of principate, and the habit of dating Tacitus, Suetonius, and the Res Gestae.
- **[Friday mix: the principate continued](days/d40.html#term-principate-continued-mix)** — Day 40, History · Friday mix: from 27 BCE to the Julio-Claudian list. A Friday mix of last week’s conventional start (27 BCE), Actium (31 BCE), the Res Gestae as advocacy, later writers, the succession of 14 CE, and the Julio-Claudian list through 68 CE.
- **[gadfly](days/d08.html#term-gadfly)** — Day 8, History · The trial of Socrates. A biting fly; in Socrates’ metaphor, the stinging critic who keeps a large, sluggish horse — the city — awake.
- **[galleries of the women’s side (Dewing)](days/d91.html#term-galleries-of-the-womens-side-dewing)** — Day 91, History · The galleries, and the meadow of stones (Dewing I.1.58–60). In Dewing I.1.58, he asks, “But who could fittingly describe the galleries (hyperôa) of the women’s side (gynaikonitis), or enumerate the many colonnades and the colonnaded aisles by means of which the church is surrounded?” He does not, in this sentence, give a count or a measured plan.
- **[Gomera, Ferro, and the thirteenth of October, in the second-voyage letter](days/d158.html#term-gomera-ferro-and-the-thirteenth-of-october-in-the-second-voyage-letter)** — Day 158, History · Gomera, Ferro, and the thirteenth of October, from the second-voyage letter. In Major’s 1870 wording, confirmed from the page, after the stay at the Great Canary the letter turns to Gomera for stores of meat, wood, and water.
- **[half-cylinder (Dewing)](days/d76.html#term-half-cylinder-dewing)** — Day 76, History · The east face of the church, and a half-cylinder, in Dewing I.1.31–32. In Dewing’s translation of Procopius, Buildings I.1.32, the east end of the church is described as masonry built not in a straight line, but curving inward on its flanks and receding at the middle, so that it forms the shape of half a circle, “which those who are skilled in su…
- **[Hellenistic](days/d18.html#term-hellenistic)** — Day 18, History · The Hellenistic world and Ptolemaic Egypt. The period, after Alexander’s death in 323 BCE, in which his empire was divided among successor kingdoms (the Diadochi and their heirs) whose courts used Greek as a language of rule, while older cultures continued.
- **[high mountains, extensive plains, and a port late in the evening, from the second-voyage letter](days/d168.html#term-high-mountains-extensive-plains-and-a-port-late-in-the-evening-from-the-second-voyage-letter)** — Day 168, History · High mountains, extensive plains, and a port late in the evening, from the second-voyage letter. In Major’s 1870 wording, confirmed from the same page, the letter continues: “On the part towards which we moved appeared very high mountains, and on that which we left extensive plains; on the sea coast there were a few small villages, whose inhabitants fled as soon as they saw the sails.
- **[historiē](days/d05.html#term-historie)** — Day 5, History · Greece: Herodotus and the idea of "inquiry". The Greek word (ἱστορίη) meaning inquiry, investigation.
- **[how we know the Republic](days/d30.html#term-how-we-know-the-republic)** — Day 30, History · Two-week synthesis: how we know the Roman Republic. A synthesis question: early Rome and early law are known through later tradition, archaeology, and fragments; the mid-Republic can be analyzed with writers such as Polybius; the late Republic speaks in participant sources that are also advocacy.
- **[Inner Sanctuary (Dewing)](days/d96.html#term-inner-sanctuary-dewing)** — Day 96, History · The treasures, and forty thousand pounds’ weight of silver (Dewing I.1.64–66). In Dewing I.1.65, “that part of the shrine which is especially sacred, where only priests may enter, which they call the Inner Sanctuary (thysiastērion), is embellished with forty thousand pounds’ weight of silver.” It is offered as a single example, because a precise account of all the treasures is impossible.
- **[inquiry](days/d05.html#term-inquiry)** — Day 5, History · Greece: Herodotus and the idea of "inquiry". A systematic asking of questions in order to find out and to explain, not only to list events.
- **[Inter Caetera (May 3, 1493)](days/d121.html#term-inter-caetera-may-3-1493)** — Day 121, History · Inter Caetera, May 3, 1493: the recital of the voyage, a first look. Inter Caetera of May 3, 1493, is a papal bull of Alexander VI, used here in Frances Gardiner Davenport’s 1917 English translation.
- **[Inter Caetera (May 4, 1493)](days/d126.html#term-inter-caetera-may-4-1493)** — Day 126, History · Inter Caetera, May 4, 1493: the line of a hundred leagues, a first look. Inter Caetera of May 4, 1493, is a second bull of Alexander VI, used here in Frances Gardiner Davenport’s 1917 English translation, Document 07.
- **[Isidorus of Miletus](days/d71.html#term-isidorus-of-miletus)** — Day 71, History · Anthemius of Tralles and Isidorus of Miletus. The second master-builder named by Procopius in Buildings I.1.24, in Dewing: “another master-builder, Isidorus by name, a Milesian by birth, a man who was intelligent and worthy to assist the Emperor Justinian.”
- **[judgment](days/d10.html#term-judgment)** — Day 10, History · Synthesis: law, citizenship and judgment. The act of deciding a case, or the standard by which people are judged.
- **[Julio-Claudian line (conventional)](days/d38.html#term-julio-claudian-line)** — Day 38, History · A later name: the Julio-Claudian line, and the same source habit. A later name for the line of principes from Augustus through Nero, conventionally: Augustus (to 14 CE), Tiberius (14–37), Gaius (“Caligula,” 37–41), Claudius (41–54), and Nero (54–68).
- **[Justinian](days/d66.html#term-justinian)** — Day 66, History · Justinian, in Procopius, Buildings I.1.6. The eastern Roman emperor in whose honour Procopius wrote The Buildings.
- **[kingship (Egyptian, this period)](days/d13.html#term-kingship)** — Day 13, History · Kingship and the afterlife: the Pyramid Texts of Unas. The office of the Egyptian king (often called pharaoh in later usage) as both a political ruler and a figure in religion: the king’s tomb, titles, and afterlife texts present him as the one who maintains order and who will join the gods.
- **[late antiquity](days/d61.html#term-late-antiquity)** — Day 61, History · 410 CE: Augustine on the sack of Rome. The name later writers give to the long transition from the later Roman world to the early medieval world.
- **[late Republic (sources)](days/d28.html#term-late-republic-sources)** — Day 28, History · Late Republic sources: Caesar as a participant, and Cicero’s letters. The first-century BCE crisis of the Republic, for which we have unusually rich literary sources — speeches, letters, commentaries — written by participants and contemporaries, alongside later narrative.
- **[lead in the joints (Dewing)](days/d86.html#term-lead-in-the-joints-dewing)** — Day 86, History · The one device: four-cornered courses and lead in the joints (Dewing I.1.50–53). In Dewing I.1.53, the pier stones “were held together neither by lime (titanos), which they call “asbestus”, nor by asphalt, the material which was the pride of Semiramis in Babylon, nor by any other such thing, but by lead (molibdos) poured into the interstices, which flowed …
- **[lex talionis](days/d03.html#term-lex-talionis)** — Day 3, History · Babylon: the laws of Hammurabi. The rule of retaliation in kind: the penalty matches the injury, ‘an eye for an eye’.
- **[light from the stones prevails (Dewing)](days/d88.html#term-light-from-the-stones-prevails-dewing)** — Day 88, History · Gold, the prevailing light, and the two stoas (Dewing I.1.54–57). In Dewing I.1.54, “The whole ceiling is overlaid with pure gold, which adds glory to the beauty, yet the light reflected from the stones prevails, shining out in rivalry with the gold.” The gold is present and adds glory; Dewing’s claim is that the stones’ light nevertheless prevails.
- **[light in the church (Procopius I.1.29–30)](days/d73.html#term-light-in-the-church)** — Day 73, History · The church’s height, proportion, and light, in Dewing I.1.27–30. Procopius’s claim, in Dewing’s I.1.29–30, that the church “abounds exceedingly in sunlight and in the reflection of the sun’s rays from the marble,” so that “one might say that its interior is not illuminated from without by the sun, but that the radiance comes into being with…
- **[Livy](days/d21.html#term-livy)** — Day 21, History · Sources for early Rome: Livy, tradition, and archaeology. Titus Livius, a Roman historian writing in the late first century BCE and early first century CE.
- **[meadow of stones (Dewing)](days/d91.html#term-meadow-of-stones-dewing)** — Day 91, History · The galleries, and the meadow of stones (Dewing I.1.58–60). In Dewing I.1.59–60, the columns and stones are such that “one might imagine that he had come upon a meadow with its flowers in full bloom.” He would “marvel at the purple of some, the green tint of others, and at those on which the crimson glows and those from which the white…
- **[Nika Insurrection (as named by Procopius)](days/d68.html#term-nika-insurrection)** — Day 68, History · The Nika rising, and the church called Sophia, in Buildings I.1.20–22. The rising in Byzantium against Justinian that Procopius, in Buildings I.1.20, calls “the Nika Insurrection,” and that he says he has already described in the Books on the Wars.
- **[the admiral’s resolve to land at break of day, from the second-voyage letter](days/d170.html#term-the-admirals-resolve-to-land-at-break-of-day-from-the-second-voyage-letter)** — Day 170, History · The admiral’s resolve to land at break of day, from the second-voyage letter. In Major’s 1870 wording, confirmed from the same page, the letter continues: “That night the admiral resolved that some of the men should land at break of day in order to confer with the natives.” Those words are the letter’s.
- **[three islands, Turuqueira, Ceyre, and Ayay, from the second-voyage letter](days/d171.html#term-three-islands-turuqueira-ceyre-and-ayay-from-the-second-voyage-letter)** — Day 171, History · Three islands, Turuqueira, Ceyre, and Ayay, from the second-voyage letter. In Major’s 1870 wording, confirmed from the pages, after last week’s resolve to land at break of day, later reserved and skipped sensational sentences are not taken up.
- **[twenty-five leagues, and a search for a “harbour,” from the second-voyage letter](days/d166.html#term-twenty-five-leagues-and-a-search-for-a-harbour-from-the-second-voyage-letter)** — Day 166, History · Twenty-five leagues, and a search for a “harbour,” from the second-voyage letter. In Major’s 1870 wording, confirmed from the page, after last week’s fall of water, the letter says: “This island is very large, and on the side where we arrived it seemed to us to be twenty-five leagues in length.
- **[“no harbour,” and formal possession with the royal banner](days/d163.html#term-no-harbour-and-formal-possession-with-the-royal-banner)** — Day 163, History · “No harbour,” and formal possession with the royal banner, from the second-voyage letter. In Major’s 1870 wording, confirmed from the page, they “proceeded more than a league in search of a port where we might anchor, but without finding one.” “When we found that there was no harbour there, the admiral decided that we should go to the other island, which appeared on the right, and which was at four or five leagues distance.” One vessel remained “seeking for a harbour.” Later, “having found a good one,” they returned to the fleet, “which had put into harbour at the other island, and there the admiral, accompanied by a great number of men, landed with the royal banner in his hands, and took formal possession on behalf of their Majesties.” “Harbour” is Major’s spelling and stays inside the quotation.
- **[no surfeit of the spectacle (Dewing)](days/d93.html#term-no-surfeit-of-the-spectacle-dewing)** — Day 93, History · The sight new each time (Dewing I.1.61–63). In Dewing I.1.63, “Of this spectacle no one has ever had a surfeit, but when present in the church men rejoice in what they see, and when they leave it they take proud delight in conversing about it.”
- **[Old Kingdom](days/d11.html#term-old-kingdom)** — Day 11, History · Egypt: the Old Kingdom and the pyramids. The period of Egyptian history in which the great pyramids of Giza were built, conventionally the Third to Sixth Dynasties.
- **[patronage, in a source caution](days/d46.html#term-patronage-in-a-source-caution)** — Day 46, History · 70 CE: Josephus on the house in Jerusalem, and a patronage caution. Support given by a more powerful person or house, which a later writer may have received.
- **[periodization](days/d20.html#term-periodization)** — Day 20, History · Two-week synthesis: sources for kingship, from Khufu to the Ptolemies. The decision to cut a long past into named periods (Old Kingdom, New Kingdom, Hellenistic) so that change can be talked about.
- **[Pliny the Younger, to Tacitus](days/d51.html#term-pliny-the-younger-to-tacitus)** — Day 51, History · 79 CE: a later letter to Tacitus, and a conventional year. A letter, in J. B. Firth’s 1900 translation (Attalus notes a few modifications), in which Pliny answers Tacitus’s request for an account of his uncle. The uncle was stationed at Misenum, in command of the fleet.
- **[Polybius (on the wars)](days/d26.html#term-polybius-wars)** — Day 26, History · The Punic Wars, with Polybius as a source. A Greek historian of the second century BCE who wrote a history of Rome’s rise, including the Punic Wars.
- **[primary source](days/d01.html#term-primary-source)** — Day 1, History · How historians work. A source produced at the time by someone involved or present: a law, a letter, a coin, an inscription, a painting, a speech written down in the period.
- **[principate](days/d31.html#term-principate)** — Day 31, History · From republic to principate: 31 and 27 BCE. The form of rule associated with Augustus and his successors, in which one man holds lasting pre-eminence while republican offices still have names and some business.
- **[proportion of breadth and length (Procopius I.1.28)](days/d73.html#term-proportion-breadth-length)** — Day 73, History · The church’s height, proportion, and light, in Dewing I.1.27–30. Procopius’s claim, in Dewing’s I.1.28, that the church’s breadth and length “have been so carefully proportioned, that it may not improperly be said to be exceedingly long and at the same time unusually broad.”
- **[Ptolemaic Egypt](days/d18.html#term-ptolemaic-egypt)** — Day 18, History · The Hellenistic world and Ptolemaic Egypt. The Hellenistic kingdom in Egypt ruled by the dynasty founded by Ptolemy, a companion of Alexander (the same Ptolemy Arrian later preferred as a source), from the late 4th century BCE until 30 BCE.
- **[Punic Wars](days/d26.html#term-punic-wars)** — Day 26, History · The Punic Wars, with Polybius as a source. Three wars between Rome and Carthage: the First (264–241 BCE), the Second (218–201 BCE, including Hannibal’s invasion of Italy, Cannae in 216, and Zama in 202), and the Third (149–146 BCE), which ended with the destruction of Carthage in 146 BCE.
- **[pyramid (Egyptian)](days/d11.html#term-pyramid)** — Day 11, History · Egypt: the Old Kingdom and the pyramids. A royal tomb of this period, with a square base and four sloping faces, built in stone, of which the Great Pyramid of Khufu at Giza is the largest.
- **[Pyramid Texts](days/d13.html#term-pyramid-texts)** — Day 13, History · Kingship and the afterlife: the Pyramid Texts of Unas. Religious texts, in the Egyptian language, carved on the walls of royal pyramids beginning with the pyramid of Unas (Wenis), last king of the 5th Dynasty, at Saqqara.
- **[Res Gestae as advocacy](days/d33.html#term-res-gestae-advocacy)** — Day 33, History · How we know: later writers, and a first-person record. The Res Gestae Divi Augusti is a first-person record of deeds attributed to Augustus, known from later copies (notably an inscription at Ankara).
- **[Roman Republic](days/d23.html#term-roman-republic)** — Day 23, History · The Republic: consuls, Senate, and Polybius as a source. The period of Roman government, after the traditional end of the kingship, in which annually elected magistrates (including two consuls) and assemblies, together with the Senate, shared the public business of the city and, later, of an empire.
- **[sack of Rome (410)](days/d61.html#term-sack-of-rome-410)** — Day 61, History · 410 CE: Augustine on the sack of Rome. The taking and plundering of Rome in 410 CE by the Goths under Alaric, as later dated in the Dods edition of Augustine.
- **[secondary source](days/d01.html#term-secondary-source)** — Day 1, History · How historians work. A source that interprets the past afterwards: a textbook, a modern biography, a documentary, a history written long after the events.
- **[seeming to float (Dewing I.1.34)](days/d78.html#term-seeming-to-float-dewing)** — Day 78, History · A structure that seems to float, in Dewing I.1.33–34. In Dewing’s translation of Buildings I.1.33–34, the upper part of the east structure ends in “the fourth part of a sphere,” and above it a crescent-shaped structure rises, “marvellous in its grace, but by reason of the seeming insecurity of its composition altogether terrifyin…
- **[Senate](days/d23.html#term-senate)** — Day 23, History · The Republic: consuls, Senate, and Polybius as a source. The council of leading Romans that advised magistrates and, in Republican practice, carried great weight in policy, finance, and foreign affairs.
- **[six islands on the aforesaid Sunday, and the pilots’ leagues](days/d161.html#term-six-islands-on-the-aforesaid-sunday-and-the-pilots-leagues)** — Day 161, History · Six islands on the aforesaid Sunday, and the pilots’ leagues, from the second-voyage letter. In Major’s 1870 wording of the second-voyage letter, confirmed from the page, after last week’s landfall “on the first Sunday after All Saints, namely, the third of November,” the letter continues: “On the morning of the aforesaid Sunday, we saw lying before us an island, and soon on the right hand another appeared: the first was high and mountainous, on the side nearest to us; the other flat, and very thickly wooded.” “As soon as it became lighter, other islands began to appear on both sides; so that on that day, there were six islands to be seen lying in different directions, and most of them of considerable size.” The pilots “reckoned on that day, that between leaving Ferro and first reaching land, we had made eight hundred leagues; others said seven hundred and eighty (so that the difference was not great), and three hundred more between Ferro and Cadiz, making in all eleven hundred leagues.” Those figures are the letter’s, not a modern official conversion.
- **[spoils, in a triumphal relief](days/d48.html#term-spoils-in-a-triumphal-relief)** — Day 48, History · The Arch of Titus: a later commemoration, not a photograph of 70 CE. Goods carried in a triumph, as later carved.
- **[stele](days/d03.html#term-stele)** — Day 3, History · Babylon: the laws of Hammurabi. An upright stone slab with a relief or an inscription, set up to be seen in public.
- **[succession of Tiberius](days/d36.html#term-succession-tiberius)** — Day 36, History · After Augustus: 14 CE, and the succession of Tiberius. Augustus died in 14 CE.
- **[synthesis](days/d10.html#term-synthesis)** — Day 10, History · Synthesis: law, citizenship and judgment. A reasoned putting-together of several sources around an idea, not a tour of one document after another.
- **[synthesis (this week)](days/d20.html#term-synthesis-essay)** — Day 20, History · Two-week synthesis: sources for kingship, from Khufu to the Ptolemies. A short argued piece that puts more than one source, from more than one period, under a single thesis, with sourcing sentences and a limit.
- **[Tacitus as a later source](days/d41.html#term-tacitus-as-a-later-source)** — Day 41, History · After 68 CE: 69, a later year-name, and Tacitus’s opening. Tacitus wrote the *Histories* later than the events of 69 CE.
- **[the Arch of Titus, as a later monument](days/d48.html#term-arch-of-titus-later-monument)** — Day 48, History · The Arch of Titus: a later commemoration, not a photograph of 70 CE. A single-bay arch on the Sacra Via in Rome, erected in honour of Titus and in commemoration of the siege of Jerusalem, but, in Platner and Ashby's account, not finished and dedicated until after Titus's death (CIL VI.945).
- **[the Augusteum, the column, and the horse, Dewing I.2.1–6](days/d106.html#term-the-augusteum-the-column-and-the-horse-dewing-i-2-1-6)** — Day 106, History · Dewing, Buildings I.2.1–6: the Augusteum, the column, and the horse. Dewing, Buildings I.2.1–6, turns from last week’s close of I.1 to a market-place before the Senate House, “which the people of Byzantium call the Augusteum.” There is a structure of stones of “not less than seven courses,” each narrower and set back, so that people sit upon them as upon seats.
- **[The Buildings (Procopius)](days/d66.html#term-the-buildings-procopius)** — Day 66, History · Justinian, in Procopius, Buildings I.1.6. A work by Procopius on the building works of Justinian, here read in H.
- **[the church called Sophia](days/d68.html#term-church-called-sophia)** — Day 68, History · The Nika rising, and the church called Sophia, in Buildings I.1.20–22. The church in Byzantium which, Procopius says, “the people of Byzantium call ‘Sophia,’ an epithet which they have most appropriately invented for God, by which they call His temple.” In the set extract it is first burned in the Nika rising, then rebuilt by Justinian “so finely…
- **[the date of the first-voyage letter, and the Lisbon postscript](days/d155.html#term-the-date-of-the-first-voyage-letter-and-the-lisbon-postscript)** — Day 155, History · The letter’s date, and the Lisbon postscript, from Major 1870. In Major’s 1870 wording, the letter closes: “This, thus briefly, in accordance with the events.
- **[the eastern arch (Dewing)](days/d98.html#term-the-eastern-arch-dewing)** — Day 98, History · The eastern arch that hung secure (Dewing I.1.67–73). In Dewing I.1.67–73, the eastern arch had been built up from either side but was not wholly completed in the middle.
- **[the figure of the Emperor, Dewing I.2.7–12](days/d108.html#term-the-figure-of-the-emperor-dewing-i-2-7-12)** — Day 108, History · Dewing, Buildings I.2.7–12: the figure of the Emperor. Dewing I.2.7–12 places a colossal bronze figure of the Emperor upon Monday’s horse.
- **[the first-voyage letter (to Luis de Santangel)](days/d136.html#term-the-first-voyage-letter-to-luis-de-santangel)** — Day 136, History · The first-voyage letter to Luis de Santangel, a first look. The first-voyage letter in this lesson is the English of R.
- **[the letter’s claim of friendship at Navidad](days/d153.html#term-the-letters-claim-of-friendship-at-navidad)** — Day 153, History · The letter’s claim of friendship at Navidad. In Major’s 1870 wording, immediately after the sailing master, the letter says: “I have also established the greatest friendship with the king of that country, so much so that he took pride in calling me his brother, and treating me as such.” That is the letter’s claim, confirmed from the 1870 page, not a later official judgment of the relationship.
- **[the pine-tree cloud](days/d53.html#term-the-pine-tree-cloud)** — Day 53, History · The pine-tree cloud, and what the set extract does not yet give. In Firth’s translation of Pliny’s letter, the unusual cloud is at first not clearly tied to one mountain; it is afterwards discovered to have been Vesuvius, and in likeness and form it more closely resembled a pine-tree than anything else, with a long, high trunk and a spread of branches.
- **[the second remedy, and the testimonial (Dewing)](days/d103.html#term-the-second-remedy-and-the-testimonial-dewing)** — Day 103, History · The second remedy, and a testimonial from the work (Dewing I.1.77–78). In Dewing I.1.77–78, “again the Emperor met the situation with a remedy.” He ordered them “immediately to remove the upper parts (akra) of the masonry which were strained, that is, the portions which came into contact with the arches, and to put them back much later, as soon a…
- **[the second-voyage letter, Cadiz and the Great Canary](days/d156.html#term-the-second-voyage-letter-cadiz-and-the-great-canary)** — Day 156, History · The second-voyage letter: Cadiz and the Great Canary, from Major, 1870. In Major’s 1870 wording of the second-voyage letter, confirmed from the page, the writer says he left Cadiz “on the twenty-fifth of September, of the year,” after which the year is missing in the text.
- **[the sight new each time (Dewing)](days/d93.html#term-the-sight-new-each-time-dewing)** — Day 93, History · The sight new each time (Dewing I.1.61–63). In Dewing I.1.61–62, anyone who enters the church to pray “understands at once that it is not by any human power or skill, but by the influence of God, that this work has been so finely turned,” and the mind is “lifted up toward God.” “This does not happen only to one who sees…
- **[the south and north arches (Dewing)](days/d101.html#term-the-south-and-north-arches-dewing)** — Day 101, History · The south and north arches, and the columns planed by flakes (Dewing I.1.74–76). In Dewing I.1.74–76, the eastern arch has been completed, but “in the process of building the other arches, indeed, those namely which are turned toward the south and the north, the following chanced to take place.” The so-called lōri “had been raised up, carrying the masonry of the church, but everything underneath was labouring under their load, making the columns (kiones) which stood there throw off tiny flakes, as if they had been planed.” The master-builders, “dismayed” once more, reported the problem to the Emperor.
- **[the third of November, “The reward, I see the land!”](days/d160.html#term-the-third-of-november-the-reward-i-see-the-land)** — Day 160, History · The third of November, “The reward, I see the land!”, from the second-voyage letter. In Major’s 1870 wording, confirmed from the page, the second-voyage letter dates the landfall: “On the first Sunday after All Saints, namely, the third of November, about dawn, a pilot of the ship Capitana cried out ‘The reward, I see the land!’” Those words are the letter’s.
- **[Treaty of Tordesillas (June 7, 1494)](days/d131.html#term-treaty-of-tordesillas-june-7-1494)** — Day 131, History · The Treaty of Tordesillas, June 7, 1494: the line of 370 leagues, a first look. The Treaty of Tordesillas, concluded June 7, 1494, is used here in Frances Gardiner Davenport’s 1917 English translation, Document 09, as posted on Wikisource.
- **[treaty of year 21](days/d15.html#term-treaty-year-21)** — Day 15, History · The Egyptian–Hittite treaty of year 21 of Ramesses II. A peace treaty between Ramesses II of Egypt and Ḫattušili III of Ḫatti, dated on the Egyptian side to year 21 of Ramesses II.
- **[Twelve Tables](days/d25.html#term-twelve-tables)** — Day 25, History · The Twelve Tables as a class of source. Rome’s early written law code, in the tradition dated to the mid-fifth century BCE (often 451–450 BCE).
- **[two further hospices, Dewing I.2.17](days/d111.html#term-two-further-hospices-dewing-i-2-17)** — Day 111, History · Dewing, Buildings I.2.17: two further hospices. Dewing, Buildings I.2.17, in the 1940 Loeb translation posted at LacusCurtius, records that Justinian, “by no means feeling either a surfeit or any sort of weariness in shewing honour to God,” established two other hospices opposite Samson’s, “in the buildings called respectively the House of Isidorus and the House of Arcadius, the Empress Theodora labouring with him in this most holy undertaking.” The passage completes last week’s reserved close and does not give an official income or a room count for these two houses.
- **[two stoas (Dewing)](days/d88.html#term-two-stoas-dewing)** — Day 88, History · Gold, the prevailing light, and the two stoas (Dewing I.1.54–57). In Dewing I.1.55–57 there are “two stoa-like colonnades (stoai), one on each side,” not separated from the church, “making the effect of its width greater,” running its whole length, and lower than the interior.
- **[Villa de Navidad, the fortress in the letter](days/d151.html#term-villa-de-navidad-the-fortress-in-the-letter)** — Day 151, History · The fortress at Villa de Navidad, from the first-voyage letter. In Major’s 1870 wording of the first-voyage letter, after naming Villa de Navidad, Columbus writes that he “fortified it with a fortress, which by this time will be quite completed, and I have left in it a sufficient number of men with arms, artillery, and provisions for more than a year, a barge, and a sailing master “skilful” in the arts necessary for building others.” The wording is the letter’s, confirmed from the 1870 page.
- **[week's history mix](days/d50.html#term-weeks-history-mix-w10)** — Day 50, History · Friday mix: 70 CE, Josephus's caution, and the Arch of Titus. A Friday mix of this week's history only: 70 CE as the conventional event-year; Josephus on the house, with a patronage caution from the Life; and the Arch of Titus as a later commemoration, with named spoils and an 1822 rebuild.
- **[week’s history mix](days/d55.html#term-weeks-history-mix-w11)** — Day 55, History · Friday mix: a later letter, 79 CE, and the pine-tree cloud. A Friday mix of this week’s history: a later letter from Pliny the Younger to Tacitus, in Firth (1900); the uncle at Misenum; the conventional event-year 79 CE; and the pine-tree cloud, with later scenes reserved.
- **[Year of the Four Emperors (later name)](days/d41.html#term-year-of-the-four-emperors)** — Day 41, History · After 68 CE: 69, a later year-name, and Tacitus’s opening. A later name for the civil-war year 69 CE after Nero’s death in 68 CE, when four men — Galba, Otho, Vitellius, and Vespasian — are usually listed.
## Literature

- **[a reserved telling](days/d44.html#term-a-reserved-telling)** — Day 44, Literature · What the opening does not yet give: later books reserved. A later part of the Aeneid that this course has not yet released as a quotation or as a full plot, and that must not be smuggled into a précis of the opening.
- **[a short attributed hollow-side passage](days/d52.html#term-short-attributed-hollow-side-passage)** — Day 52, Literature · A short attributed hollow-side passage, and what is still reserved. A third short, attributed extract from Dryden’s 1697 Book II, the four lines immediately after last week’s steed passage, in which the pretence is named and soldiers are hidden in the hollow side of the machine.
- **[a short attributed steed passage](days/d49.html#term-short-attributed-steed-passage)** — Day 49, Literature · A short attributed steed passage, and what is still reserved. A second short, attributed extract from Dryden's Book II, in which the Greeks, weary of the war, raise a fabric like a huge steed of pine, pretending it is a vow for their return.
- **[a will that unwills (Inferno II)](days/d79.html#term-will-that-unwills)** — Day 79, Literature · Inferno II, lines 1–42: day departing, and a will that unwills. In Longfellow’s Inferno II, lines 37–42, the speaker compares himself to one “who unwills what he willed, / And by new thoughts doth his intention change, / So that from his design he quite withdraws.” On “that dark hillside,” thinking “consumed the emprise, / Which was so ver…
- **[Aeneid](days/d42.html#term-aeneid)** — Day 42, Literature · Virgil, Aeneid: Dryden’s opening, taught carefully. Virgil’s Latin epic of Aeneas, a Trojan who is driven toward Italy.
- **[aidōs](days/d07.html#term-aidos)** — Day 7, Literature · Iliad, Books 4–6. Shame before others; a sense of honor that answers to the community’s gaze.
- **[analytical essay](days/d09.html#term-analytical-essay)** — Day 9, Literature · Writing about the Iliad: the analytical essay. A short argued piece in which every paragraph begins with a claim, every quotation is introduced and explained, and the other side is acknowledged.
- **[Antigone (opening situation)](days/d32.html#term-antigone-opening)** — Day 32, Literature · Sophocles, Antigone: two sisters, and an edict. Sophocles’s tragedy Antigone opens with two sisters, Antigone and Ismene, after a war in which their brothers have died on opposite sides.
- **[aristeia](days/d07.html#term-aristeia)** — Day 7, Literature · Iliad, Books 4–6. A hero’s ‘best moment’: a stretch of the poem in which one warrior dominates the battlefield.
- **[canto](days/d62.html#term-canto)** — Day 62, Literature · Inferno, Canto I: the opening, in Longfellow’s 1867 translation. One of the chief numbered divisions of a long poem.
- **[catalogue](days/d04.html#term-catalogue)** — Day 4, Literature · Iliad, Books 2–3. A formal list built into a poem.
- **[Charlotte on Jane and Bingley (Pride and Prejudice, Chapter 6)](days/d147.html#term-charlotte-on-jane-and-bingley-pride-and-prejudice-chapter-6)** — Day 147, Literature · Pride and Prejudice, Chapter 6: Charlotte on Jane and Bingley, a first look. In the 1813 Pride and Prejudice, Volume I, Chapter 6, the ladies of Longbourn wait on those of Netherfield, and the visit is returned.
- **[Charon (Inferno III)](days/d92.html#term-charon-inferno-iii)** — Day 92, Literature · Charon, and the shore of Acheron (Inferno III.70–111). Charon, in Longfellow’s Inferno III.82–99, is “an old man, hoary with the hair of eld,” coming in a boat, crying, “Woe unto you, ye souls depraved!” He is later “the ferryman of the livid fen, / Who round about his eyes had wheels of flame,” and “Charon the demon, with the eye…
- **[Creon’s claim (first look)](days/d34.html#term-creon-claim)** — Day 34, Literature · Antigone and Creon: two claims, still no ending. Creon, as ruler in the play, defends the edict as a public command that the city must obey.
- **[Dryden's Book II opening](days/d47.html#term-drydens-book-ii-opening)** — Day 47, Literature · Virgil, Aeneid Book II: Dryden's opening, taught carefully. A short, attributed extract from John Dryden's 1697 translation of *Aeneid* Book II, in which Aeneas, at a queen's command, begins to tell a fate that hurts to remember.
- **[Dryden’s 1697 opening](days/d42.html#term-drydens-1697-opening)** — Day 42, Literature · Virgil, Aeneid: Dryden’s opening, taught carefully. A short, attributed extract from John Dryden’s translation of *Aeneid* Book I, first published in 1697, as given on Wikisource: “Arms, and the Man I sing, who forc'd by Fate, And haughty Juno's unrelenting Hate; Expell'd and exil'd, left the Trojan Shoar.”
- **[ekphrasis](days/d17.html#term-ekphrasis)** — Day 17, Literature · Iliad, Books 17–22: the shield, the return, the death of Hector. A set-piece description of a work of art inside a poem.
- **[embassy (to Achilles)](days/d12.html#term-embassy)** — Day 12, Literature · Iliad, Books 7–9: the embassy to Achilles. The episode in Iliad Book 9 in which Odysseus, Ajax, and Phoenix go to Achilles’ camp with Agamemnon’s offers, and Achilles refuses to return.
- **[epithet](days/d02.html#term-epithet)** — Day 2, Literature · Homer, Iliad, Book 1. A fixed descriptive phrase attached to a name, used and reused in oral poetry (‘swift-footed Achilles’, ‘rosy-fingered Dawn’).
- **[Haemon’s place (first look)](days/d37.html#term-haemon-place)** — Day 37, Literature · Antigone continued: a third voice, still no ending. Haemon is Creon’s son, and he is betrothed to Antigone.
- **[in medias res](days/d02.html#term-in-medias-res)** — Day 2, Literature · Homer, Iliad, Book 1. ‘Into the middle of things’: the story begins not at the first event, but in the midst of an already ongoing action.
- **[in medias res (Odyssey)](days/d24.html#term-in-medias-res-odyssey)** — Day 24, Literature · Odyssey, Books 5–8: Calypso, the raft, and the Phaeacians. Starting a narrative in the middle of the larger story.
- **[Inferno](days/d62.html#term-inferno)** — Day 62, Literature · Inferno, Canto I: the opening, in Longfellow’s 1867 translation. The first of the three canticles of Dante’s Divine Comedy, the journey through Hell.
- **[invocation](days/d02.html#term-invocation)** — Day 2, Literature · Homer, Iliad, Book 1. The opening move of an epic in which the poet asks the Muse to sing, and names the subject of the poem.
- **[ironic opening (Pride and Prejudice)](days/d122.html#term-ironic-opening-pride-and-prejudice)** — Day 122, Literature · Pride and Prejudice, Volume I, Chapter 1: the opening, a first look. The novel opens with a sentence that presents a “truth universally acknowledged” as if it were settled, and the next sentence shows that the “truth” is what surrounding families have already decided.
- **[Jane’s ride in the rain, and Elizabeth’s three miles, in Chapter 7](days/d154.html#term-janes-ride-in-the-rain-and-elizabeths-three-miles-in-chapter-7)** — Day 154, Literature · Pride and Prejudice, Chapter 7: the rain, and a walk of three miles. - **[Limbo (Inferno IV)](days/d99.html#term-limbo-inferno-iv)** — Day 99, Literature · Limbo, the Mighty One, and the four poets (Inferno IV.43–102). In Longfellow IV.43–45, great grief seizes the speaker’s heart, “Because some people of much worthiness / I knew, who in that Limbo were suspended.” This is the set name, in this translation, for the first circle already read on Tuesday.
- **[Longbourn, Meryton, and the militia, in Chapter 7](days/d152.html#term-longbourn-meryton-and-the-militia-in-chapter-7)** — Day 152, Literature · Pride and Prejudice, Chapter 7: Longbourn, Meryton, and the militia. - **[nostos](days/d27.html#term-nostos)** — Day 27, Literature · Odyssey, Books 9–12: the wanderings, told in Scheria. A homecoming or return, especially the return of a Greek hero from Troy.
- **[paradeigma (the Meleager story)](days/d12.html#term-meleager-paradeigma)** — Day 12, Literature · Iliad, Books 7–9: the embassy to Achilles. A story told inside another story as an example meant to persuade: Phoenix tells Achilles the tale of Meleager, who also withdrew in anger and was later begged to save his city, as a warning and a model.
- **[Patroclus in Achilles’ armor](days/d14.html#term-patroclus-in-armor)** — Day 14, Literature · Iliad, Books 11–16: Patroclus. The decision, in Book 16, that Patroclus will put on Achilles’ armor, lead the Myrmidons, and drive the Trojans from the ships, but go no further — a command Achilles gives and Patroclus does not keep.
- **[Phaeacians](days/d24.html#term-phaeacians)** — Day 24, Literature · Odyssey, Books 5–8: Calypso, the raft, and the Phaeacians. The people of Scheria, who receive Odysseus after his raft wreck, host him, hear his name, and (in the poem’s design) will send him home.
- **[Pride and Prejudice, Chapter 10: Darcy’s letter, and “You write uncommonly fast”](days/d167.html#term-pride-and-prejudice-chapter-10-darcys-letter-and-you-write-uncommonly-fast)** — Day 167, Literature · Pride and Prejudice, Chapter 10: Darcy’s letter, and “You write uncommonly fast”. Austen’s Pride and Prejudice, Volume I, Chapter 10, in the 1813 wording.
- **[Pride and Prejudice, Chapter 10: “My good opinion once lost is lost for ever”](days/d169.html#term-pride-and-prejudice-chapter-10-my-good-opinion-once-lost-is-lost-for-ever)** — Day 169, Literature · Pride and Prejudice, Chapter 10: “My good opinion once lost is lost for ever”. Later in Volume I, Chapter 10, in the 1813 wording, Darcy says, “I have faults enough, but they are not, I hope, of understanding.” He continues, “My temper I dare not vouch for.” “My temper would perhaps be called resentful.—My good opinion once lost is lost for ever.” Elizabeth answers, “Implacable resentment is a shade in a character.” She then says, “And your defect is a propensity to hate every body.” He replies, “And yours, is wilfully to misunderstand them.” “Every body” stays inside the quotation.
- **[Pride and Prejudice, Chapter 11: a ball at Netherfield, and vanity and pride](days/d174.html#term-pride-and-prejudice-chapter-11-a-ball-at-netherfield-and-vanity-and-pride)** — Day 174, Literature · Pride and Prejudice, Chapter 11: a ball at Netherfield, and vanity and pride. - **[Pride and Prejudice, Chapter 11: after dinner, and “no enjoyment like reading”](days/d172.html#term-pride-and-prejudice-chapter-11-after-dinner-and-no-enjoyment-like-reading)** — Day 172, Literature · Pride and Prejudice, Chapter 11: after dinner, and “no enjoyment like reading”. - **[Pride and Prejudice, Chapter 9, Mrs. Bennet at Netherfield](days/d162.html#term-pride-and-prejudice-chapter-9-mrs-bennet-at-netherfield)** — Day 162, Literature · Pride and Prejudice, Chapter 9: Mrs. Bennet at Netherfield. The return of Hector’s body to Priam, for burial, after the supplication and the giving of gifts, with a pause in the fighting agreed for the funeral.
- **[simile](days/d04.html#term-simile)** — Day 4, Literature · Iliad, Books 2–3. A comparison that names itself with ‘like’ or ‘as’, often extended over several lines in Homer, usually from nature or ordinary work.
- **[suitors](days/d22.html#term-suitors)** — Day 22, Literature · Odyssey, Books 1–4: the Telemachy. The men who occupy Odysseus’s house in Ithaca, pressing Penelope to remarry and consuming the household’s stores, while Telemachus is not yet able to drive them out.
- **[supplication (Priam)](days/d19.html#term-supplication)** — Day 19, Literature · Iliad, Books 23–24: the funeral, the ransom, a last written response. The scene in Book 24 in which Priam comes by night to Achilles’ shelter, kisses his hands, and asks for Hector’s body, appealing to Achilles’ own father, Peleus.
- **[Telemachy](days/d22.html#term-telemachy)** — Day 22, Literature · Odyssey, Books 1–4: the Telemachy. The opening movement of the Odyssey (Books 1–4), whose foreground is Telemachus in Ithaca and on his journey to Pylos and Sparta, rather than Odysseus’s travels at sea.
- **[the assembly (Pride and Prejudice, Chapter 3)](days/d132.html#term-the-assembly-pride-and-prejudice-chapter-3)** — Day 132, Literature · Pride and Prejudice, Chapter 3: the assembly, a first look. In the 1813 wording of Pride and Prejudice, Volume I, Chapter 3, as posted on Wikisource, the Netherfield party enters the assembly: “Mr.
- **[the close of Antigone](days/d39.html#term-close-of-antigone)** — Day 39, Literature · Antigone: the close of the play, taught carefully. By the end of the play, the edict’s cost has come home: Antigone is dead; Haemon and Eurydice (Creon’s wife) are dead; Creon remains, seeing too late the wreck of his house.
- **[the contrast of Bingley and Darcy (Pride and Prejudice, Chapter 4)](days/d137.html#term-the-contrast-of-bingley-and-darcy-pride-and-prejudice-chapter-4)** — Day 137, Literature · Pride and Prejudice, Chapter 4: Jane and Elizabeth alone, a first look. In the 1813 Pride and Prejudice, Volume I, Chapter 4, Jane and Elizabeth talk alone after the assembly, and the chapter then contrasts Bingley and Darcy.
- **[the death of Hector](days/d17.html#term-hector-death)** — Day 17, Literature · Iliad, Books 17–22: the shield, the return, the death of Hector. The climax of Book 22: Achilles, now back in the war, chases Hector around the walls of Troy and kills him, then mistreats the body.
- **[the divided city, and the close of Inferno VI](days/d114.html#term-the-divided-city-and-the-close-of-inferno-vi)** — Day 114, Literature · Inferno VI.58–115: the divided city, and the close of the canto. Longfellow’s Inferno VI, lines 58–115, continues Ciacco’s speech and then closes the canto.
- **[the first circle (Inferno IV)](days/d97.html#term-the-first-circle-inferno-iv)** — Day 97, Literature · The first circle: sighs, and “without hope we live on in desire” (Inferno IV.1–42). In Longfellow’s Inferno IV.1–42, a heavy thunder wakes the speaker on the verge of “the abysmal valley dolorous.” The guide, “pallid utterly,” leads him into “the foremost circle that surrounds the abyss.” There are “lamentations none, but only sighs,” from “sorrow without tor…
- **[the four poets, and “the sixth was I” (Inferno IV)](days/d99.html#term-the-four-poets-and-the-sixth-was-i-inferno-iv)** — Day 99, Literature · Limbo, the Mighty One, and the four poets (Inferno IV.43–102). In Longfellow IV.79–102, a voice says, “All honor be to the pre-eminent Poet; / His shade returns again, that was departed.” Four mighty shades approach, “nor sorrowful nor glad.” The guide names them: “That one is Homer, Poet sovereign; / He who comes next is Horace, the sati…
- **[the gate of Inferno](days/d87.html#term-the-gate-of-inferno)** — Day 87, Literature · The gate of Inferno III (Longfellow, lines 1–21). The gate of Inferno, in Longfellow’s Inferno III.1–11, bears an inscription that begins “Through me the way is to the city dolent” and ends “All hope abandon, ye who enter in!” The speaker beheld “These words in sombre color … Written upon the summit of a gate.”
- **[the great refusal (as worded)](days/d89.html#term-the-great-refusal-as-worded)** — Day 89, Literature · The neutrals: “look, and pass” (Inferno III.22–69). In Longfellow III.58–60 the speaker “beheld the shade of him / Who made through cowardice the great refusal.” The set wording goes no farther.
- **[the Greyhound (Inferno I)](days/d77.html#term-greyhound-inferno)** — Day 77, Literature · Inferno I, lines 91–111: the Greyhound. In Longfellow’s translation of Inferno I, lines 100–111, Virgil says that the beast will go on until “the Greyhound / Comes, who shall make her perish in her pain.” The Greyhound “shall not feed on either earth or pelf, / But upon wisdom, and on love and virtue”; “’Twixt Feltr…
- **[the leaves simile (Inferno III)](days/d94.html#term-the-leaves-simile-inferno-iii)** — Day 94, Literature · The leaves, “My son,” and the close of Inferno III (lines 112–136). In Longfellow’s Inferno III.112–117, the people leave the margin as leaves fall: “As in the autumn-time the leaves fall off, / First one and then another, till the branch / Unto the earth surrenders all its spoils; / In similar wise the evil seed of Adam / Throw themselves fro…
- **[the Lucases, and pride and vanity (Pride and Prejudice, Chapter 5)](days/d142.html#term-the-lucases-and-pride-and-vanity-pride-and-prejudice-chapter-5)** — Day 142, Literature · Pride and Prejudice, Chapter 5: the Lucases after the assembly, a first look. In the 1813 Pride and Prejudice, Volume I, Chapter 5, the Lucases visit Longbourn the morning after the assembly.
- **[the Master of those who know (Inferno IV)](days/d104.html#term-the-master-of-those-who-know-inferno-iv)** — Day 104, Literature · The Master of those who know, and “where nothing shines” (Inferno IV.121–151). In Longfellow IV.130–135, when the speaker has “lifted up my brows a little,” he beholds “the Master I beheld of those who know, / Sit with his philosophic family.
- **[the neutrals (Inferno III)](days/d89.html#term-the-neutrals-inferno-iii)** — Day 89, Literature · The neutrals: “look, and pass” (Inferno III.22–69). The neutrals, in Longfellow’s Inferno III.22–69, are “the melancholy souls of those / Who lived withouten infamy or praise,” commingled with angels “who have not rebellious been, / Nor faithful were to God, but were for self.” The guide’s instruction is, “Let us not speak of t…
- **[the noble castle (Inferno IV)](days/d102.html#term-the-noble-castle-inferno-iv)** — Day 102, Literature · The noble castle, and the green enamel (Inferno IV.103–120). In Longfellow IV.103–120, the six, already including the speaker, “went on as far as to the light” and “came unto a noble castle’s foot, / Seven times encompassed with lofty walls, / Defended round by a fair rivulet.” They pass the water “even as firm ground,” enter “through p…
- **[the panther (Longfellow, Inferno I)](days/d67.html#term-the-panther-longfellow-inferno-i)** — Day 67, Literature · Inferno, Canto I: the mountain and the panther, in Longfellow’s lines 16–36. The first of the three beasts in the set lines of Canto I, in Longfellow’s wording: “A panther light and swift exceedingly, / Which with a spotted skin was covered o’er,” that impedes the speaker on the desert slope and does not move from before his face.
- **[the saintly Lady, Beatrice](days/d82.html#term-the-saintly-lady-beatrice)** — Day 82, Literature · Inferno II: the saintly Lady, named Beatrice (II.52–74). In Longfellow, Inferno II.52–74, a “fair, saintly Lady” calls to Virgil “among those… who are in suspense,” and later names herself: “Beatrice am I, who do bid thee go.” She asks him to help “a friend of mine, and not the friend of fortune,” impeded on the desert slope.
- **[the second circle, Minos, and the hurricane, Inferno V](days/d107.html#term-the-second-circle-minos-and-the-hurricane-inferno-v)** — Day 107, Literature · Inferno V.1–72: the second circle, Minos, and the hurricane. Inferno V, in Longfellow, descends “out of the first circle down to the second, that less space begirds, and so much greater dole, that goads to wailing.” Minos “standeth horribly, and snarls,” examines the transgressions, and “girds himself with his tail as many times as grades he wishes it should be thrust down.” He warns of the “dolorous hostelry.” The place is “mute of all light,” and “the infernal hurricane that never rests” hurtles the spirits.
- **[the third circle, Cerberus, and Ciacco, Inferno VI](days/d112.html#term-the-third-circle-cerberus-and-ciacco-inferno-vi)** — Day 112, Literature · Inferno VI.1–57: the third circle, Cerberus, and Ciacco. Longfellow’s Inferno VI, lines 1–57, places the speaker in the third circle of rain “Eternal, maledict, and cold, and heavy,” with hail, sombre-hued water, and snow.
- **[the three beasts (Longfellow, Inferno I)](days/d69.html#term-the-three-beasts-longfellow-inferno-i)** — Day 69, Literature · Inferno, Canto I: the lion and the she-wolf, in Longfellow’s lines 37–60. In the set lines of Canto I, the panther already assigned, then “a lion’s aspect” coming “with head uplifted, and with ravenous hunger,” and “a she-wolf,” “laden in her meagreness,” that makes the speaker relinquish “the hope … of the height.”
- **[the two who go together, and Francesca’s speech, Inferno V](days/d109.html#term-the-two-who-go-together-and-francescas-speech-inferno-v)** — Day 109, Literature · Inferno V.73–142: the two who go together, and Francesca’s speech. Inferno V.73–142, in Longfellow, turns to “those two, who go together, and seem upon the wind to be so light.” The guide says, “Thou 'lt mark, when they shall be nearer to us.” They come “as turtle-doves,” “from the band where Dido is.” One spirit speaks: “Love, that on gentle heart doth swiftly seize,” “Love, that exempts no one beloved from loving,” “Love has conducted us unto one death.” The speaker names her, “Thine agonies, Francesca.” She says, “There is no greater sorrow than to be mindful of the happy time in misery,” and tells of reading “of Launcelot,” until “one point only was it that o’ercame us,” and “that day no farther did we read therein.” “The other one did weep so, that, for pity, I swooned away as if I had been dying, and fell, even as a dead body falls.” Keep “Thou 'lt,” “o’ercame,” and “Caïna.” A complete recitation of every line is not required.
- **[the undisclosed visit (Pride and Prejudice, Chapter 2)](days/d127.html#term-the-undisclosed-visit-pride-and-prejudice-chapter-2)** — Day 127, Literature · Pride and Prejudice, Chapter 2: the visit already paid, a first look. In the 1813 wording of Volume I, Chapter 2, “Mr.
- **[thesis](days/d09.html#term-thesis)** — Day 9, Literature · Writing about the Iliad: the analytical essay. One sentence that can be argued against, stating the judgment the essay will defend.
- **[three Ladies benedight](days/d84.html#term-three-ladies-benedight)** — Day 84, Literature · Inferno II: three Ladies, recovered courage, and the way (II.94–142). In Longfellow, Inferno II.94–126, a “gentle Lady… in Heaven” asks Lucìa to help “thy faithful one”; Lucìa comes to Beatrice, “sitting with the ancient Rachel,” and Beatrice then comes to Virgil.
- **[Virgilius (Longfellow, Inferno I)](days/d72.html#term-virgilius-longfellow-inferno-i)** — Day 72, Literature · Inferno I, lines 61–90: Virgilius. The figure who presents himself in Longfellow’s Inferno I, lines 61–90: “Not man; man once I was,” born “Sub Julio,” who lived at Rome “under the good Augustus,” a poet who sang “that just / Son of Anchises,” and whom the speaker recognizes as “that Virgilius and that fountain.”
- **[Virgilius as guide (Longfellow, Inferno I)](days/d74.html#term-virgilius-as-guide)** — Day 74, Literature · Inferno I, lines 112–136: “I will be thy guide”. In Longfellow’s Inferno I, lines 112–136, Virgilius offers to be the speaker’s guide “through the eternal place,” and at the end “he moved on, and I behind him followed.”
- **[wanderings (Books 9–12)](days/d27.html#term-wanderings-9-12)** — Day 27, Literature · Odyssey, Books 9–12: the wanderings, told in Scheria. The adventures Odysseus narrates to the Phaeacians in Books 9–12, which belong earlier in the story’s time than Books 5–8.
