- **[what is still reserved in Book II](days/d54.html#term-what-is-still-reserved-in-book-ii)** — Day 54, Literature · What is still reserved in Book II. A sitting that keeps later Book II reserved: Tenedos and a concealed fleet, the Trojans unbarring the gates, named quarters in the deserted camp, Sinon, later night scenes, and Priam.
- **[written response (Odyssey so far)](days/d29.html#term-written-response-odyssey)** — Day 29, Literature · A written response to Odyssey 1–12. A short, sourced piece of writing — here, in the spirit of this week’s three-paragraph essay — on a design question in Odyssey 1–12 (and, if you have been assigned them, no further).
- **[“My son,” in Inferno III](days/d94.html#term-my-son-in-inferno-iii)** — Day 94, Literature · The leaves, “My son,” and the close of Inferno III (lines 112–136). In Longfellow III.121–129 the guide says, “My son,” and explains that those who perish in the wrath of God meet here out of every land, ready to pass over because celestial Justice spurs them, so that fear is turned into desire.
- **[“where nothing shines” (Inferno IV)](days/d104.html#term-where-nothing-shines-inferno-iv)** — Day 104, Literature · The Master of those who know, and “where nothing shines” (Inferno IV.121–151). Inferno IV closes, in Longfellow 147–151: “The sixfold company in two divides; / Another way my sapient Guide conducts me / Forth from the quiet to the air that trembles; / And to a place I come where nothing shines.” That is the set wording of the close of the canto.
- **[“without hope we live on in desire”](days/d97.html#term-without-hope-we-live-on-in-desire)** — Day 97, Literature · The first circle: sighs, and “without hope we live on in desire” (Inferno IV.1–42). In Longfellow IV.40–42, the guide says that for these defects, “and not for other guilt, / Lost are we, and are only so far punished, / That without hope we live on in desire.” That is the set wording of the punishment in this stretch.
## Writing & Rhetoric

- **[a precis of the set opening](days/d49.html#term-precis-of-the-set-opening-w10)** — Day 49, Writing & Rhetoric · A precis of the set opening, in your own words. A very short restatement, in your own words, of only the set extract, preserving the point and the attribution, without later scenes and without copied phrasing presented as your own.
- **[a précis of the set extract](days/d54.html#term-a-precis-of-the-set-extract-w11)** — Day 54, Writing & Rhetoric · A précis of the set Pliny extract, in your own words. A short précis, in your own words, of only the set Firth sentences: the delayed identification of the mountain as Vesuvius, and the pine-tree likeness (trunk and branches).
- **[a short attributed quotation](days/d47.html#term-short-attributed-quotation-w10)** — Day 47, Writing & Rhetoric · A short attributed quotation from Dryden's Book II opening. A brief extract, copied accurately, with the translator, the work, and the publication given, and with no words added as if they were in the extract.
- **[a short attributed quotation from Firth’s Pliny](days/d52.html#term-short-attributed-quotation-from-firths-pliny)** — Day 52, Writing & Rhetoric · A short attributed quotation from Firth’s Pliny. A short extract copied accurately, attributed to Pliny the Younger’s letter to Tacitus as rendered by J.
- **[a short attributed quotation, in a sentence](days/d42.html#term-short-attributed-quotation-in-a-sentence)** — Day 42, Writing & Rhetoric · A short attributed quotation from Dryden’s opening. A short public-domain line, copied exactly, named by author, translator if any, work, and date, and tucked into your own sentence without pretending the rest of the work has been quoted.
- **[ad hominem](days/d74.html#term-ad-hominem)** — Day 74, Writing & Rhetoric · Ad hominem. A fallacy in which a claim is rejected (or accepted) because of a trait of the person who offers it, instead of an answer to the claim or to the reasons given for it.
- **[although-sentence](days/d09.html#term-although-sentence)** — Day 9, Writing & Rhetoric · Franklin's method, part 3: verse into prose. A sentence that concedes a real point on the other side, then states the judgment it can still defend.
- **[appositive](days/d03.html#term-appositive)** — Day 3, Writing & Rhetoric · Appositives and subordinating conjunctions. A noun phrase set beside another noun to rename it: ‘Leonardo, the draughtsman, …’.
- **[assembled short essay](days/d84.html#term-assembled-short-essay)** — Day 84, Writing & Rhetoric · The short complete essay, assembled. An assembled short essay is the opening, the body paragraphs in outline order, the other-side paragraph, and the close, copied and then repaired so that they read as one piece: pronouns and tense agree, quotations stay exact, and no heading is left out or repeated.
- **[attribution](days/d18.html#term-attribution)** — Day 18, Writing & Rhetoric · Quotations and attribution. The naming of who said or wrote the words, and in what work or on what occasion, so that a reader can find the source.
- **[attribution (review)](days/d28.html#term-attribution-review-w6)** — Day 28, Writing & Rhetoric · Evidence and attribution, in three paragraphs. Naming a source when you quote or précis it, so a reader can tell whose words or whose claim you are using.
- **[because / but / so, on a later letter](days/d51.html#term-because-but-so-on-a-later-letter)** — Day 51, Writing & Rhetoric · Because, but, so: a later letter, in translation. A because / but / so sentence that keeps Pliny the Younger’s letter to Tacitus in its job: it is a later request from a historian, in a sourced translation, not a measured log of 79 CE and not a photograph.
- **[because / but / so, on a source](days/d41.html#term-because-but-so-on-a-source)** — Day 41, Writing & Rhetoric · Because, but, so: a source caution in one sentence. The same three stems already taught, now used to keep a source caution in one sentence: a fact *because* of a named source, *but* a limit on what that source is, *so* a careful claim.
- **[because / but / so, on a source caution](days/d46.html#term-because-but-so-source-caution-w10)** — Day 46, Writing & Rhetoric · Because, but, so: a source caution in one sentence. A because / but / so sentence that uses only a source caution already taught today, and that does not invent a verdict the source does not support.
- **[chreia (progymnasmata)](days/d131.html#term-chreia-progymnasmata)** — Day 131, Writing · A chreia, a first look, after last week’s narrative. A chreia, in this first look, is a short, useful anecdote that attributes a saying, a deed, or both to a named person.
- **[circular reasoning](days/d76.html#term-circular-reasoning)** — Day 76, Writing & Rhetoric · Circular reasoning. Circular reasoning is a flaw in which a claim is supported by a restatement of the same claim, or by a premise that already assumes what was supposed to be shown.
- **[claim](days/d08.html#term-claim)** — Day 8, Writing & Rhetoric · Trivium II, logic: claim, evidence, warrant. A sentence that can be argued with, which the rest of the paragraph or essay must support.
- **[clause](days/d05.html#term-clause)** — Day 5, Writing & Rhetoric · Trivium I, grammar: the anatomy of a sentence. A group of words with a subject and a verb.
- **[commonplace (progymnasmata)](days/d151.html#term-commonplace-progymnasmata)** — Day 151, Writing · A commonplace, a first look, after last week’s confirmation. A commonplace, in this course, is an amplification of a general, already-acknowledged fault of a type, not a trial of a living person, and not a new claim that still has to be confirmed or refuted.
- **[comparison (syncrisis), a first look](days/d166.html#term-comparison-syncrisis-a-first-look)** — Day 166, Writing · Comparison (syncrisis), a first look. A comparison, also called syncrisis, is the next progymnasma after last week’s invective and week 32’s encomium.
- **[comparison step](days/d17.html#term-comparison-step)** — Day 17, Writing & Rhetoric · Franklin's method: reconstruction from the hints. The last step of a reconstruction: laying your version beside the original and naming, in writing, two or three specific choices the author made that you did not — a verb, a delay, an apposition, a transition — not a vague judgment that the original is ‘better’.
- **[concession](days/d19.html#term-concession)** — Day 19, Writing & Rhetoric · Concession in a two-paragraph argument. A real point on the other side that you state fairly, before you show why your thesis still stands.
- **[concession (three-paragraph close)](days/d29.html#term-concession-three)** — Day 29, Writing & Rhetoric · Concession and return in a three-paragraph close. A real limit or opposing point, granted in the closing paragraph, after which you return to the thesis in new words.
- **[concession paragraph](days/d38.html#term-concession-paragraph-w8)** — Day 38, Writing & Rhetoric · The concession paragraph, revisited. The middle paragraph of a three-paragraph essay, when its job is to grant a real point on the other side, in that side’s terms, and then return to the essay’s own job without a sneer or a sudden new subject.
- **[concluding sentence](days/d16.html#term-concluding-sentence)** — Day 16, Writing & Rhetoric · From one paragraph to two: the two-paragraph outline. The last sentence of a paragraph or of a short essay that does not merely repeat the topic sentence, but closes the thought — often by stating a consequence, a limit, or a return to the thesis in new words.
- **[confirmation (progymnasmata)](days/d146.html#term-confirmation-progymnasmata)** — Day 146, Writing · A confirmation, a first look, after last week’s refutation. A confirmation, in this course, answers a claim and shows why it holds.
- **[conjunctive adverb](days/d13.html#term-conjunctive-adverb)** — Day 13, Writing & Rhetoric · The semicolon and the conjunctive adverb. A word such as however, therefore, moreover, meanwhile, or otherwise that shows the relation between two complete sentences.
- **[coordinated series](days/d21.html#term-coordinated-series)** — Day 21, Writing & Rhetoric · Parallelism in a series. Two or more elements joined by a coordinating conjunction (and, or, but) or by punctuation that does the same job, which must be parallel if they share a single grammatical slot.
- **[copia](days/d02.html#term-copia)** — Day 2, Writing & Rhetoric · Imitatio: copywork and copia. Plenty of expression: many accurate ways of saying the same idea, produced on purpose.
- **[dangling modifier](days/d23.html#term-dangling-modifier)** — Day 23, Writing & Rhetoric · Participial phrases, and dangling modifiers. A participial phrase that is not clearly attached to the noun it is meant to describe, so the sentence seems to say the wrong thing.
- **[diction](days/d07.html#term-diction)** — Day 7, Writing & Rhetoric · Franklin's method, part 2: reconstruction. The choice of words: precise, ordinary, or borrowed from the model, each chosen on purpose.
- **[encomium (progymnasmata)](days/d156.html#term-encomium-progymnasmata)** — Day 156, Writing · An encomium, a first look, after last week’s commonplace. An encomium, in this course, is a praise of a general type, or of an already-taught work, not a praise of a living person, and not a first look at a new untried claim.
- **[essay close](days/d83.html#term-essay-close)** — Day 83, Writing & Rhetoric · The opening and the close. The close of this short analytical essay returns to the thesis in new wording, after the body and the other side have been heard, and stops.
- **[essay opening](days/d83.html#term-essay-opening)** — Day 83, Writing & Rhetoric · The opening and the close. The opening of this short analytical essay names the set text or question and states the thesis, without a long warm-up and without announcing the outline (“In this essay I will…”).
- **[ethos](days/d10.html#term-ethos)** — Day 10, Writing & Rhetoric · Trivium III, rhetoric: the three appeals. The character the speaker or writer seems to have: credible, careful, fair.
- **[evidence](days/d08.html#term-evidence)** — Day 8, Writing & Rhetoric · Trivium II, logic: claim, evidence, warrant. The material that is supposed to support a claim: a quotation, a fact, a detail from a source, given accurately.
- **[expansion](days/d01.html#term-expansion)** — Day 1, Writing & Rhetoric · The sentence: kernels and expansion. The deliberate adding of information to a kernel by answering a short list of questions (when, where, why, how), without losing the original verb.
- **[fable (progymnasmata)](days/d121.html#term-fable-progymnasmata)** — Day 121, Writing · A fable, a first look: Townsend’s Crow and the Pitcher. A fable, as the first of the classical progymnasmata, is a short invented narrative, often with animals that act and speak, that ends in a stated moral.
- **[fair two-claim plan](days/d37.html#term-fair-two-claim-plan)** — Day 37, Writing & Rhetoric · A three-paragraph plan: two claims, stated fairly. A three-paragraph outline whose job is to state two opposing or contrasting claims fairly, without a verdict that depends on scenes not yet taught.
- **[false dilemma](days/d73.html#term-false-dilemma)** — Day 73, Writing & Rhetoric · The false dilemma. A fallacy in which two options are presented as if they were the only ones, when at least one other genuine option has been left out.
- **[Franklin hints](days/d24.html#term-franklin-hints-w5)** — Day 24, Writing & Rhetoric · Franklin’s method: hints from the new model. Notes that record the joints and moves of a model paragraph — order of ideas, not full sentences — from which you later reconstruct a paragraph of your own and then compare it with the model.
- **[Franklin hints from Inferno IV.121–151](days/d101.html#term-franklin-hints-from-inferno-iv-121-151)** — Day 101, Writing & Rhetoric · Franklin’s method: hints from Inferno IV.121–151. Franklin’s method, already taught, begins by making short hints from a set passage.
- **[Franklin hints from Inferno IV.25–42](days/d96.html#term-franklin-hints-from-inferno-iv-25-42)** — Day 96, Writing & Rhetoric · Franklin’s method: hints from Inferno IV.25–42. Franklin’s method, already taught, begins by making short hints from a set passage, then later reconstructing from the hints alone.
- **[Franklin hints from Inferno V.1–72](days/d106.html#term-franklin-hints-from-inferno-v-1-72)** — Day 106, Writing · Franklin’s method: hints from Inferno V.1–72. Franklin’s method, already taught, is used today to make a short hint-list from Inferno V, lines 1–72, in Longfellow’s 1867 translation.
- **[Franklin hints from Inferno VI.1–57](days/d111.html#term-franklin-hints-from-inferno-vi-1-57)** — Day 111, Writing · Franklin’s method: hints from Inferno VI.1–57. Franklin’s method, already taught, is used here to make hints from Longfellow’s Inferno VI, lines 1–57, and not to invent a scene the poem does not give.
- **[Franklin reconstruction of Inferno IV.121–151](days/d102.html#term-franklin-reconstruction-of-inferno-iv-121-151)** — Day 102, Writing & Rhetoric · Franklin’s method: reconstruction of Inferno IV.121–151. The second step of Franklin’s method, already taught, is to reconstruct the passage from the hints alone, then compare with the original.
- **[Franklin reconstruction of Inferno IV.25–42](days/d97.html#term-franklin-reconstruction-of-inferno-iv-25-42)** — Day 97, Writing & Rhetoric · Franklin’s method: reconstruction of Inferno IV.25–42. The second step of Franklin’s method, already taught, is to reconstruct the passage from the hints alone, then compare with the original.
- **[Franklin reconstruction of Inferno V.1–72](days/d107.html#term-franklin-reconstruction-of-inferno-v-1-72)** — Day 107, Writing · Franklin’s method: a reconstruction from the Inferno V.1–72 hints. Franklin’s method, already taught, is used today to reconstruct Inferno V.1–72 from yesterday’s hints, in full sentences, keeping the author’s order and Longfellow’s set wording where a hint named a phrase.
- **[Franklin reconstruction of Inferno VI.1–57](days/d112.html#term-franklin-reconstruction-of-inferno-vi-1-57)** — Day 112, Writing · Franklin’s method: a reconstruction from the Inferno VI.1–57 hints. Franklin’s method, already taught, is used here to reconstruct Inferno VI.1–57 from yesterday’s hints, then to compare the reconstruction with Longfellow’s wording, without adding a scene the poem does not give.
- **[Franklin's method](days/d04.html#term-franklins-method)** — Day 4, Writing & Rhetoric · Franklin's method, part 1: making hints. A practice from Benjamin Franklin’s Autobiography: take a passage, jot brief hints of the ideas in order, put the passage away, and rewrite from the hints; then compare with the original.
- **[Franklin's method, with hints](days/d48.html#term-franklins-method-hints-w10)** — Day 48, Writing & Rhetoric · Franklin's method: hints from Dryden's Book II opening. A reconstruction, from brief notes, of a short set passage already attributed, followed by a comparison with the original words.
- **[Franklin’s hints, from a short opening](days/d43.html#term-franklin-hints-from-a-short-opening)** — Day 43, Writing & Rhetoric · Franklin’s method: hints from Dryden’s opening. Franklin’s method, already taught: make brief hints from a model, set the model aside, and reconstruct a sentence of your own from the hints, then compare — without pretending you have memorized a longer extract.
- **[Franklin’s method, with hints from the set extract](days/d53.html#term-franklins-method-hints-from-the-set-extract-w11)** — Day 53, Writing & Rhetoric · Franklin’s method: hints from the set Pliny extract. Franklin’s reconstruction method, already taught: set the extract aside, keep a few hints, and try to rebuild the sentences in your own wording; then compare, honestly, with Firth’s translation.
- **[grammar](days/d05.html#term-grammar)** — Day 5, Writing & Rhetoric · Trivium I, grammar: the anatomy of a sentence. In the trivium, the craft of accurate sentences: parts of speech, agreement, and punctuation that make the structure visible.
- **[grounds (Toulmin)](days/d61.html#term-grounds-toulmin)** — Day 61, Writing & Rhetoric · The Toulmin model: claim, grounds, and warrant. Toulmin’s name for the supporting material under a claim — a fact, a detail, or a short quotation given accurately.
- **[hint (for reconstruction)](days/d14.html#term-hint-word)** — Day 14, Writing & Rhetoric · Franklin's method: hints from a new model. A brief cue — a word or a short phrase — written down in order, after you have read a model and turned it over, so that you can later rebuild the passage from the cues rather than from memory of the exact wording.
- **[hint (in Franklin's method)](days/d04.html#term-franklin-hint)** — Day 4, Writing & Rhetoric · Franklin's method, part 1: making hints. A brief cue for an idea, written in order, just strong enough to recall the thought and not the original wording.
- **[hints from a set simile](days/d91.html#term-hints-from-a-set-simile)** — Day 91, Writing & Rhetoric · Franklin’s method: hints from the leaves simile. Hints from a set simile, in Franklin’s method already taught, are brief cues for each line or tightly bound group, written in order, strong enough to recall the thought and not the original wording.
- **[hints from a set verse inscription](days/d86.html#term-hints-from-a-set-verse-inscription)** — Day 86, Writing & Rhetoric · Franklin’s method: hints from the Inferno III gate. Hints from a set verse inscription, in Franklin’s method already taught, are brief cues for each line or tightly bound group of lines, written in order, strong enough to recall the thought and not the original wording.
- **[imitatio](days/d02.html#term-imitatio)** — Day 2, Writing & Rhetoric · Imitatio: copywork and copia. The Renaissance school practice of first copying an excellent passage exactly, noticing how it is built, then writing a new version that keeps the structure and changes the content.
- **[imitatio](days/d22.html#term-imitatio-declaration)** — Day 22, Writing & Rhetoric · Imitatio: a new public-domain model. A writing exercise in which you keep the structure of a model sentence or passage and change the content.
- **[imitatio of a series](days/d32.html#term-imitatio-series)** — Day 32, Writing & Rhetoric · Imitatio: Bacon, “Of Studies,” the opening series. Imitatio here means writing a new sentence that keeps the grammatical shape of a model — today, a three-part series — without copying the model’s content or lifting a long passage.
- **[integrated quotation](days/d36.html#term-integrated-quotation)** — Day 36, Writing & Rhetoric · Integrating a short quotation. A short, attributed quotation placed inside a sentence or paragraph of your own, so that the quotation supports the paragraph’s job and does not replace the topic sentence.
- **[invective (progymnasmata)](days/d161.html#term-invective-progymnasmata)** — Day 161, Writing · An invective, a first look, after last week’s encomium. An invective, in this course, is a blame of a general type, or of an already-taught fault of a type, not a blame of a living person, and not a first look at a new untried claim.
- **[kernel](days/d01.html#term-kernel)** — Day 1, Writing & Rhetoric · The sentence: kernels and expansion. A short, complete sentence that can later be expanded: a subject and a verb, with nothing added yet.
- **[limit of a claim](days/d19.html#term-limit-of-claim)** — Day 19, Writing & Rhetoric · Concession in a two-paragraph argument. A precise statement of what the thesis does not try to prove, so that the argument stays as strong as the evidence.
- **[logos](days/d10.html#term-logos)** — Day 10, Writing & Rhetoric · Trivium III, rhetoric: the three appeals. The appeal to reason: the structure of claim, evidence, and warrant, and the consistency of the argument.
- **[maxim (progymnasmata)](days/d136.html#term-maxim-progymnasmata)** — Day 136, Writing · A maxim, a first look, after last week’s chreia. A maxim in this course is a short general precept, not attributed to one named person on one occasion.
- **[model passage](days/d12.html#term-model-passage)** — Day 12, Writing & Rhetoric · Imitatio: a new model passage. A short, complete stretch of writing chosen for imitation: first copied exactly, then analyzed for its choices, then varied.
- **[multi-paragraph outline](days/d78.html#term-multi-paragraph-outline)** — Day 78, Writing & Rhetoric · A multi-paragraph outline, for an analytical essay. A multi-paragraph outline plans an analytical essay before the draft: a thesis, then a short plan for each paragraph (a claim and the grounds or quotations it will use), written in order.
- **[narrative (progymnasmata)](days/d126.html#term-narrative-progymnasmata)** — Day 126, Writing · A narrative, a first look, after last week’s fable. A narrative, as the next exercise after last week’s fable, is a short account of a complete action, told in order, that makes clear who did what, when, and where.
- **[opening that places the occasion](days/d12.html#term-occasional-opening)** — Day 12, Writing & Rhetoric · Imitatio: a new model passage. A first sentence that locates the writing in time, place, or circumstance before it states a claim, so the reader knows why the words are being spoken or written now.
- **[other-side paragraph](days/d82.html#term-other-side-paragraph)** — Day 82, Writing & Rhetoric · The other-side paragraph. An other-side paragraph states a real objection or a real alternative reading, in a form that the other side could accept, and then answers it without sneering.
- **[paragraph from the outline](days/d79.html#term-paragraph-from-the-outline)** — Day 79, Writing & Rhetoric · From the outline, one paragraph of the essay. A paragraph written from a multi-paragraph outline opens with the planned claim, introduces and explains a short quotation or ground, and stays inside that paragraph’s job.
- **[parallelism](days/d21.html#term-parallelism)** — Day 21, Writing & Rhetoric · Parallelism in a series. A series of words, phrases, or clauses written in the same grammatical shape, so the parts can be heard as a matched set.
- **[parenthetical interruption](days/d33.html#term-parenthetical-interruption)** — Day 33, Writing & Rhetoric · A parenthetical interruption that does not break the series. A word, phrase, or clause inserted into a sentence, often set off by commas or dashes, that adds a comment without taking over the grammatical job of the sentence.
- **[participial phrase](days/d23.html#term-participial-phrase)** — Day 23, Writing & Rhetoric · Participial phrases, and dangling modifiers. A phrase headed by a participle (often an -ing or a past form used as a modifier) that adds a circumstance or a description to a noun or to a clause, without itself being the main verb.
- **[pathos](days/d10.html#term-pathos)** — Day 10, Writing & Rhetoric · Trivium III, rhetoric: the three appeals. The appeal to feeling: the reader’s pity, anger, or loyalty, used on purpose and still tied to a claim.
- **[period (a long constructed sentence)](days/d14.html#term-period-sentence)** — Day 14, Writing & Rhetoric · Franklin's method: hints from a new model. A sentence that holds several related clauses in a designed order, often delaying the main point until the thought is complete.
- **[post hoc](days/d77.html#term-post-hoc)** — Day 77, Writing & Rhetoric · Post hoc. Post hoc (short for post hoc, ergo propter hoc: after this, therefore because of this) is the flaw of treating a later event as caused by an earlier one only because it came after.
- **[précis](days/d15.html#term-precis)** — Day 15, Writing & Rhetoric · Summary and précis. A very tight summary, often a third of the original length or less, that keeps the argument’s structure and proportion: what the passage spends most time on stays most visible, and what it mentions once stays brief.
- **[précis](days/d25.html#term-precis-w5)** — Day 25, Writing & Rhetoric · Précis of the week’s model. A short complete statement, in your own words, of the meaning (and, if asked, of the structure) of a passage, without examples, decoration, or your opinion of the passage.
- **[précis of a draft](days/d40.html#term-precis-of-draft)** — Day 40, Writing & Rhetoric · Précis and a close: the week’s essay, and the play if it has now been finished. A compact restatement, in your own words, of what your three-paragraph essay did — its two claims, its grant or contrast, and its close — not a new essay and not a string of quotations.
- **[précis of a model sentence](days/d35.html#term-precis-model-sentence)** — Day 35, Writing & Rhetoric · Précis of the week’s model. A compact restatement, in your own words, of what a passage does — here, what a one-sentence model does, not a new essay on study.
- **[précis of a set building extract](days/d88.html#term-precis-of-a-set-building-extract)** — Day 88, Writing & Rhetoric · A précis of Dewing I.1.50–53. A précis of a set building extract, using the précis already taught, is a short restatement in your own words that keeps Dewing’s points, order, and proportion, without adding a new opinion and without simply quoting.
- **[précis of a short opening](days/d44.html#term-precis-of-a-short-opening)** — Day 44, Writing & Rhetoric · A précis of the set opening, in your own words. A very short restatement, in your own words, of the set opening’s claims, without new plot, without unset lines, and without borrowing Dryden’s wording as if it were yours.
- **[précis of Dewing I.1.64–66](days/d98.html#term-precis-of-dewing-i-1-64-66)** — Day 98, Writing & Rhetoric · A précis of Dewing I.1.64–66. A précis, already taught, is a short restatement in your own words that keeps the author’s points, order, and proportion.
- **[précis of Dewing I.1.67–73](days/d99.html#term-precis-of-dewing-i-1-67-73)** — Day 99, Writing & Rhetoric · A précis of Dewing I.1.67–73. A précis of Dewing I.1.67–73 keeps his points, order, and proportion: the church was built not with money alone but also with labor of the mind and the other powers of the soul, as he will “shew”; the eastern arch was unfinished in the middle; the piers began to crack; Anthemius and Isidorus, having no hope in their skill, went to the Emperor; he commanded them to complete the curve, saying, “For when it rests upon itself, it will no longer need the props beneath it”; and, because there are many witnesses, Dewing will tell it; the artisans did as instructed, and the arch hung secure.
- **[précis of Dewing I.1.74–76](days/d103.html#term-precis-of-dewing-i-1-74-76)** — Day 103, Writing & Rhetoric · A précis of Dewing I.1.74–76. A précis, already taught, is a short restatement in your own words that keeps the author’s points, order, and proportion.
- **[précis of Dewing I.1.77–78](days/d104.html#term-precis-of-dewing-i-1-77-78)** — Day 104, Writing & Rhetoric · A précis of Dewing I.1.77–78. A précis of Dewing I.1.77–78 keeps his points, order, and proportion: the Emperor again met the situation with a remedy; he ordered them to remove at once the upper parts of the masonry that were strained, the portions in contact with the arches, and to put them back much late…
- **[précis of Dewing I.2.13–16](days/d109.html#term-precis-of-dewing-i-2-13-16)** — Day 109, Writing · A précis of Dewing, Buildings I.2.13–16. A précis, already taught, is used today on Dewing I.2.13–16.
- **[précis of Dewing I.2.17–19](days/d113.html#term-precis-of-dewing-i-2-17-19)** — Day 113, Writing · A précis of Dewing, Buildings I.2.17–19. A précis, already taught, condenses Dewing, Buildings I.2.17–19, without inventing an official shrine count or an income figure, and without cutting I.2.17 mid-sentence.
- **[précis of Dewing I.2.1–12](days/d108.html#term-precis-of-dewing-i-2-1-12)** — Day 108, Writing · A précis of Dewing, Buildings I.2.1–12. A précis, already taught, is a short restatement in your own words that keeps the author’s points, order, and proportion.
- **[précis of Dewing’s galleries and stones](days/d93.html#term-precis-of-dewings-galleries-and-stones)** — Day 93, Writing & Rhetoric · A précis of Dewing I.1.58–60. A précis of Dewing I.1.58–60, using the précis already taught, is a short restatement in your own words that keeps Dewing’s points, order, and proportion: the galleries of the women’s side and the surrounding colonnades, which he will not try to describe fittingly or enumerate; then the beauty of the columns and stones, imagined as a meadow in full bloom, with purple, green, crimson, and flashing white, and Nature varying the most contrasting colors.
- **[précis practice, Dewing I.2.13–19](days/d114.html#term-precis-practice-dewing-i-2-13-19)** — Day 114, Writing · A précis of Dewing, Buildings I.2.13–19, combined. A second précis pass over the already-taught close of Buildings I.2: last week’s I.2.13–16 (Eirenê and the hospice of Samson) with today’s I.2.17–19 (the two further hospices, and Dewing’s refusal to catalogue the rest).
- **[quotation](days/d18.html#term-quotation)** — Day 18, Writing & Rhetoric · Quotations and attribution. Someone else’s exact words, placed in quotation marks (or indented as a block, for a longer extract), introduced by a sentence of your own, and followed by an explanation in your own words.
- **[reconstruction](days/d17.html#term-reconstruction)** — Day 17, Writing & Rhetoric · Franklin's method: reconstruction from the hints. The step in Franklin’s method in which you rebuild a model passage from your hints alone, in complete sentences, and only then compare what you wrote with the original to see which choices you missed.
- **[reconstruction (three paragraphs)](days/d27.html#term-reconstruction-three)** — Day 27, Writing & Rhetoric · Reconstruction from a three-paragraph outline. A Franklin step in which you write a short three-paragraph piece from a hint-list only, then compare it with your outline or with a model, and note the joints you kept or missed.
- **[refutation (progymnasmata)](days/d141.html#term-refutation-progymnasmata)** — Day 141, Writing · A refutation, a first look, after last week’s maxim. A refutation in this course examines a claim and gives reasons why the claim should not be accepted.
- **[revision](days/d07.html#term-revision)** — Day 7, Writing & Rhetoric · Franklin's method, part 2: reconstruction. A second (or third) pass that changes structure, order, or wording after a draft exists, using the original as a standard of comparison.
- **[revision pass](days/d20.html#term-revision-pass)** — Day 20, Writing & Rhetoric · Two-week check: a two-paragraph essay. A second writing, after the draft is complete, in which you check a short list — thesis, topic sentences, evidence, attribution, concession or limit, concluding sentence, and punctuation of joins — and change the sentences that fail, rather than recopying the whole for neatness.
- **[second body paragraph](days/d81.html#term-second-body-paragraph)** — Day 81, Writing & Rhetoric · A second body paragraph, from the outline. A second body paragraph carries the next main reason or next stage of the already-made outline, in the same order, with its own evidence and a sentence that ties that evidence back to the thesis.
- **[semicolon](days/d13.html#term-semicolon)** — Day 13, Writing & Rhetoric · The semicolon and the conjunctive adverb. The mark (;) that can join two closely related complete sentences without a coordinating conjunction.
- **[sentence combining](days/d11.html#term-sentence-combining)** — Day 11, Writing & Rhetoric · Sentence combining and transitions. The skill of joining two or more short, complete sentences into one sentence that keeps all the meaning and is easier to follow, using a conjunction, a relative clause, an appositive, or a joining punctuation mark.
- **[single-paragraph outline](days/d06.html#term-single-paragraph-outline)** — Day 6, Writing & Rhetoric · The single-paragraph outline. A plan for one paragraph: a topic sentence (a claim), then a short list of the points or evidence in order, written before the draft.
- **[straw man](days/d72.html#term-straw-man)** — Day 72, Writing & Rhetoric · The straw man. A fallacy in which a person answers a weaker, distorted, or invented version of someone else’s claim, as if that version were the claim that had been made.
- **[subordinate clause](days/d03.html#term-subordinate-clause)** — Day 3, Writing & Rhetoric · Appositives and subordinating conjunctions. A clause that cannot stand alone as a sentence; it starts with a word such as because, although, when, who, or that, and it depends on a main clause.
- **[summary](days/d15.html#term-summary)** — Day 15, Writing & Rhetoric · Summary and précis. A shorter account, in your own words, of what a passage says, in the same order, without new examples and without your opinion of it.
- **[three-paragraph draft](days/d39.html#term-three-paragraph-draft-w8)** — Day 39, Writing & Rhetoric · A three-paragraph essay, drafted. The three paragraphs written out in full from a plan: a first paragraph that sets one claim, a middle paragraph that states the other claim or a concession, and a close that keeps the promise of the topic sentences.
- **[three-paragraph essay](days/d30.html#term-three-paragraph-essay)** — Day 30, Writing & Rhetoric · Two-week check: a three-paragraph essay. A complete short argument or exposition with one thesis, three paragraphs (opening, support, close), at least one attributed piece of evidence or a précis of a source, a real concession or limit, and a concluding return to the thesis in new words.
- **[three-paragraph outline](days/d26.html#term-three-paragraph-outline)** — Day 26, Writing & Rhetoric · The three-paragraph outline. A plan for a short essay of three paragraphs: an opening paragraph that states a thesis, a middle paragraph that develops one line of support (with evidence or a précis of a source), and a closing paragraph that includes a concession or a limit and then returns to the thesis in new words.
- **[topic sentence](days/d06.html#term-topic-sentence)** — Day 6, Writing & Rhetoric · The single-paragraph outline. The claim that opens a paragraph and that the rest of the paragraph must support.
- **[Toulmin model](days/d61.html#term-toulmin-model)** — Day 61, Writing & Rhetoric · The Toulmin model: claim, grounds, and warrant. A named way of analyzing an argument into a claim, the data or grounds that support it, and a warrant — the general rule that makes those grounds count as support for that claim.
- **[transition](days/d11.html#term-transition)** — Day 11, Writing & Rhetoric · Sentence combining and transitions. A word or short phrase that shows the relation between two sentences or two parts of a sentence: addition, contrast, cause, result, or sequence (for example, however, therefore, next, for instance).
- **[two-paragraph essay](days/d20.html#term-two-paragraph-essay)** — Day 20, Writing & Rhetoric · Two-week check: a two-paragraph essay. A complete short argument with one thesis, two paragraphs (each with a topic sentence and support), at least one introduced and explained quotation or a precise précis of a source, a real concession or a limit, and a concluding sentence that returns to the thesis in new words.
- **[two-paragraph outline](days/d16.html#term-two-paragraph-outline)** — Day 16, Writing & Rhetoric · From one paragraph to two: the two-paragraph outline. A short plan for a piece that has two paragraphs, each with its own topic sentence and supporting points, under one overall thesis.
- **[variety](days/d09.html#term-variety)** — Day 9, Writing & Rhetoric · Franklin's method, part 3: verse into prose. Deliberate change in sentence length and opening, so that a paragraph is not a string of the same pattern.
- **[warrant](days/d08.html#term-warrant)** — Day 8, Writing & Rhetoric · Trivium II, logic: claim, evidence, warrant. The reason that the evidence counts as support for the claim: the general rule or principle that joins the two.
- **[week's writing close](days/d50.html#term-weeks-writing-close-w10)** — Day 50, Writing & Rhetoric · A close: caution, quotation, and a precis. A short close that gathers this week's writing: a because / but / so source-caution sentence, an attributed Dryden line, and a precis of the set opening only.
- **[week’s writing close](days/d45.html#term-weeks-writing-close)** — Day 45, Writing & Rhetoric · A close: caution, quotation, and a précis. A short close that gathers the week’s writing: a source-caution sentence, an attributed Dryden line, and a précis of the set opening only.
- **[week’s writing close](days/d55.html#term-weeks-writing-close-w11)** — Day 55, Writing & Rhetoric · A close: caution, quotation, and a précis. A Friday close of this week’s writing: a because / but / so caution about a later letter in translation; an attributed Firth quotation; Franklin’s hints; and a précis of the set extract only.
## Latin

- **[a negative result with ut nōn, a first look](days/d167.html#term-a-negative-result-with-ut-non-a-first-look)** — Day 167, Latin · A negative result with ut nōn, a first look. A negative result, in this first look, is marked by ut nōn and the already-taught present subjunctive, not by nē.
- **[a purpose clause with nē and ēgredior](days/d162.html#term-a-purpose-clause-with-ne-and-egredior)** — Day 162, Latin · A purpose clause with nē and ēgredior, a first look. A negative purpose may also use the already-taught present subjunctive of ēgredior: nē ēgrediātur, in order that he not go out, or in order that she not go out.
- **[a purpose clause with nē, a first look](days/d161.html#term-a-purpose-clause-with-ne-a-first-look)** — Day 161, Latin · A purpose clause with nē, a first look. A purpose clause with nē, in this first look, marks a negative purpose: in order that something not happen.
- **[a purpose clause with ut and ēgredior](days/d157.html#term-a-purpose-clause-with-ut-and-egredior)** — Day 157, Latin · A purpose clause with ut and ēgredior, a first look. A purpose clause may also use the already-taught present subjunctive of ēgredior: ut ēgrediātur, in order that he go out, or in order that she go out.
- **[a purpose clause with ut, a first look](days/d156.html#term-a-purpose-clause-with-ut-a-first-look)** — Day 156, Latin · A purpose clause with ut, a first look. A purpose clause with ut, in this first look, uses ut and the present subjunctive already taught, to mark the purpose of an action.
- **[a result clause with ut, a first look](days/d166.html#term-a-result-clause-with-ut-a-first-look)** — Day 166, Latin · A result clause with ut, a first look. A result clause with ut, in this first look, marks what happens as a result, not a purpose.
- **[a result clause with ēgredior, a first look](days/d168.html#term-a-result-clause-with-egredior-a-first-look)** — Day 168, Latin · A result clause with ēgredior, a first look. A result clause may also use the already-taught present subjunctive of ēgredior.
- **[a short original reading](days/d44.html#term-short-original-reading)** — Day 44, Latin · A short original reading, using Capitula I–X only. A few original Latin sentences, written for this lesson, that use only patterns from Capitula I–X.
- **[a short original reading](days/d49.html#term-short-original-reading-w10)** — Day 49, Latin · A short original reading, using Capitula I–X only. A few original Latin sentences, written for this lesson, that use only patterns from Capitula I–X.
- **[a short original reading](days/d54.html#term-short-original-reading-w11)** — Day 54, Latin · A short original reading, using Capitula I–X only. A short original I–X text, not a retelling of Pliny, Dryden, or 79 CE, using this week’s reviewed patterns: et, sed, an accusative object, cūr / quia, and is, ea, eum, eam.
- **[ablative](days/d11.html#term-ablative)** — Day 11, Latin · Capitulum V, part 1: the ablative, and in, ex, ab. The case used in this chapter after the prepositions in (when it means ‘in’ or ‘on’), ex (‘out of’), and ab (‘away from’), and later also after cum and sine.
- **[ablative absolute](days/d91.html#term-ablative-absolute)** — Day 91, Latin · Capitulum XVI: the ablative absolute, a first look. An ablative absolute is a phrase in the ablative, loosely attached to the main sentence, that gives a circumstance — often time or attendant situation.
- **[ablative absolute with a perfect participle](days/d96.html#term-ablative-absolute-perfect-participle)** — Day 96, Latin · The ablative absolute with a perfect participle. An ablative absolute may be built with a noun and a perfect participle, both ablative.
- **[ablative of agent](days/d19.html#term-agent-ablative)** — Day 19, Latin · Capitulum VI, part 5: a first look at the passive. The construction ā or ab + ablative that names the person who does the action of a passive verb.
- **[ablative of price](days/d29.html#term-ablative-of-price)** — Day 29, Latin · Capitulum VIII, part 4: the ablative of price, and tantus. With verbs of buying, selling, and costing (emere, vēndere, cōnstāre), the price is put in the ablative, with no preposition: you pay with that money.
- **[accusative](days/d06.html#term-accusative)** — Day 6, Latin · Capitulum III, part 1: the accusative. The case that often marks the direct object: the person or thing the verb acts on.
- **[accusative and infinitive (AcI)](days/d63.html#term-accusative-and-infinitive)** — Day 63, Latin · Capitulum XI, part 3: the accusative and infinitive. A construction after verbs such as vidēre, dīcere, and putāre: an accusative names the person or thing who does the action of an infinitive.
- **[AcI with esse](days/d64.html#term-aci-with-esse)** — Day 64, Latin · Capitulum XI, part 4: AcI with esse. The same accusative-and-infinitive construction, with esse as the infinitive.
- **[ad and ab](days/d14.html#term-ad-versus-ab)** — Day 14, Latin · Capitulum VI, part 1: prepositions with the accusative. A pair of opposites: ad + accusative means ‘to, toward’; ab + accusative is wrong — ab takes the ablative and means ‘away from’.
- **[adjective agreement](days/d46.html#term-adjective-agreement)** — Day 46, Latin · Review of Capitula I–X: adjective agreement. An adjective takes the gender, number, and case of the noun it describes.
- **[agreement](days/d02.html#term-agreement)** — Day 2, Latin · Capitulum I, part 2. The rule that an adjective takes the same gender and number (and, later, the same case) as the noun it describes.
- **[alter](days/d76.html#term-alter)** — Day 76, Latin · Capitulum XIV: uter, neuter, alter, and uterque. Alter (altera, alterum) means the one, or the other, of two.
- **[at and antequam](days/d84.html#term-at-and-antequam)** — Day 84, Latin · Capitulum XV: sī, nisi, at, and antequam. At means but on the other hand; it stands first in its clause and sets two sides or two people against each other.
- **[Capitula I–X review](days/d45.html#term-capitula-i-x-review)** — Day 45, Latin · Capitula I–X: Friday check. A check that uses only Latin already taught in Capitula I–X: first, second, and third family nouns, a place form, a present finite verb, and an active infinitive, in original sentences.
- **[Capitulum IX check](days/d35.html#term-capitulum-ix-check)** — Day 35, Latin · Capitulum IX: Friday check. A Friday check of this week’s Latin: third-declension nominative, genitive (-is), and accusative (-em); numerals that decline (ūnus, duo, trēs) and numerals that do not (quattuor, centum); quot?; magnus numerus + genitive plural; cēterī.
- **[Capitulum X check](days/d40.html#term-capitulum-x-check)** — Day 40, Latin · Capitulum X: Friday check. A Friday check of this week’s Latin: third-declension nouns homō, leō, and pēs (stem from the genitive; accusative in -em); posse + a present infinitive, active or passive (vidēre / vidērī, vocāre / vocārī, habēre / habērī; also esse and īre in the active).
- **[case](days/d08.html#term-case)** — Day 8, Latin · Capitulum III, part 3 and review. The role a noun or adjective is playing in the sentence, shown by its ending: subject, possessor, object, and others still to come.
- **[case after a preposition](days/d20.html#term-case-after-preposition)** — Day 20, Latin · Capitula V–VI: two-week check. The rule that the preposition, not a translation from English, chooses the case: one closed list takes the ablative (in, ex, ab, cum, sine), and one takes the accusative (ad, ante, post, inter, prope, circum, apud, per).
- **[comparative adjective](days/d68.html#term-comparative-adjective)** — Day 68, Latin · Capitulum XII, part 3: the comparative (-ior, -ius). The degree of an adjective that compares two things (in English, often the -er form, or “more”).
- **[conjugation](days/d10.html#term-conjugation)** — Day 10, Latin · Capitulum IV, part 2 and two-week check. The pattern of endings a verb follows as the person, number, and (later) tense change.
- **[cuius](days/d05.html#term-cuius)** — Day 5, Latin · Capitulum II, part 2: cuius? and the genitive in use. The question word ‘whose?’, asking for a possessor.
- **[cum and sine](days/d12.html#term-cum-sine)** — Day 12, Latin · Capitulum V, part 2: cum and sine; rooms of the villa. A pair of opposite prepositions, both used with the ablative: cum means ‘with’ (accompaniment), and sine means ‘without’.
- **[cūr and quia](days/d52.html#term-cur-and-quia)** — Day 52, Latin · Review of Capitula I–X: cūr and quia. A question-and-reason pair already taught in Capitula I–X (Day 7): cūr asks why; quia begins the because-clause.
- **[dative](days/d21.html#term-dative)** — Day 21, Latin · Capitulum VII, part 1: the dative, ‘to’ and ‘for’. The case that typically marks the person or thing to whom or for whom something is given, shown, or said.
- **[dative of interest](days/d78.html#term-dative-of-interest)** — Day 78, Latin · Capitulum XIV: mēcum and tēcum, and a dative of interest. The dative of interest (dativus commodi) names the person for whom something is so, or for whom it matters: Mihi necesse est labōrāre, it is necessary for me to work.
- **[dative of possession](days/d67.html#term-dative-of-possession)** — Day 67, Latin · Capitulum XII, part 2: the dative of possession. A use of the dative with a form of esse to say that someone has something: the possessor is in the dative, and the thing possessed is in the nominative.
- **[dative plural](days/d22.html#term-dative-plural)** — Day 22, Latin · Capitulum VII, part 2: dative plural, and verbs that take two objects. The ‘to/for’ form for more than one recipient, in the first and second declensions ending in -īs (puellīs, puerīs, servīs).
- **[declension](days/d10.html#term-declension)** — Day 10, Latin · Capitulum IV, part 2 and two-week check. The pattern of endings a noun (and its adjectives) follows as the case and number change.
- **[declining numerals](days/d33.html#term-declining-numerals)** — Day 33, Latin · Capitulum IX, part 3: numerals — ūnus, duo, trēs, and the rest. In Latin, ūnus, duo, and trēs change endings (they decline).
- **[deponent imperfect in use](days/d112.html#term-deponent-imperfect-in-use)** — Day 112, Latin · The deponent imperfect, used in short sentences. The imperfect of a deponent, already named yesterday, is used here in short original sentences: loquēbar and cōnābar for the ongoing or habitual past, still looking passive and translated as active.
- **[deponent imperfect review, week 23](days/d114.html#term-deponent-imperfect-review-week-23)** — Day 114, Latin · The deponent imperfect, a second mixed practice. A second mixed practice of this week’s deponent imperfect with last week’s perfect and with the present table, still in original sentences.
- **[deponent perfect in use](days/d107.html#term-deponent-perfect-in-use)** — Day 107, Latin · The deponent perfect, used in short sentences. The deponent perfect, yesterday’s first look, is used today in short sentences.
- **[deponent perfect participle in the absolute](days/d98.html#term-deponent-perfect-participle-in-the-absolute)** — Day 98, Latin · Deponent perfect participles in the absolute. A deponent verb, already taught in the third person, the infinitive, and the present participle, also has a perfect participle.
- **[deponent perfect review, week 22](days/d109.html#term-deponent-perfect-review-week-22)** — Day 109, Latin · The deponent perfect, a second mixed practice. A second short practice of the deponent perfect: locūtus sum and cōnātus sum in all six persons, with the participle agreeing, and with last week’s present and the two absolutes only as a contrast if a question asks for one.
- **[deponent present participle in the absolute](days/d93.html#term-deponent-present-participle-in-the-absolute)** — Day 93, Latin · Capitulum XVI: the ablative absolute with a deponent participle. A deponent verb, already taught in the third person and the infinitive, also has a present participle that is active in meaning, as deponents are.
- **[deponent tenses and absolutes, mixed](days/d108.html#term-deponent-tenses-and-absolutes-mixed)** — Day 108, Latin · Deponent perfect, present, and the two absolutes, mixed. Today the deponent perfect (locūtus sum) is mixed with the already-taught present (loquor) and with the two times of the ablative absolute: a present-participle absolute is contemporaneous with the main verb; a perfect-participle absolute is already complete when the main verb happens.
- **[deponent tenses, mixed](days/d113.html#term-deponent-tenses-mixed)** — Day 113, Latin · Deponent present, imperfect, and perfect, mixed. A mixed practice of the three deponent tenses already taught: present loquor, imperfect loquēbar, and perfect locūtus sum, still looking passive and translated as active, with the two already-taught absolutes kept distinct from the finite tenses.
- **[deponent verb](days/d88.html#term-deponent-verb)** — Day 88, Latin · Capitulum XVI: deponent verbs, a first look. A deponent verb has forms that look passive but are translated as active.
- **[deponents and absolutes, mixed](days/d103.html#term-deponents-and-absolutes-mixed)** — Day 103, Latin · Deponents in the present, mixed with last week’s absolutes. A short mixed practice: the present deponent table taught on Monday, and last week’s ablative absolutes (present participle, contemporaneous; perfect participle, already complete).
- **[direct object](days/d06.html#term-direct-object)** — Day 6, Latin · Capitulum III, part 1: the accusative. The person or thing that receives the action of the verb.
- **[domī](days/d87.html#term-domi)** — Day 87, Latin · Capitulum XV: domī, and a close of the chapter’s new grammar. Domī means at home, with no preposition.
- **[double object](days/d22.html#term-double-object)** — Day 22, Latin · Capitulum VII, part 2: dative plural, and verbs that take two objects. A verb such as dare can take both an accusative (the thing) and a dative (the person).
- **[est and sunt](days/d01.html#term-est-and-sunt)** — Day 1, Latin · Capitulum I, part 1. The Latin verbs meaning ‘is’ (est) and ‘are’ (sunt).
- **[et and sed](days/d51.html#term-et-and-sed)** — Day 51, Latin · Review of Capitula I–X: et, sed, and an accusative object. Two linking words already used in Capitula I–X: et joins, sed contrasts.
- **[eum / eam](days/d23.html#term-eum-eam)** — Day 23, Latin · Capitulum VII, part 3: reflexive sē, versus eum and eam. The accusative of is / ea: ‘him’ or ‘her’, referring to someone other than the subject (in the ordinary case taught here).
- **[first- and second-person present deponents](days/d101.html#term-first-and-second-person-present-deponents)** — Day 101, Latin · Deponents in the first and second persons, present tense. A deponent verb, already taught in the third person and the infinitive, also has first- and second-person present forms that look passive and are translated as active.
- **[first- and second-person present of sequor](days/d126.html#term-first-and-second-person-present-of-sequor)** — Day 126, Latin · The first- and second-person present of sequor, a first look. Sequor is an already-taught deponent, I follow, still looking passive and translated as active.
- **[first- and second-person present of ēgredior](days/d127.html#term-first-and-second-person-present-of-egredior)** — Day 127, Latin · The first- and second-person present of ēgredior, a first look. Ēgredior is a deponent, I go out, still looking passive and translated as active.
- **[first-and-second-family nouns](days/d41.html#term-first-second-family-nouns)** — Day 41, Latin · Review of Capitula I–X: first and second family nouns. Nouns whose endings follow the first pattern (often ending in -a in the nominative singular, like puella) or the second (often -us or -um, like hortus).
- **[fiō, fierī](days/d89.html#term-fio-fieri)** — Day 89, Latin · Capitulum XVI: fiō, and more deponents in the third person. Fiō, fierī means to become, to be made, or to happen.
- **[fortis, -e](days/d66.html#term-fortis-e)** — Day 66, Latin · Capitulum XII, part 1: third-declension adjectives (fortis, -e). A third-declension adjective meaning strong or brave.
- **[fourth declension](days/d69.html#term-fourth-declension)** — Day 69, Latin · Capitulum XII, part 4: the fourth declension (exercitus, metus). A declension whose nominative singular often ends in -us and whose genitive singular ends in -ūs (with a long ū), not the second-declension genitive -ī.
- **[future of sequor](days/d136.html#term-future-of-sequor)** — Day 136, Latin · The future of sequor, a first look. The future of sequor is a deponent future of the third conjugation: sequar, sequēris, sequētur, sequēmur, sequēminī, sequentur, I shall follow, you will follow, and so on.
- **[future of ēgredior](days/d137.html#term-future-of-egredior)** — Day 137, Latin · The future of ēgredior, a first look. The future of ēgredior is a deponent future of the third conjugation in -iō: ēgrediar, ēgrediēris, ēgrediētur, ēgrediēmur, ēgrediēminī, ēgredientur, I shall go out, you will go out, and so on.
- **[future perfect of sequor](days/d146.html#term-future-perfect-of-sequor)** — Day 146, Latin · The future perfect of sequor, a first look. The future perfect of sequor is secūtus erō, secūtus eris, secūtus erit, secūtī erimus, secūtī eritis, secūtī erunt.
- **[future perfect of ēgredior](days/d147.html#term-future-perfect-of-egredior)** — Day 147, Latin · The future perfect of ēgredior, a first look. The future perfect of ēgredior is ēgressus erō, ēgressus eris, ēgressus erit, ēgressī erimus, ēgressī eritis, ēgressī erunt.
- **[gender (Latin)](days/d02.html#term-latin-gender)** — Day 2, Latin · Capitulum I, part 2. The class of a Latin noun — masculine, feminine, or neuter — which adjectives and some pronouns must follow.
- **[genitive](days/d04.html#term-genitive)** — Day 4, Latin · Capitulum II, part 1: gender and the genitive. The case that often marks a possessor or a ‘of’ relation: whose thing it is.
- **[habitāre](days/d17.html#term-habitat)** — Day 17, Latin · Capitulum VI, part 3: the locative. The Latin verb meaning ‘to live, to dwell’.
- **[hic, haec, hoc](days/d25.html#term-hic-haec-hoc)** — Day 25, Latin · Capitulum VII: hic, haec, hoc, and a Friday check. The near demonstrative: ‘this’ (near the speaker), introduced in Capitulum VII.
- **[ille, illa, illud](days/d26.html#term-ille-illa-illud)** — Day 26, Latin · Capitulum VIII, part 1: ille, and hic against is. The far demonstrative: ‘that’ (farther from the speaker, or already distinguished from ‘this’).
- **[imperative](days/d09.html#term-imperative)** — Day 9, Latin · Capitulum IV, part 1: calling and commanding. The verb form used to give a command: ‘do this’ or ‘do not do this’.
- **[imperfect of a deponent](days/d111.html#term-imperfect-of-a-deponent)** — Day 111, Latin · The imperfect of a deponent, a first look. The imperfect of a deponent is formed on the present stem with the already-taught imperfect endings, and it still looks passive and is translated as active: loquēbar, loquēbāris, loquēbātur, loquēbāmur, loquēbāminī, loquēbantur.
- **[in + ablative](days/d24.html#term-in-ablative-place)** — Day 24, Latin · Capitulum VII, part 4: in with the accusative or the ablative. With in, the ablative marks place where: in hortō, in the garden — no motion into the place, just location.
- **[in + accusative](days/d24.html#term-in-accusative)** — Day 24, Latin · Capitulum VII, part 4: in with the accusative or the ablative. With in, the accusative marks motion into or onto a place: in hortum, into the garden.
- **[inclusive counting in Roman dates](days/d74.html#term-inclusive-counting-roman-dates)** — Day 74, Latin · Capitulum XIII: counting inclusively backward. Days other than the named day and the day before it are counted backward from the next Kalendae, Nonae, or Īdūs, and the starting point is counted as one of the days.
- **[indirect object](days/d21.html#term-indirect-object)** — Day 21, Latin · Capitulum VII, part 1: the dative, ‘to’ and ‘for’. The person or thing that receives the direct object, or for whom the action is done.
- **[inquit](days/d79.html#term-inquit)** — Day 79, Latin · Capitulum XIV: rēs, nihil, and inquit. Inquit means says or said, and it is typically inserted inside direct speech, rather than heading the sentence as a plain dīcit might.
- **[is, ea, eum, and eam](days/d53.html#term-is-ea-eum-eam)** — Day 53, Latin · Review of Capitula I–X: is, ea, eum, and eam. Third-person pronouns already taught in Capitula I–X (Day 7): is and ea are nominative (he, she); eum and eam are accusative (him, her).
- **[is, ea, id (reminder)](days/d26.html#term-is-ea-id-reminder)** — Day 26, Latin · Capitulum VIII, part 1: ille, and hic against is. The pronoun of reference: ‘he, she, it / that one just mentioned,’ without the strong near/far pointing of hic and ille.
- **[Kalendae](days/d72.html#term-kalendae)** — Day 72, Latin · Capitulum XIII: Kalendae, Nonae, and Īdūs. The first day of a Roman month (Kalendae, Kalendārum, feminine plural).
- **[licet](days/d83.html#term-licet)** — Day 83, Latin · Capitulum XV: nōs and vōs, and licet. Licet is an impersonal verb: it is allowed.
- **[locative](days/d17.html#term-locative)** — Day 17, Latin · Capitulum VI, part 3: the locative. A special case used with the names of towns, cities, and small islands (and a few other words) to answer ubi? (where?).
- **[loquitur and cōnātur](days/d88.html#term-loquitur-and-conatur)** — Day 88, Latin · Capitulum XVI: deponent verbs, a first look. Loquitur means he or she speaks; cōnātur means he or she tries.
- **[mixed Latin review, week 20](days/d99.html#term-mixed-latin-review-week-20)** — Day 99, Latin · Mixed absolutes, deponents, and fiō. A short mixed practice of this week’s perfect-participle absolute, last week’s present-participle absolute, deponents in the third person and the infinitive, and fiō / fierī.
- **[motion toward](days/d15.html#term-motion-toward)** — Day 15, Latin · Place where and motion: a Friday check. A construction that answers quō? (where to?): in this chapter, usually ad + accusative (ad vīllam), as opposed to place where (in + ablative) and motion from (ab or ex + ablative).
- **[mēcum](days/d78.html#term-mecum)** — Day 78, Latin · Capitulum XIV: mēcum and tēcum, and a dative of interest. Mēcum means with me. With the personal pronouns, cum is written after the pronoun and joined to it: mēcum, tēcum, sēcum, nōbīscum, vōbīscum, not cum mē.
- **[mēnsis](days/d71.html#term-mensis)** — Day 71, Latin · Capitulum XIII: the year and the months. The Latin word for a month.
- **[natural method](days/d01.html#term-natural-method)** — Day 1, Latin · Capitulum I, part 1. Learning Latin by reading Latin that is made understandable through pictures, context, and a few notes, rather than by starting from an English grammar list alone.
- **[neuter plural](days/d03.html#term-neuter-plural)** — Day 3, Latin · Capitulum I, part 3 and review. The plural of a neuter noun (often ending in -a, like oppida).
- **[nominative](days/d08.html#term-nominative)** — Day 8, Latin · Capitulum III, part 3 and review. The case of the subject: the form of the noun (and of its adjective) that you have used from the first chapter for who or what the sentence is about.
- **[Nonae](days/d72.html#term-nonae)** — Day 72, Latin · Capitulum XIII: Kalendae, Nonae, and Īdūs. A named day in each Roman month: the seventh of March, May, July, and October, and the fifth of the other months (Nonae, Nonārum, feminine plural).
- **[nouns with an infinitive](days/d39.html#term-nouns-with-infinitive)** — Day 39, Latin · Capitulum X, part 4: nouns and infinitives together. A mix, not a new ending: today’s sentences combine a third-declension noun (homō, leō, pēs, or last week’s rēx, vōx, canis) with posse + an active or passive infinitive.
- **[nōs and vōs](days/d83.html#term-nos-and-vos)** — Day 83, Latin · Capitulum XV: nōs and vōs, and licet. Nōs means we or us; vōs means you (plural).
- **[passive](days/d19.html#term-passive)** — Day 19, Latin · Capitulum VI, part 5: a first look at the passive. A verb form in which the subject is the person or thing that the action happens to, not the one who does it.
- **[perfect of a deponent](days/d106.html#term-perfect-of-a-deponent)** — Day 106, Latin · The perfect of a deponent, a first look. The perfect of a deponent is formed with the already-seen perfect participle plus sum: locūtus sum, locūtus es, locūtus est, locūtī sumus, locūtī estis, locūtī sunt.
- **[perfect of sequor](days/d141.html#term-perfect-of-sequor)** — Day 141, Latin · The perfect of sequor, a first look. The perfect of sequor uses the already-taught deponent perfect pattern: the perfect participle plus the present of sum.
- **[perfect of ēgredior](days/d142.html#term-perfect-of-egredior)** — Day 142, Latin · The perfect of ēgredior, a first look. The perfect of ēgredior uses the same deponent perfect pattern: the participle ēgressus plus the present of sum.
- **[place phrases (in, ad, ē)](days/d47.html#term-place-phrases-in-ad-e)** — Day 47, Latin · Review of Capitula I–X: place phrases with in, ad, and ē. Short preposition phrases you already have: in + the place-where form (in hortō, in the garden); ad + the toward form (ad hortum, to the garden); ē or ex + the place-from form (ē hortō, out of the garden).
- **[place where](days/d13.html#term-place-where)** — Day 13, Latin · Capitulum V, part 3 and review. The construction in + ablative that answers ubi? (where?): in hortō, in vīllā, in ātriō.
- **[pluperfect of a deponent](days/d121.html#term-pluperfect-of-a-deponent)** — Day 121, Latin · The pluperfect of a deponent, a first look. The pluperfect of a deponent uses the already-taught perfect participle with the imperfect of sum, and it still looks passive and is translated as active: locūtus eram, locūtus erās, locūtus erat, locūtī erāmus, locūtī erātis, locūtī erant.
- **[pluperfect of sequor](days/d131.html#term-pluperfect-of-sequor)** — Day 131, Latin · The pluperfect of sequor, a first look. The pluperfect of sequor uses the already-taught pattern of a deponent pluperfect: the perfect participle plus the imperfect of sum.
- **[pluperfect of ēgredior](days/d132.html#term-pluperfect-of-egredior)** — Day 132, Latin · The pluperfect of ēgredior, a first look. The pluperfect of ēgredior is formed in the same way: the perfect participle ēgressus plus the imperfect of sum.
- **[posse + infinitive](days/d37.html#term-posse-infinitive)** — Day 37, Latin · Capitulum X, part 2: the present infinitive, active. posse means ‘to be able / can.’ In this first look we use possum (I am able), potes (you are able), and potest (he/she is able), followed by a present active infinitive.
- **[possessive adjective](days/d05.html#term-possessive-adjective)** — Day 5, Latin · Capitulum II, part 2: cuius? and the genitive in use. An adjective such as meus, mea, meum (‘my’) that shows ownership and agrees with the noun it describes.
- **[possum, the full present](days/d82.html#term-possum-full-present)** — Day 82, Latin · Capitulum XV: sum and possum, the full present tables. Posse, to be able, is pot- placed in front of esse.
- **[preposition (with the ablative)](days/d11.html#term-preposition-abl)** — Day 11, Latin · Capitulum V, part 1: the ablative, and in, ex, ab. A small word placed before a noun to show a relation such as place or accompaniment.
- **[preposition (with the accusative)](days/d14.html#term-preposition-acc)** — Day 14, Latin · Capitulum VI, part 1: prepositions with the accusative. In Capitulum VI a new closed list of prepositions takes the accusative: ad (to, toward), ante (in front of, before), post (behind, after), inter (between, among), prope (near), circum (around), apud (at the house of, with), per (through).
- **[present deponent review, week 21](days/d104.html#term-present-deponent-review-week-21)** — Day 104, Latin · The present deponent table, a second mixed practice. A second short practice of loquor and cōnor in all six present persons, with last week’s absolutes only as a contrast if a question asks for one.
- **[present deponent table in use](days/d102.html#term-present-deponent-table-in-use)** — Day 102, Latin · The present deponent table, used in short sentences. The whole present table of a deponent can now be used in a short sentence: loquor, loqueris, loquitur, loquimur, loquiminī, loquuntur, and the same persons of cōnor.
- **[present first person](days/d81.html#term-present-first-person)** — Day 81, Latin · Capitulum XV: the present in the first and second persons. The first-person present endings are -ō (I) in the singular and -mus (we) in the plural.
- **[present infinitive (active)](days/d37.html#term-present-infinitive-active)** — Day 37, Latin · Capitulum X, part 2: the present infinitive, active. The ‘to …’ form of the verb, not limited to a person in the way a finite form is.
- **[present infinitive (passive)](days/d38.html#term-present-infinitive-passive)** — Day 38, Latin · Capitulum X, part 3: the present infinitive, passive. The ‘to be …-ed’ form that pairs with yesterday’s active infinitive.
- **[present infinitive, active](days/d43.html#term-present-infinitive-active-review)** — Day 43, Latin · Review of Capitula I–X: the present, and the active infinitive. The ‘to …’ form of a verb, in the present, active: vidēre, ‘to see.’ It can sit with a verb such as potest (‘is able’).
- **[present participle (Latin)](days/d77.html#term-present-participle-la)** — Day 77, Latin · Capitulum XIV: the present participle. The Latin present participle describes an action going on at the same time as the main verb.
- **[present second person](days/d81.html#term-present-second-person)** — Day 81, Latin · Capitulum XV: the present in the first and second persons. The second-person present endings are -s (you, singular) and -tis (you, plural).
- **[present subjunctive of sequor](days/d151.html#term-present-subjunctive-of-sequor)** — Day 151, Latin · The present subjunctive of sequor, a first look. The present subjunctive of sequor is sequar, sequāris, sequātur, sequāmur, sequāminī, sequantur.
- **[present subjunctive of ēgredior](days/d152.html#term-present-subjunctive-of-egredior)** — Day 152, Latin · The present subjunctive of ēgredior, a first look. The present subjunctive of ēgredior is ēgrediar, ēgrediāris, ēgrediātur, ēgrediāmur, ēgrediāminī, ēgrediantur.
- **[prior, posterior, and īnferior](days/d86.html#term-prior-posterior-inferior)** — Day 86, Latin · Capitulum XV: prior, posterior, and īnferior. These three comparatives order or locate, rather than only comparing a quality such as longior.
- **[pronoun](days/d07.html#term-pronoun)** — Day 7, Latin · Capitulum III, part 2: verbs and pronouns. A short word that stands in for a noun (I, you, he, she, it, we, they), often already marked by the verb ending in Latin.
- **[prīdiē](days/d73.html#term-pridie)** — Day 73, Latin · Capitulum XIII: the named day, and prīdiē. “On the day before,” used with the accusative of Kalendae, Nonae, or Īdūs (and the agreeing month): prīdiē Īdūs Mārtiās is 14 March.
- **[question words (quis, quid, ubi)](days/d48.html#term-question-words-quis-quid-ubi)** — Day 48, Latin · Review of Capitula I–X: quis, quid, and ubi. Three question words already used in Capitula I–X: quis (who), quid (what), and ubi (where).
- **[quis and quid](days/d04.html#term-quis-and-quid)** — Day 4, Latin · Capitulum II, part 1: gender and the genitive. Question words: quis, ‘who?’, for a person; quid, ‘what?’, for a thing.
- **[quot](days/d34.html#term-quot)** — Day 34, Latin · Capitulum IX, part 4: quot? and a large or small number. An interrogative word meaning “how many?” It does not decline in the use taught today.
- **[quō](days/d15.html#term-quo)** — Day 15, Latin · Place where and motion: a Friday check. The Latin question-word meaning ‘where to?’, asking for a motion-toward answer.
- **[reflexive sē](days/d23.html#term-reflexive-se)** — Day 23, Latin · Capitulum VII, part 3: reflexive sē, versus eum and eam. The pronoun sē (accusative; also used as ablative) refers back to the subject of its own clause: the subject acts on himself, herself, or themselves.
- **[relative pronoun (quī, quae, quod)](days/d27.html#term-relative-pronoun)** — Day 27, Latin · Capitulum VIII, part 2: the relative quī, quae, quod. A pronoun that introduces a relative clause and refers back to a noun (its antecedent).
- **[review (of a chapter)](days/d03.html#term-chapter-review)** — Day 3, Latin · Capitulum I, part 3 and review. A check that you can still read a sentence, hear est and sunt, and make an adjective agree, before the next chapter adds case.
- **[rooms of the villa](days/d12.html#term-villa-rooms)** — Day 12, Latin · Capitulum V, part 2: cum and sine; rooms of the villa. The main named parts of the house and garden in this chapter: ātrium, cubiculum, tablīnum, peristylium, hortus, ōstium.
- **[rēs](days/d79.html#term-res)** — Day 79, Latin · Capitulum XIV: rēs, nihil, and inquit. Rēs, reī is a fifth-declension feminine noun meaning a thing, a matter, or an affair.
- **[sequitur and ēgreditur](days/d89.html#term-sequitur-and-egreditur)** — Day 89, Latin · Capitulum XVI: fiō, and more deponents in the third person. Sequitur means he or she follows; ēgreditur means he or she goes out.
- **[sī and nisi](days/d84.html#term-si-and-nisi)** — Day 84, Latin · Capitulum XV: sī, nisi, at, and antequam. Sī means if. Nisi means if not, unless, or except. Nisi is ne + sī in origin as a compound; in this course it is learned as a conjunction that sets the negative case of an if-clause.
- **[tantus, -a, -um](days/d29.html#term-tantus)** — Day 29, Latin · Capitulum VIII, part 4: the ablative of price, and tantus. An adjective meaning ‘so great, of such a size (or price).’ It often pairs with quantus (‘how great, as great as’).
- **[the ablative of the named day](days/d73.html#term-ablative-named-day)** — Day 73, Latin · Capitulum XIII: the named day, and prīdiē. The Kalends, Nones, or Ides themselves are expressed in the ablative, with the month adjective agreeing: Kalendīs Mārtiīs, Nonīs Aprīlibus, Īdibus Octōbribus.
- **[the accusative as object](days/d51.html#term-accusative-as-object)** — Day 51, Latin · Review of Capitula I–X: et, sed, and an accusative object. In these I–X sentences, the thing had or seen takes the accusative (rosam, not rosa) when it is the object of the verb.
- **[the Latin month names](days/d71.html#term-latin-month-names)** — Day 71, Latin · Capitulum XIII: the year and the months. The twelve month names in Latin: Iānuārius, Februārius, Mārtius, Aprīlis, Maius, Iūnius, Iūlius, Augustus, September, October, November, December.
- **[third declension](days/d31.html#term-third-declension)** — Day 31, Latin · Capitulum IX, part 1: a third pattern of endings. A large family of Latin nouns whose nominative singular does not follow the first-declension -a or second-declension -us / -um pattern, and whose genitive singular typically ends in -is (rēx, rēgis; vōx, vōcis).
- **[third-conjugation -iō verbs](days/d28.html#term-third-io-verbs)** — Day 28, Latin · Capitulum VIII, part 3: capiō, accipiō, faciō in the present. A small group of third-conjugation verbs whose first principal part ends in -iō (today: capiō, accipiō, faciō).
- **[third-declension accusative](days/d32.html#term-third-declension-accusative)** — Day 32, Latin · Capitulum IX, part 2: the accusative of the new family. For many third-declension nouns, the accusative singular adds -em to the stem (the stem is seen in the genitive: rēg- from rēgis, so rēgem; vōc- from vōcis, so vōcem).
- **[third-declension adjective](days/d66.html#term-third-declension-adjective)** — Day 66, Latin · Capitulum XII, part 1: third-declension adjectives (fortis, -e). An adjective that uses third-declension endings and agrees with its noun in case, number, and gender.
- **[third-declension neuter](days/d61.html#term-third-declension-neuter)** — Day 61, Latin · Capitulum XI, part 1: third-declension neuters (corpus, caput). A neuter noun of the third family (genitive singular in -is).
- **[third-declension neuter (more nouns)](days/d62.html#term-third-declension-neuter-more)** — Day 62, Latin · Capitulum XI, part 2: pectus, ōs, crūs, and cor. The same neuter rule applied to more body nouns of the third family: pectus, pectoris (chest); ōs, ōris (mouth or face, with a long ō); crūs, crūris (leg).
- **[third-declension types (homō, leō, pēs)](days/d36.html#term-third-decl-types)** — Day 36, Latin · Capitulum X, part 1: more nouns of the third family. More nouns of the third family, still with genitive singular in -is and accusative singular in -em.
- **[third-family noun](days/d42.html#term-third-family-noun)** — Day 42, Latin · Review of Capitula I–X: third-family nouns. A noun whose nominative may look unlike hortus or puella (for example rēx, nōmen), and whose other endings follow the third pattern already used in Capitula IX–X.
- **[three place questions](days/d20.html#term-three-place-questions)** — Day 20, Latin · Capitula V–VI: two-week check. The trio ubi? (where?), quō? (where to?), unde? (where from?), each with its own construction for common nouns and for town names.
- **[town names (motion)](days/d16.html#term-town-motion)** — Day 16, Latin · Capitulum VI, part 2: town names, quō and unde. With the names of towns, cities, and small islands, Latin usually drops the preposition: the accusative alone means ‘to’ (Rōmam it), and the ablative alone means ‘from’ (Rōmā venit).
- **[two times of the ablative absolute](days/d97.html#term-two-times-of-the-ablative-absolute)** — Day 97, Latin · The two times of the ablative absolute, contrasted. A present-participle absolute, already taught last week, is going on at the same time as the main verb.
- **[two-week Latin check](days/d30.html#term-two-week-latin-check)** — Day 30, Latin · Capitula VII–VIII: two-week check. A cumulative check of Capitula VII–VIII: the dative; reflexive sē versus eum / eam; in + accusative versus in + ablative; hic and ille; the relative quī, quae, quod; capiō / accipiō / faciō in the present; and the ablative of price.
- **[ubi](days/d13.html#term-ubi)** — Day 13, Latin · Capitulum V, part 3 and review. The Latin question-word meaning ‘where?’, used to ask for a place-where answer in the ablative after in.
- **[unde](days/d16.html#term-unde)** — Day 16, Latin · Capitulum VI, part 2: town names, quō and unde. The Latin question-word meaning ‘where from?’, asking for a motion-from answer: ab or ex + ablative for common nouns, and the bare ablative for town names.
- **[uterque](days/d76.html#term-uterque)** — Day 76, Latin · Capitulum XIV: uter, neuter, alter, and uterque. Uterque (utraque, utrumque) means each of the two, or both taken as a pair.
- **[verb ending](days/d07.html#term-verb-ending)** — Day 7, Latin · Capitulum III, part 2: verbs and pronouns. The last part of a Latin verb, which shows who is acting (I, you, he or she, we, you plural, they) and, later, tense.
- **[via](days/d18.html#term-via)** — Day 18, Latin · Capitulum VI, part 4: on the road. The Latin word for a road or way (also, by extension, a journey).
- **[vocative](days/d09.html#term-vocative)** — Day 9, Latin · Capitulum IV, part 1: calling and commanding. The case used for calling or addressing someone: the form you use when you speak to a person, not about them.
- **[week’s Latin close](days/d50.html#term-weeks-latin-close-w10)** — Day 50, Latin · Capitula I–X: Friday check. A short Friday check of this week’s I–X review only: adjective agreement, the place phrases in / ad / ē, the question words quis, quid, and ubi, and the short original reading.
- **[week’s Latin close](days/d55.html#term-weeks-latin-close-w11)** — Day 55, Latin · Capitula I–X: Friday check. A Friday check of this week’s I–X review: et and sed, the accusative object, cūr and quia, and is, ea, eum, eam, plus the short original reading.
- **[Īdūs](days/d72.html#term-idus)** — Day 72, Latin · Capitulum XIII: Kalendae, Nonae, and Īdūs. A named day in each Roman month: the fifteenth of March, May, July, and October, and the thirteenth of the other months (Īdūs, Īduum, feminine plural, fourth declension).
- **[īre](days/d18.html#term-ire)** — Day 18, Latin · Capitulum VI, part 4: on the road. The Latin verb meaning ‘to go’.
## Spanish

- **[-ar verb](days/d05.html#term-ar-verb)** — Day 5, Modern language · Spanish: present tense of -ar verbs. A Spanish verb whose infinitive ends in -ar.
- **[-er and -ir verbs](days/d08.html#term-er-ir-verbs)** — Day 8, Modern language · Spanish: -er and -ir verbs. The two other regular infinitive classes.
