Camestres is a second-figure syllogism of the form AEE: a universal affirmative premise, a universal negative premise, and a universal negative conclusion, with the middle term as the predicate of both premises.
- **[carpus](days/d75.html#term-carpus)** — Day 75, Elective · Anatomy: the hand — carpus, metacarpals, and phalanges. The wrist, treated in this lesson as a group of short bones between the forearm and the metacarpals, not as a list of eight names to be memorized today.
- **[categorical sentences (A, E, I, O)](days/d26.html#term-categorical-aeio)** — Day 26, Logic · All, some, and none: a first look at A, E, I, and O. Four standard forms of sentence about classes: A, “All S are P”; E, “No S are P”; I, “Some S are P”; O, “Some S are not P.” The letters are traditional names.
- **[Celarent](days/d61.html#term-celarent)** — Day 61, Logic · Celarent, and the major and minor terms. The traditional name for a valid syllogism in the first figure whose vowels are E, A, and E: No M are P; All S are M; therefore No S are P.
- **[Cesare](days/d76.html#term-cesare)** — Day 76, Elective · Logic: Cesare, a second-figure EAE syllogism. Cesare is a second-figure syllogism of the form EAE: a universal negative premise, a universal affirmative premise, and a universal negative conclusion, with the middle term as the predicate of both premises.
- **[churches of the Mother of God (Dewing I.3)](days/d123.html#term-churches-of-the-mother-of-god-dewing-i-3)** — Day 123, Seminar · Art history: Dewing, Buildings I.3.1–5, Blachernae, a first look. Dewing, Buildings I.3, opens the churches of Mary the Mother of God, because, in his wording, “true reason manifestly demands that from God one must proceed to the Mother of God,” and because this is “the wish of the Emperor himself.” The first sanctuary described in Byzantium in this chapter is at Blachernae, outside the fortifications, on the sea, supported by “sections of Parian stone which stand there to serve as columns.”
- **[clavicle](days/d05.html#term-clavicle)** — Day 5, Elective · Artistic anatomy 1: the skeleton under the skin. The collarbone: a subcutaneous landmark running from the sternum to the point of the shoulder (near the acromion).
- **[clef](days/d02.html#term-clef)** — Day 2, Music theory · Pitch, staff and clefs. The sign at the start of a staff that fixes which line is a named pitch: the treble (G) clef or the bass (F) clef in this lesson.
- **[columns in a choral dance (Dewing I.1.35)](days/d78.html#term-columns-choral-dance)** — Day 78, Elective · Art history: columns in a choral dance, from Dewing I.1.35. In Dewing’s translation of Buildings I.1.35, the columns on either side of the east structure “do not stand in a straight line, but they retreat inward in the pattern of the semicircle, as if they were yielding to one another in a choral dance,” and above them hangs a crescent-shaped structure.
- **[complementary events](days/d126.html#term-complementary-events)** — Day 126, Seminar · Logic: complementary events, P(not A) = 1 − P(A), a first look. Two events are complementary when one is exactly the cases in which the other does not happen, and one of the two must happen.
- **[composite view](days/d08.html#term-composite-view)** — Day 8, Art history · Egypt: the art of permanence. The Egyptian convention of showing each part of a figure in its most characteristic aspect: head in profile, eye as if from the front, shoulders from the front, legs and feet in profile.
- **[compound interest A = P(1 + r)^n](days/d09.html#term-compound-interest)** — Day 9, Economics & personal finance · Compound interest. Interest paid on the principal and on the interest already earned.
- **[conditional p → q](days/d01.html#term-conditional)** — Day 1, Logic · Propositions and truth tables. The ‘if p then q’ connective.
- **[conditional probability](days/d146.html#term-conditional-probability)** — Day 146, Seminar · Logic: conditional probability, P(A|B) = P(A and B) / P(B), a first look. The conditional probability of A given B, written P(A|B), is P(A and B) / P(B), provided P(B) is not 0.
- **[contrapositive ¬q → ¬p](days/d01.html#term-contrapositive)** — Day 1, Logic · Propositions and truth tables. The statement formed by swapping and negating both sides of a conditional.
- **[contrapposto](days/d18.html#term-contrapposto)** — Day 18, Art history · The kouros and contrapposto. A standing pose in which the weight rests mainly on one leg, the other is relaxed, and the hips and shoulders tilt in opposite directions, so the spine forms a slight S-curve.
- **[contrasting period](days/d87.html#term-contrasting-period)** — Day 87, Elective · Music: contrasting and parallel periods. A contrasting period, in this lesson’s school sense, is a period whose consequent does not begin like the antecedent.
- **[converse](days/d21.html#term-converse)** — Day 21, Logic · Converse, inverse, and contrapositive. The statement obtained by swapping a conditional’s antecedent and consequent.
- **[credit (buying now, paying later)](days/d64.html#term-credit-finance)** — Day 64, Economics · Credit, and a bank that holds deposits and makes loans. An arrangement in which a person or a shop allows a purchase now, with payment later, in one later sum or in parts.
- **[curves of the vertebral column, from Gray](days/d110.html#term-curves-of-the-vertebral-column-from-gray)** — Day 110, Seminar · Artistic anatomy: the curves of the vertebral column, from Gray 1918. Gray 1918, in the public-domain text used here from Theodora, “The Vertebral Column as a Whole,” describes four curves viewed laterally, called cervical, thoracic, lumbar, and pelvic.
- **[Darapti](days/d96.html#term-darapti)** — Day 96, Elective · Logic: the third figure, and Darapti. Darapti is a third-figure syllogism of the form AAI: a universal affirmative major, a universal affirmative minor, and a particular affirmative conclusion.
- **[Darii](days/d66.html#term-darii)** — Day 66, Logic · Darii, a first-figure AII syllogism. A valid first-figure syllogism of the form AII: all M are P; some S are M; therefore some S are P.
- **[Datisi](days/d111.html#term-datisi)** — Day 111, Seminar · Logic: Datisi, a first look at a third-figure AII. Datisi is a valid third-figure mood, AII-3: All M are P; some M are S; therefore some S are P.
- **[De Morgan’s laws ¬(P ∧ Q) ⇔ ¬P ∨ ¬Q; ¬(P ∨ Q) ⇔ ¬P ∧ ¬Q](days/d11.html#term-de-morgan)** — Day 11, Logic · De Morgan’s laws. Two equivalences that move a negation through ‘and’ and ‘or’, and switch the connective: not (P and Q) is equivalent to (not P) or (not Q); not (P or Q) is equivalent to (not P) and (not Q).
- **[deceptive cadence](days/d77.html#term-deceptive-cadence)** — Day 77, Elective · Music: the deceptive cadence. A deceptive cadence is a common-practice close that goes from V to vi (or V to VI in a minor key), so the ear, prepared for I, lands on the relative tonic instead.
- **[demand and supply](days/d19.html#term-demand-supply)** — Day 19, Economics & personal finance · Supply and demand. Demand is the relationship between the price of a good and the quantity buyers are willing and able to buy, other things equal.
- **[denying the antecedent](days/d41.html#term-denying-the-antecedent)** — Day 41, Logic · Affirming the consequent, and denying the antecedent. An invalid move: from “If P, then Q” and “not P,” to conclude “not Q.” The antecedent is denied, but Q may still hold for another reason.
- **[disjunctive syllogism](days/d51.html#term-disjunctive-syllogism)** — Day 51, Logic · Disjunctive syllogism, and an inclusive “or”. A valid form: P or Q; not P; therefore Q.
- **[Doryphoros (Polykleitos)](days/d23.html#term-doryphoros)** — Day 23, Art history · High Classical Greece: the Parthenon and the Doryphoros. The “Spear-Bearer,” a bronze athlete statue designed by Polykleitos in the fifth century BCE, known mainly from later marble copies.
- **[equal installment repayment](days/d89.html#term-equal-installment-repayment)** — Day 89, Elective · Economics: equal installments on a known total (invented figures). An equal installment repayment, in this lesson, takes a known total — a principal plus an already-computed simple-interest charge — and divides it into equal payments.
- **[equally likely outcomes](days/d121.html#term-equally-likely-outcomes)** — Day 121, Seminar · Logic: equally likely outcomes, a first look. Outcomes are equally likely when each of the distinct possible results of a chance process has the same chance.
- **[equilibrium, shortage, and surplus](days/d19.html#term-equilibrium-price)** — Day 19, Economics & personal finance · Supply and demand. The equilibrium price is the price at which the quantity demanded equals the quantity supplied.
- **[expected value on an invented fair cube](days/d156.html#term-expected-value-on-an-invented-fair-cube)** — Day 156, Seminar · Logic: expected value on an invented fair cube, a first look. The expected value of an invented fair six-faced cube, in this first look, is the average of the face values, each equally likely: E = (1 + 2 + 3 + 4 + 5 + 6) / 6 = 21/6 = 7/2.
- **[fallacy](days/d06.html#term-fallacy)** — Day 6, Logic · Validity, soundness and two classic fallacies. A tempting but invalid form of argument.
- **[Felapton](days/d101.html#term-felapton)** — Day 101, Seminar · Logic: Felapton, a first look at a third-figure particular negative. Felapton is a valid third-figure mood, EAO-3: No M are P; all M are S; therefore some S are not P.
- **[femur](days/d65.html#term-femur)** — Day 65, Elective · Anatomy: the leg — femur, tibia, and fibula. The thigh bone: the long bone of the upper leg, between the hip and the knee.
- **[Ferio](days/d71.html#term-ferio)** — Day 71, Elective · Logic: Ferio, a first-figure EIO syllogism. A valid first-figure syllogism of the form EIO: no M are P; some S are M; therefore some S are not P.
- **[Ferison](days/d106.html#term-ferison)** — Day 106, Seminar · Logic: Ferison, a first look at a third-figure EIO. Ferison is a valid third-figure mood, EIO-3: No M are P; some M are S; therefore some S are not P.
- **[Festino](days/d86.html#term-festino)** — Day 86, Elective · Logic: Festino, a second-figure EIO syllogism. Festino is a second-figure syllogism of the form EIO: a universal negative premise, a particular affirmative premise, and a particular negative conclusion, with the middle term as the predicate of both premises.
- **[fibula](days/d65.html#term-fibula)** — Day 65, Elective · Anatomy: the leg — femur, tibia, and fibula. The thinner, outer (lateral) of the two long bones of the lower leg, beside the tibia, between the knee and the ankle.
- **[flakes, delayed replacement, and a testimonial, reread](days/d103.html#term-flakes-delayed-replacement-and-a-testimonial-reread)** — Day 103, Seminar · Art history: Dewing’s flakes, delayed replacement, and a testimonial. Dewing I.1.74–78, already read in this week’s history lessons, is reread today as a question of repair.
- **[foot (verse)](days/d10.html#term-verse-foot)** — Day 10, Elective · Poetry: meter and the iambic pentameter. The repeating unit of meter: iamb (da-DUM), trochee (DUM-da), anapest (da-da-DUM), dactyl (DUM-da-da), spondee (DUM-DUM).
- **[foramen transversarium](days/d125.html#term-foramen-transversarium)** — Day 125, Seminar · Artistic anatomy: a cervical vertebra, and the foramen transversarium, from Gray 1918. In Henry Gray, Anatomy of the Human Body, 20th ed., 1918, “The Cervical Vertebrae,” as posted at theodora.com, the cervical vertebrae “are the smallest of the true vertebrae, and can be readily distinguished from those of the thoracic or lumbar regions by the presence of a foramen in each transverse process.” That opening is the foramen transversarium.
- **[formal analysis](days/d03.html#term-formal-analysis)** — Day 3, Art history · How to look; the first images. A description of what is visibly there in a work — line, shape, mass, value, color, texture, space, composition — before any interpretation.
- **[half cadence](days/d52.html#term-half-cadence)** — Day 52, Music · A half cadence, as a pause, not an end. A common-practice pause that ends on V, the dominant, rather than closing to I.
- **[hierarchical scale](days/d08.html#term-hierarchical-scale)** — Day 8, Art history · Egypt: the art of permanence. The convention of making the most important figure (a pharaoh or a god) larger than the others, not because they stand closer, but because of rank.
- **[humerus](days/d70.html#term-humerus)** — Day 70, Elective · Anatomy: the arm — humerus, radius, and ulna. The bone of the upper arm, between the shoulder and the elbow.
- **[humerus, radius, and ulna](days/d35.html#term-arm-bones)** — Day 35, Elective · Artistic anatomy: the arm (humerus, radius, and ulna). The three long bones of the arm, as named in artistic anatomy: the humerus in the upper arm; the radius and the ulna in the forearm.
- **[I, IV, and V (major key)](days/d27.html#term-i-iv-v-major)** — Day 27, Music theory · I, IV, and V in a major key, and a first authentic cadence. The primary triads of a major key: the tonic (I, built on degree 1), the subdominant (IV, on degree 4), and the dominant (V, on degree 5).
- **[iambic pentameter](days/d10.html#term-iambic-pentameter)** — Day 10, Elective · Poetry: meter and the iambic pentameter. A line of five iambs (five da-DUM feet), the most common meter of English verse from Chaucer onward, and the line of Shakespeare’s sonnets and blank verse.
- **[imagery](days/d30.html#term-imagery)** — Day 30, Elective · Poetry 3: imagery and metaphor, with a public-domain poem. Language that appeals to the senses — seeing, hearing, touch, and the rest — so a reader can picture or otherwise sense a scene or an object.
- **[incentive](days/d44.html#term-incentive)** — Day 44, Economics & personal finance · An incentive (an invented shop example). A reason that makes a choice more or less attractive, often a gain or a loss that follows the choice.
- **[inclusive or, in a first look](days/d51.html#term-inclusive-or-in-a-first-look)** — Day 51, Logic · Disjunctive syllogism, and an inclusive “or”. An “or” that is true when at least one side is true, including the case where both are true.
- **[income tax (invented figures)](days/d129.html#term-income-tax-invented-figures)** — Day 129, Seminar · Economics: an income tax on invented figures, distinguished from a sales tax. An income tax, in this first look, is a named percent taken from an invented amount of income, not added to a purchase price.
- **[independent events](days/d136.html#term-independent-events)** — Day 136, Seminar · Logic: independent events, P(A and B) = P(A) × P(B), a first look. Two events are independent when the happening of one does not change the probability of the other.
- **[independent events (invented figures)](days/d151.html#term-independent-events-invented-figures)** — Day 151, Seminar · Logic: independent events on invented figures, a first look. Two events are independent, in this first look, when the occurrence of one does not change the probability of the other.
- **[insurance (invented figures)](days/d134.html#term-insurance-invented-figures)** — Day 134, Seminar · Economics: insurance on invented figures, distinguished from a tax. Insurance, in this first look, is an invented arrangement in which a named premium is paid in advance, and a named payment would be made if a named invented event happens.
- **[interpretation](days/d03.html#term-interpretation)** — Day 3, Art history · How to look; the first images. A claim about meaning or purpose, clearly marked as such, and paired with the evidence that would test it.
- **[interval](days/d12.html#term-interval)** — Day 12, Music theory · Intervals. The distance between two pitches, named by counting letter-names inclusively, then qualified as perfect, major, or minor (and, later, augmented or diminished).
- **[interval inversion](days/d37.html#term-interval-inversion)** — Day 37, Music theory · Interval inversion, with the octave as the frame. The partner of an interval when one of the two notes is moved by an octave, so that the old top note is now below, or the old bottom note is now above.
- **[inverse](days/d21.html#term-inverse-conditional)** — Day 21, Logic · Converse, inverse, and contrapositive. The statement obtained by negating both sides of a conditional, without swapping them.
- **[inversion of a short figure](days/d97.html#term-inversion-of-a-short-figure)** — Day 97, Elective · Music: inversion of a short invented figure. An inversion, in this lesson’s school sense, takes a short figure and turns its intervals upside down: a step up becomes a step down of the same size, a skip down becomes a skip up, and so on, starting from a chosen note.
- **[investing on invented figures, a first look](days/d154.html#term-investing-on-invented-figures-a-first-look)** — Day 154, Seminar · Economics: investing on invented figures, a first look. Investing, in this invented classroom arrangement, is using some of a leftover already set aside in order to buy an invented item that is later sold, so that a gain or a loss on that invented sale can be named.
- **[inward eye](days/d55.html#term-inward-eye)** — Day 55, Elective · Poetry 6: Wordsworth, “I Wandered Lonely as a Cloud” (first published 1807). Wordsworth’s figure, in the last stanza of “I Wandered Lonely as a Cloud,” for the later memory of the scene: the daffodils “flash upon that inward eye / Which is the bliss of solitude.” It is a figure in a poem, not a claim in a lesson on the anatomy of the eye.
- **[Justinian’s buildings about the bay, Dewing I.6.1–4](days/d168.html#term-justinians-buildings-about-the-bay-dewing-i-6-1-4)** — Day 168, Seminar · Art history: Justinian’s buildings about the bay, Dewing I.6.1–4. Dewing’s 1940 translation of Procopius, Buildings I.6.1–4, as posted on LacusCurtius, public domain in the United States.
- **[Keats, On First Looking into Chapman's Homer](days/d50.html#term-keats-on-first-looking-into-chapmans-homer)** — Day 50, Elective · Poetry 5: Keats, “On First Looking into Chapman's Homer” (1817). A sonnet by John Keats, published in *Poems* (1817), in the public domain, about first encountering George Chapman's English Homer.
- **[key signature](days/d07.html#term-key-signature)** — Day 7, Music theory · The major scale and key signatures. The sharps or flats placed at the start of the staff to keep a scale’s pattern, added in a fixed order (sharps: F C G D A E B; flats: B E A D G C F).
- **[kouros](days/d18.html#term-kouros)** — Day 18, Art history · The kouros and contrapposto. An Archaic Greek standing youth, typically in marble, usually nude, with the left foot forward, the weight still even on both legs, the arms at the sides, and often an ‘Archaic smile’.
- **[Laocoön (date caution)](days/d28.html#term-laocoon-caution)** — Day 28, Art history · Hellenistic sculpture: the Nike of Samothrace, and the Laocoön with a date caution. The Vatican Laocoön group, a marble of Laocoön and his sons attacked by serpents, famous since its Renaissance rediscovery.
- **[lead joints and prevailing light (Dewing)](days/d88.html#term-lead-joints-and-prevailing-light-dewing)** — Day 88, Elective · Art history: Dewing’s lead joints, and gold against the light of the stones. This elective gathers two claims already read in history this week.
- **[light and marble in the church (Procopius I.1.29–30)](days/d73.html#term-light-and-marble-in-the-church)** — Day 73, Elective · Art history: light and marble in the church, from Dewing I.1.29–30. Procopius’s description, in Dewing I.1.29–30, of the church as abounding “in sunlight and in the reflection of the sun’s rays from the marble,” so that the radiance might seem to come into being within it.
- **[logical equivalence](days/d11.html#term-logical-equivalence)** — Day 11, Logic · De Morgan’s laws. Two statements that have the same truth value in every possible case.
- **[major and minor triad](days/d17.html#term-major-minor-triad)** — Day 17, Music theory · The triad. A major triad has a major third and a perfect fifth above the root (the upper interval, from the third to the fifth, is then a minor third).
- **[major scale](days/d07.html#term-major-scale)** — Day 7, Music theory · The major scale and key signatures. A scale whose steps follow the pattern whole, whole, half, whole, whole, whole, half (W W H W W W H).
- **[major term and minor term](days/d61.html#term-major-and-minor-term)** — Day 61, Logic · Celarent, and the major and minor terms. In a syllogism, the major term (P) is the predicate of the conclusion, and the minor term (S) is the subject of the conclusion.
- **[market](days/d14.html#term-market)** — Day 14, Economics & personal finance · Markets and prices. A meeting of buyers and sellers of a particular good or service, in which they can trade.
- **[meadow, colors, and the sight new each time (Dewing)](days/d93.html#term-meadow-colours-and-the-sight-new-each-time-dewing)** — Day 93, Elective · Art history: Dewing’s meadow of stones, and the sight new each time. This elective gathers two claims already read in history this week.
- **[medium of exchange](days/d29.html#term-medium-of-exchange)** — Day 29, Economics & personal finance · Money: medium of exchange, unit of account, store of value. The job of money as a thing commonly accepted in payment, so that trade does not have to be a direct swap of two particular goods.
- **[metacarpals](days/d75.html#term-metacarpals)** — Day 75, Elective · Anatomy: the hand — carpus, metacarpals, and phalanges. The long bones of the palm, lying between the carpus and the phalanges; one metacarpal leads toward each digit.
- **[metaphor](days/d30.html#term-metaphor)** — Day 30, Elective · Poetry 3: imagery and metaphor, with a public-domain poem. A comparison made by speaking of one thing as if it were another, without a comparing word such as *like* or *as* (those usually mark a simile).
- **[metatarsal](days/d80.html#term-metatarsal)** — Day 80, Elective · Anatomy: the foot — tarsus, metatarsals, and phalanges. A metatarsal is one of the long bones of the midfoot, between the tarsus and the phalanges of the toes, the counterpart of a metacarpal in the hand.
- **[meter (verse)](days/d10.html#term-verse-meter)** — Day 10, Elective · Poetry: meter and the iambic pentameter. The pattern of stressed and unstressed syllables in a line of verse.
- **[minor triad](days/d42.html#term-minor-triad)** — Day 42, Music theory · The minor triad, beside the major triad already taught. A three-note chord built from a root, a minor third above the root, and a perfect fifth above the root (or a major third above the third).
- **[minuet and trio](days/d142.html#term-minuet-and-trio)** — Day 142, Seminar · Music: minuet and trio, a first look, on an invented figure. Minuet and trio is a form in which a minuet is stated, a contrasting trio follows, and the minuet then returns, often marked da capo, giving an A–B–A shape.
- **[modus ponens](days/d46.html#term-modus-ponens)** — Day 46, Logic · Modus ponens and modus tollens. A valid form: If P, then Q; P; therefore Q.
- **[modus tollens](days/d46.html#term-modus-tollens)** — Day 46, Logic · Modus ponens and modus tollens. A valid form: If P, then Q; not Q; therefore not P.
- **[money (three jobs)](days/d29.html#term-money-three-jobs)** — Day 29, Economics & personal finance · Money: medium of exchange, unit of account, store of value. In this course’s working definition, money is whatever, in a given setting, does three jobs: a medium of exchange (you can pay with it), a unit of account (prices and debts can be written in it), and a store of value (it can be kept and later spent, with the usual cautions about inflation and risk, which are named, not computed today).
- **[musical period](days/d82.html#term-musical-period)** — Day 82, Elective · Music: a period, antecedent and consequent. A musical period, in the common-practice school sense taught here, is a pair of phrases that belong together: an antecedent that usually ends with a weaker close, often a half cadence, and a consequent that usually answers with a stronger close, often an authentic cadence, on the tonic.
- **[musical sequence](days/d92.html#term-musical-sequence)** — Day 92, Elective · Music: a sequence, a first look. A musical sequence, in this lesson’s school sense, is a short figure or phrase that is then repeated at a different pitch, usually starting a step or more higher or lower, so that the shape is heard again, moved.
- **[mutually exclusive events](days/d131.html#term-mutually-exclusive-events)** — Day 131, Seminar · Logic: mutually exclusive events, P(A or B) = P(A) + P(B), a first look. Two events are mutually exclusive when they cannot both happen in the same outcome.
- **[natural minor scale](days/d22.html#term-natural-minor-scale)** — Day 22, Music theory · The natural minor scale, and relative minor. A seven-note scale whose stepwise pattern of whole and half steps is whole–half–whole–whole–half–whole–whole (W H W W H W W).
- **[necessary condition](days/d16.html#term-necessary-condition)** — Day 16, Logic · Necessary and sufficient conditions. A condition that must hold if the result holds: if P is necessary for Q, then Q → P (equivalently, ¬P → ¬Q).
- **[Nike of Samothrace](days/d28.html#term-nike-of-samothrace)** — Day 28, Art history · Hellenistic sculpture: the Nike of Samothrace, and the Laocoön with a date caution. The marble “Winged Victory” now in the Louvre, a Hellenistic statue of the goddess Nike, usually dated to the second century BCE (often around the early second century; exact year is not settled, and this course will not invent one).
- **[octave as a frame](days/d37.html#term-octave-as-frame)** — Day 37, Music theory · Interval inversion, with the octave as the frame. The octave (from Latin octavus, “eighth”) is the interval of eight letter names, inclusive, for example C to the next C.
- **[opportunity cost](days/d04.html#term-opportunity-cost)** — Day 4, Economics & personal finance · Scarcity and opportunity cost. The value of the single best alternative given up when a choice is made, not the sum of every alternative.
- **[origin and insertion](days/d15.html#term-origin-insertion)** — Day 15, Elective · Artistic anatomy 2: muscles and tendons. The two attachments of a muscle.
- **[Ozymandias (Shelley, 1818)](days/d40.html#term-ozymandias-shelley)** — Day 40, Elective · Poetry 4: Shelley, “Ozymandias” (1818), a short public-domain model. A sonnet by Percy Bysshe Shelley, first published in 1818, in the public domain.
- **[Pantheon (Hadrianic building, Agrippan inscription)](days/d38.html#term-pantheon-dating-caution)** — Day 38, Art history · The Pantheon in Rome, with a dating caution. The Pantheon in Rome is a circular temple with a concrete dome and a central oculus, as we have it, generally treated as a rebuild under Hadrian in the early second century CE (a conventional range often given around 118–125 CE).
- **[parallel period](days/d87.html#term-parallel-period)** — Day 87, Elective · Music: contrasting and parallel periods. A parallel period, in this lesson’s school sense, is a period whose consequent begins like the antecedent, usually with the same opening figure, before it continues to a stronger cadence.
- **[Parthenon](days/d23.html#term-parthenon)** — Day 23, Art history · High Classical Greece: the Parthenon and the Doryphoros. The temple of Athena on the Acropolis in Athens, built in the mid-fifth century BCE (a conventional dating is about 447–432 BCE).
- **[pelvis (artistic)](days/d25.html#term-pelvis-artistic)** — Day 25, Elective · Artistic anatomy 3: the torso — rib cage and pelvis. The bony basin at the base of the trunk, a volume that carries the abdomen and meets the thigh at the hip, with a front plane (including the anterior superior iliac spines as useful landmarks) and a tilt of its own.
- **[pendentive](days/d83.html#term-pendentive)** — Day 83, Elective · Art history: the pendentive, as a later name for Dewing’s four triangles. A pendentive is a concave triangular section of vaulting that makes the transition from a square base to the circle of a dome and helps carry the dome’s weight.
- **[perfect fifth and major third (first look)](days/d32.html#term-p5-m3-first-look)** — Day 32, Music theory · Intervals: the perfect fifth and the major third. In a major scale, the interval from the first degree to the fifth is a perfect fifth (for example, C up to G).
- **[perfect, major, and minor](days/d12.html#term-perfect-and-major)** — Day 12, Music theory · Intervals. Qualities of intervals.
- **[phalanges of the hand](days/d75.html#term-phalanges-of-the-hand)** — Day 75, Elective · Anatomy: the hand — carpus, metacarpals, and phalanges. The bones of the digits: each finger has three phalanges (proximal, middle, and distal); the thumb has two (proximal and distal).
- **[pitch](days/d02.html#term-pitch)** — Day 2, Music theory · Pitch, staff and clefs. How high or low a note sounds: the perceived frequency of the sound.
- **[plagal cadence](days/d72.html#term-plagal-cadence)** — Day 72, Elective · Music: authentic and plagal cadences. A cadence that arrives from IV to I: the subdominant chord moves to the tonic chord.
- **[price (as a signal)](days/d14.html#term-price-signal)** — Day 14, Economics & personal finance · Markets and prices. The amount of money (or other payment) asked or agreed for a good or service, which also serves as a signal: a high price discourages some buying and encourages some selling and production; a low price does the reverse, other things equal.
- **[principal from the amount](days/d79.html#term-principal-from-the-amount)** — Day 79, Elective · Economics: finding the principal from the amount. If the amount after simple interest is A = P + I, and I = P × r × t, then A = P(1 + r t), so the principal is P = A / (1 + r t).
- **[principal P](days/d09.html#term-principal)** — Day 9, Economics & personal finance · Compound interest. The original amount of money borrowed or invested, before interest.
- **[profit, in an invented shop example](days/d49.html#term-profit-in-an-invented-shop-example)** — Day 49, Economics & personal finance · Profit as a leftover (an invented shop example). In this first look, profit is a leftover: the amount that remains when cost is subtracted from the money taken in (the price received, or the takings) in a labeled, invented example.
- **[proposition](days/d01.html#term-proposition)** — Day 1, Logic · Propositions and truth tables. A sentence that is either true or false (not a command, not a question).
- **[radius and ulna](days/d70.html#term-radius-and-ulna)** — Day 70, Elective · Anatomy: the arm — humerus, radius, and ulna. The two long bones of the forearm, between the elbow and the wrist.
- **[relative minor](days/d22.html#term-relative-minor)** — Day 22, Music theory · The natural minor scale, and relative minor. The minor scale that shares its key signature (its pitch set) with a given major scale, and starts a minor third below the major tonic, or on the sixth degree of the major scale.
- **[retrograde inversion of a short figure](days/d107.html#term-retrograde-inversion-of-a-short-figure)** — Day 107, Seminar · Music: retrograde inversion of a short invented figure, a first look. Retrograde inversion, in this first look, is a short invented figure first inverted — each rise becomes a fall of the same distance, still running forwards — and then played backwards, so that the last inverted interval is written first.
- **[retrograde of a short figure](days/d102.html#term-retrograde-of-a-short-figure)** — Day 102, Seminar · Music: retrograde of a short invented figure, a first look. Retrograde, in this first look, is a short invented figure played backwards: the last note becomes the first, and the first becomes the last, with the same stepwise distances now running in reverse time.
- **[rib cage (artistic)](days/d25.html#term-rib-cage-artistic)** — Day 25, Elective · Artistic anatomy 3: the torso — rib cage and pelvis. The bony thorax as an artistic form: a rounded cage, wider than it is deep in a typical academic model, attaching to the spine, with a lower opening (the costal margin) that is not a straight cut-off.
- **[rondo form](days/d132.html#term-rondo-form)** — Day 132, Seminar · Music: rondo form, A–B–A–C–A, a first look. Rondo form, in this first look, is a musical shape in which a first strain A returns after each contrasting strain: A–B–A–C–A.
- **[rule of 72](days/d09.html#term-rule-of-72)** — Day 9, Economics & personal finance · Compound interest. A short estimate: money at r percent a year doubles in about 72/r years, a good approximation for rates between about 2% and 15%.
- **[sales tax (invented figures)](days/d124.html#term-sales-tax-invented-figures)** — Day 124, Seminar · Economics: a sales tax on an invented price, a first look. A sales tax, in this lesson, is a named percent of an invented price, added to that price to give a total.
- **[saving (invented figures)](days/d149.html#term-saving-invented-figures)** — Day 149, Seminar · Economics: saving on invented figures, a first look. Saving, in this invented classroom arrangement, is setting aside some or all of a budget’s remainder, rather than spending it.
- **[saving a leftover](days/d39.html#term-saving-a-leftover)** — Day 39, Economics & personal finance · Saving a leftover (an invented example). In this first look, saving is a leftover from a labeled budget that is not spent in the same period, but is set aside.
- **[scarcity](days/d04.html#term-scarcity)** — Day 4, Economics & personal finance · Scarcity and opportunity cost. The fact that resources (time, money, materials, attention) are limited relative to what we want, so every choice gives something up.
- **[second figure](days/d76.html#term-second-figure)** — Day 76, Elective · Logic: Cesare, a second-figure EAE syllogism. In a second-figure syllogism, the middle term is the predicate of both premises.
- **[simple and compound compared](days/d84.html#term-simple-and-compound-compared)** — Day 84, Elective · Economics: simple and compound interest, compared (already taught). A comparison of simple and compound interest, in this lesson, uses the already-taught formulas on the same invented principal, rate, and time, and names the difference honestly.
- **[simple discount, percent off](days/d104.html#term-simple-discount-percent-off)** — Day 104, Seminar · Economics: a simple discount on invented figures, a first look. A simple discount, in this first look, takes a percent off an invented price.
- **[simple interest](days/d69.html#term-simple-interest)** — Day 69, Economics · Simple interest, in a labeled invented example. Interest computed only on the original principal, as I = P × r × t, where P is the principal, r is the interest rate per time unit as a decimal, and t is the time in those same units (here, years).
- **[simple interest on a declining principal](days/d94.html#term-simple-interest-on-a-declining-principal)** — Day 94, Elective · Economics: simple interest on a declining principal (invented figures). Simple interest on a declining principal, in this lesson, means that each year’s interest is computed with the already-taught simple-interest formula, but on the principal still outstanding after a labeled invented principal repayment.
- **[soundness](days/d06.html#term-soundness)** — Day 6, Logic · Validity, soundness and two classic fallacies. The property of an argument that is valid and whose premises are actually true.
- **[specialization](days/d24.html#term-specialization)** — Day 24, Economics & personal finance · Specialization and trade (a classroom table). The choice to concentrate work on a narrower set of tasks, and to rely on others for the rest, rather than making everything oneself.
- **[square of opposition (first look)](days/d26.html#term-square-of-opposition)** — Day 26, Logic · All, some, and none: a first look at A, E, I, and O. A traditional diagram of how A, E, I, and O relate: A and O are contradictories (one true, the other false); E and I are contradictories; A and E are contraries (they cannot both be true, but both can be false); I and O are subcontraries (they cannot both be false, but both can be true), on the standard reading taught here, which assumes there is at least one S (an existential assumption this course will name rather than hide).
- **[staff](days/d02.html#term-staff)** — Day 2, Music theory · Pitch, staff and clefs. The five lines and four spaces on which notes are written.
- **[subcutaneous landmark](days/d05.html#term-subcutaneous-landmark)** — Day 5, Elective · Artistic anatomy 1: the skeleton under the skin. A place where bone lies just under the skin and does not change shape with fat or muscle, so it is a reliable measuring point in figure drawing.
- **[successive discounts](days/d109.html#term-successive-discounts)** — Day 109, Seminar · Economics: successive discounts, a first look. A successive discount, in this first look, takes one percent off an invented price, then takes a second percent off the amount that remains, not off the original price again.
- **[sufficient condition](days/d16.html#term-sufficient-condition)** — Day 16, Logic · Necessary and sufficient conditions. A condition that is enough to guarantee the result: if P is sufficient for Q, then P → Q.
- **[sunk cost](days/d04.html#term-sunk-cost)** — Day 4, Economics & personal finance · Scarcity and opportunity cost. A cost already paid that cannot be recovered, and that should not affect a forward-looking decision.
- **[syllogism](days/d31.html#term-syllogism)** — Day 31, Logic · The syllogism: a first look at Barbara. A form of argument with two premises and a conclusion, in which a middle term links the other two terms.
- **[tarsus](days/d80.html#term-tarsus)** — Day 80, Elective · Anatomy: the foot — tarsus, metatarsals, and phalanges. The tarsus is the group of bones in the posterior foot, the counterpart of the carpus in the hand.
- **[tendon](days/d15.html#term-tendon)** — Day 15, Elective · Artistic anatomy 2: muscles and tendons. The tough, cord-like or flattened connective tissue that often joins a muscle to a bone.
- **[ternary form](days/d127.html#term-ternary-form)** — Day 127, Seminar · Music: ternary form, A–B–A, a first look. Ternary form is a three-part musical shape, A–B–A: a first strain, a contrasting middle strain, and a return of the first strain.
- **[the addition rule for overlapping events](days/d141.html#term-the-addition-rule-for-overlapping-events)** — Day 141, Seminar · Logic: the addition rule for overlapping events, a first look. When two events can both happen, P(A or B) = P(A) + P(B) − P(A and B).
- **[the atlas (first cervical vertebra)](days/d130.html#term-the-atlas-first-cervical-vertebra)** — Day 130, Seminar · Artistic anatomy: Gray 1918, the atlas, a first look. In Gray, 1918, as posted at Theodora, the first cervical vertebra is named the atlas “because it supports the globe of the head.” Its chief peculiarity is that it has no body, “due to the fact that the body of the atlas has fused with that of the next vertebra.” Its other peculiarities are that it has no spinous process, “is ring-like, and consists of an anterior and a posterior arch and two lateral masses.” Official lengths remain reserved.
- **[the axis (second cervical vertebra)](days/d135.html#term-the-axis-second-cervical-vertebra)** — Day 135, Seminar · Artistic anatomy: Gray 1918, the axis, a first look. In Gray, 1918, as posted at Theodora, the second cervical vertebra “is named the epistropheus or axis because it forms the pivot upon which the first vertebra, carrying the head, rotates.” “The most distinctive characteristic of this bone is the strong odontoid process which rises perpendicularly from the upper surface of the body.” Official lengths remain reserved.
- **[the church at the Heraeum](days/d133.html#term-the-church-at-the-heraeum)** — Day 133, Seminar · Art history: Dewing, Buildings I.3.10–15, the Heraeum and the next shrines. In Dewing, Buildings I.3.10–15, the 1940 Loeb translation as posted on LacusCurtius, Justinian, “Also in the Heraeum, which they now call the Hieron, he built a church to the Mother of God which it is not easy to describe.” The same passage then names a church to St.
- **[the Church of Acacius (Dewing, I.4.25–26)](days/d153.html#term-the-church-of-acacius-dewing-i-4-25-26)** — Day 153, Seminar · Art history: the Church of Acacius, from Dewing, Buildings I.4.25–26. In Dewing’s 1940 translation of Procopius, Buildings I.4.25–26, Justinian found the Church of Acacius “fallen into ruin, and he took it down and rebuilt it from the foundations, so as to make it a building of marvellous size.” “It is carried on all sides on columns of astonishing whiteness, and the floor is covered with similar stone, from which such a brilliant light is reflected that it gives the impression that the whole church is coated with snow.” “And two stoas are thrown out in front of it, one of them making a court (peristylos), the other facing the market-place.” “Marvellous” stays inside the quotation.
- **[the church of the Apostles (Dewing, Buildings I.4.9–16)](days/d148.html#term-the-church-of-the-apostles-dewing-buildings-i-4-9-16)** — Day 148, Seminar · Art history: Dewing, Buildings I.4.9–16, the church of the Apostles. In Dewing’s 1940 translation of Procopius, Buildings I.4.9–16, Justinian pulls down the old church of all the Apostles, “shaken by the passage of time,” and rebuilds it “more worthy both in size and in beauty.” “Two straight lines were drawn, intersecting each other at the middle in the form of a cross.” Official lengths are not invented.
- **[the churches of Peter and Paul and of Saints Sergius and Bacchus (Dewing, Buildings I.4.1–8)](days/d143.html#term-the-churches-of-peter-and-paul-and-of-saints-sergius-and-bacchus-dewing-buildings-i-4-1-8)** — Day 143, Seminar · Art history: Dewing, Buildings I.4.1–8, Peter and Paul, and Saints Sergius and Bacchus. In Dewing’s 1940 translation of Procopius, Buildings I.4.1–8, Justinian builds a church of Peter and Paul at the residence once called Hormisdas, later joined to the Palace, and also a shrine of Saints Sergius and Bacchus, with another shrine at an angle.
- **[the coccyx, Gray 1918](days/d170.html#term-the-coccyx-gray-1918)** — Day 170, Seminar · Anatomy: the coccyx, Gray 1918. Gray, Anatomy of the Human Body, 1918, “The Sacral and Coccygeal Vertebræ,” as posted by Theodora.
- **[the eight carpal bones](days/d90.html#term-the-eight-carpal-bones)** — Day 90, Elective · Anatomy: the eight carpal bones, from Gray 1918. Gray, 1918, names eight carpal bones in two rows.
- **[the first thoracic vertebra (Gray, 1918)](days/d150.html#term-the-first-thoracic-vertebra-gray-1918)** — Day 150, Seminar · Anatomy: the first thoracic vertebra, from Gray 1918, a first look. In Gray’s 1918 Anatomy, via Theodora, the first thoracic vertebra “has, on either side of the body, an entire articular facet for the head of the first rib, and a demi-facet for the upper half of the head of the second rib.” “The body is like that of a cervical vertebra, being broad transversely.” Official lengths are not invented, and the lesson is not a diagnosis.
- **[the Flavian amphitheater (the Colosseum)](days/d53.html#term-the-flavian-amphitheater-the-colosseum)** — Day 53, Art history · The Flavian amphitheater (the Colosseum), from Platner. The amphitheater ordinarily known as the Colosseum, in Platner and Ashby’s entry Amphitheatrum Flavium (1929): built by Vespasian on the site of the stagnum of Nero’s domus Aurea; carried to the second arcade and dedicated before Vespasian’s death in 79; Titus added the third and fourth stories and celebrated the dedication of the enlarged building in 80 with games that lasted one hundred days.
- **[the hand, in three parts](days/d45.html#term-the-hand-in-three-parts)** — Day 45, Elective · Artistic anatomy: the hand (carpals, metacarpals, phalanges). A first naming of the hand after last week’s arm (humerus, radius, and ulna): the wrist bones (the carpals), the palm bones (the metacarpals), and the finger bones (the phalanges).
- **[the leading tone in a minor key, a first look](days/d47.html#term-leading-tone-in-a-minor-key)** — Day 47, Music theory · A leading tone in a minor key, and a major V. The seventh scale degree raised by a half step so that it lies a half step below the tonic, often used to build a major V chord in a minor key.
- **[the lumbar vertebrae (Gray, 1918)](days/d160.html#term-the-lumbar-vertebrae-gray-1918)** — Day 160, Seminar · Anatomy: the lumbar vertebrae, from Gray, 1918. In Gray, Anatomy of the Human Body, 1918, “The Lumbar Vertebræ,” the lumbar vertebrae are “the largest segments of the movable part of the vertebral column,” and “can be distinguished by the absence of a foramen in the transverse process, and by the absence of facets on the sides of the body.” The body is “large, wider from side to side than from before backward, and a little thicker in front than behind.” The vertebral foramen is “triangular.” The spinous process is “thick, broad, and somewhat quadrilateral,” and “projects backward.” “Vertebræ” is Gray’s spelling and stays inside the quotation.
- **[the mandible (Gray)](days/d100.html#term-the-mandible-gray)** — Day 100, Elective · Anatomy: the mandible, from Gray 1918. In Henry Gray, Anatomy of the Human Body, 20th ed., 1918, “The Mandible (Lower Jaw),” the mandible is “the largest and strongest bone of the face,” and “serves for the reception of the lower teeth.” “It consists of a curved, horizontal portion, the body, and two perpendicular portions, the rami, which unite with the ends of the body nearly at right angles.” It “articulates with the two temporal bones.” Last week already named it as the movable exception among the twenty-two bones of the skull.
- **[the name ‘Minoan’](days/d13.html#term-minoan-name)** — Day 13, Art history · The Aegean: Cycladic, Minoan, Mycenaean. A modern name, coined by Arthur Evans in the early twentieth century from Minos, the mythical king of Crete, for the Bronze Age culture he was excavating at Knossos.
- **[the rebuilt shrine of Michael (Dewing, Buildings I.3.16–18)](days/d138.html#term-the-rebuilt-shrine-of-michael-dewing-buildings-i-3-16-18)** — Day 138, Seminar · Art history: Dewing, Buildings I.3.16–18, the rebuilt shrine of Michael. In Dewing’s 1940 translation of Procopius, Buildings I.3.16–18, the small, badly lighted shrine of Michael, torn down last week “even to the lowest foundations,” is rebuilt: Justinian, “increasing its size to the proportions which it now displays, … transformed it into a marvellously beautiful building.” The church “is in the form of a rectangle (tetrapleuron), and the length appears not much greater than the width.” Official measurements are not invented.
- **[the sacrum (Gray, 1918)](days/d165.html#term-the-sacrum-gray-1918)** — Day 165, Seminar · Anatomy: the sacrum, from Gray, 1918. In Gray, Anatomy of the Human Body, 1918, “The Sacral and Coccygeal Vertebræ,” “The sacrum is a large, triangular bone, situated in the lower part of the vertebral column and at the upper and back part of the pelvic cavity, where it is inserted like a wedge between the two hip bones; its upper part or base articulates with the last lumbar vertebra, its apex with the coccyx.” It is “curved upon itself and placed very obliquely.” The pelvic surface “is concave from above downward, and slightly so from side to side,” and “its middle part is crossed by four transverse ridges.” “Vertebræ” is Gray’s spelling and stays inside the quotation when that spelling is used.
- **[the sea around Byzantium (Dewing, I.5.1–6)](days/d158.html#term-the-sea-around-byzantium-dewing-i-5-1-6)** — Day 158, Seminar · Art history: the sea around Byzantium, from Dewing, Buildings I.5.1–6. In Dewing’s 1940 translation of Procopius, Buildings I.5.1–6, as posted on LacusCurtius, after naming other shrines “along the inlet which the inhabitants call Ceras,” the writer says he will “presently shew, after first explaining how the sea adorns Byzantium.” The sea “is set most beautifully all about it, forming curving bays, contracting into narrow straits, and spreading into a great open sea,” and “offers the quiet shelter of harbours to navigators.” “There are two seas embracing it, the Aegean on the one side and the sea called the Euxine on the other; these unite with each other to the east of the city.” “Shew” and “harbours” are Dewing’s spellings and stay inside quotations.
- **[the seven tarsal bones](days/d85.html#term-the-seven-tarsal-bones)** — Day 85, Elective · Anatomy: the seven tarsal bones, from Gray 1918. Gray, Anatomy of the Human Body, 1918, states: “The tarsal bones are seven in number, viz., the calcaneus, talus, cuboid, navicular, and the first, second, and third cuneiforms.” Last week taught the tarsus as a group and reserved these names; today the names are taught from that sentence, not guessed.
- **[the seventh cervical vertebra (vertebra prominens)](days/d140.html#term-the-seventh-cervical-vertebra-vertebra-prominens)** — Day 140, Seminar · Artistic anatomy: Gray 1918, the seventh cervical vertebra, a first look. In Gray, Anatomy of the Human Body, 1918, “The Cervical Vertebrae,” the seventh cervical vertebra is distinguished by “a long and prominent spinous process, hence the name vertebra prominens.” The process “is thick, nearly horizontal in direction, not bifurcated, but terminating in a tubercle to which the lower end of the ligamentum nuchæ is attached.” Official lengths remain reserved.
- **[the shrine at Pegê](days/d128.html#term-the-shrine-at-pege)** — Day 128, Seminar · Art history: Dewing, Buildings I.3.6–9, the shrine at Pegê. In Dewing, Buildings I.3.6–9, the 1940 Loeb translation as posted on LacusCurtius, Justinian “dedicated to the Virgin another shrine in the place called Pegê.” The surroundings are a dense grove of cypresses, a flowering meadow, a park of shrubs, and “a spring bubbling silently forth with a gentle stream of sweet water.” Of the church itself Dewing says “it surpasses most shrines both in beauty and in size.” Both this church and last week’s church at Blachernae were erected outside the city-wall, “in order that both of them may serve as invincible defences to the circuit-wall of the city.” Invent no official length, and no official shrine count.
- **[the shrine of the martyr Anthimus, Dewing I.6.9–14](days/d173.html#term-the-shrine-of-the-martyr-anthimus-dewing-i-6-9-14)** — Day 173, Seminar · Art history: the shrine of the martyr Anthimus, Dewing I.6.9–14. Dewing’s 1940 translation of Procopius, Buildings I.6.9–14, as posted on LacusCurtius, public domain in the United States.
- **[the skull, cranium and face (Gray)](days/d95.html#term-the-skull-cranium-and-face-gray)** — Day 95, Elective · Anatomy: the skull, from Gray 1918 — cranium and face. Gray, 1918, writes: “The skull is supported on the summit of the vertebral column, and is of an oval shape, wider behind than in front.
- **[the thoracic vertebrae (Gray, 1918)](days/d145.html#term-the-thoracic-vertebrae-gray-1918)** — Day 145, Seminar · Anatomy: the thoracic vertebrae, from Gray 1918, a first look. In Gray’s 1918 Anatomy, via Theodora, the thoracic vertebrae “are intermediate in size between those of the cervical and lumbar regions; they increase in size from above downward.” They are “distinguished by the presence of facets on the sides of the bodies for articulation with the heads of the ribs, and facets on the transverse processes of all, except the eleventh and twelfth, for articulation with the tubercles of the ribs.” Official lengths are not invented, and the lesson is not a diagnosis.
- **[the twelfth thoracic vertebra (Gray, 1918)](days/d155.html#term-the-twelfth-thoracic-vertebra-gray-1918)** — Day 155, Seminar · Anatomy: the twelfth thoracic vertebra, from Gray, 1918. Gray, Anatomy of the Human Body, 1918, “The Thoracic Vertebræ,” as posted by Theodora, describes the twelfth thoracic vertebra as having “the same general characteristics as the eleventh,” but “distinguished from it by its inferior articular surfaces being convex and directed lateralward, like those of the lumbar vertebræ; by the general form of the body, laminæ, and spinous process, in which it resembles the lumbar vertebræ; and by each transverse process being subdivided into three elevations, the superior, inferior, and lateral tubercles.” “Vertebræ” and “laminæ” stay inside the quotation.
- **[the two hospices and Theodora, reread](days/d113.html#term-the-two-hospices-and-theodora-reread)** — Day 113, Seminar · Art history: Dewing’s two hospices, and Theodora “labouring with him,” reread. A rereading, already used as a seminar method on Dewing, of Buildings I.2.17–19: the two further hospices in the House of Isidorus and the House of Arcadius, “shewing honour to God,” “the Empress Theodora labouring with him,” and Dewing’s refusal to catalogue the remaining shrines.
- **[the unarmed figure and the cross, reread](days/d108.html#term-the-unarmed-figure-and-the-cross-reread)** — Day 108, Seminar · Art history: Dewing’s unarmed figure, reread. Dewing I.2.7–12, already read in this week’s history, is reread today as a question of what the sculptor is made to signify.
- **[the work not with money alone (Dewing)](days/d98.html#term-the-work-not-with-money-alone-dewing)** — Day 98, Elective · Art history: Dewing’s eastern arch, reread. Dewing I.1.67–73, already read in history this week, is reread here as a claim about how the church was made: “not with money alone,” but also “with labour of the mind and with the other powers of the soul.” The story of the eastern arch — the cracked piers, the Emperor’s command to complete the curve, the arch that “hung secure” — is the example he uses to “shew” that claim.
- **[theme and variations](days/d137.html#term-theme-and-variations)** — Day 137, Seminar · Music: theme and variations, a first look. Theme and variations is a form in which a short theme is stated and then repeated with changes, each repetition a variation, while the theme remains recognizable.
- **[third figure](days/d96.html#term-third-figure)** — Day 96, Elective · Logic: the third figure, and Darapti. In a third-figure syllogism, the middle term is the subject of both premises.
- **[three straits and a bay of more than forty stades (Dewing, I.5.7–13)](days/d163.html#term-three-straits-and-a-bay-of-more-than-forty-stades-dewing-i-5-7-13)** — Day 163, Seminar · Art history: three straits and a bay of more than forty stades, from Dewing, Buildings I.5.7–13. In Dewing’s 1940 translation of Procopius, Buildings I.5.7–13, as posted on LacusCurtius, after last week’s account of how the sea adorns Byzantium, the writer describes three straits.
- **[three-year repayment table (invented)](days/d99.html#term-three-year-repayment-table-invented)** — Day 99, Elective · Economics: a three-year repayment table, invented figures. Last week’s simple interest on a declining principal is written today as a three-year table, on invented figures.
- **[tibia](days/d65.html#term-tibia)** — Day 65, Elective · Anatomy: the leg — femur, tibia, and fibula. The shinbone: the inner (medial) and usually the larger of the two long bones of the lower leg, between the knee and the ankle.
- **[trade (classroom model)](days/d24.html#term-trade-classroom)** — Day 24, Economics & personal finance · Specialization and trade (a classroom table). An exchange of goods or tasks in which each side gives up something and receives something.
- **[triad](days/d17.html#term-triad)** — Day 17, Music theory · The triad. A three-note chord built from a root, a third above the root, and a fifth above the root.
- **[triad inversion](days/d62.html#term-triad-inversion)** — Day 62, Music theory · Triad inversion: the third or the fifth in the bass. A change of which triad tone is in the bass: root position has the root in the bass; first inversion has the third in the bass; second inversion has the fifth in the bass.
- **[undistributed middle](days/d36.html#term-undistributed-middle)** — Day 36, Logic · A first look at the undistributed middle. An invalid syllogistic form in which the middle term never refers to the whole of its class in either premise, so it does not actually link the other two terms.
- **[unit price](days/d54.html#term-unit-price)** — Day 54, Economics · Unit price, in an invented shop. The price of one unit, in an invented shop example: total price divided by the number of units (when the units are the same kind).
- **[validity](days/d06.html#term-validity)** — Day 6, Logic · Validity, soundness and two classic fallacies. A property of an argument’s form: the conclusion must be true whenever the premises are true.
- **[vertebral column, from Gray](days/d105.html#term-vertebral-column-from-gray)** — Day 105, Seminar · Artistic anatomy: the vertebral column, from Gray 1918. Gray 1918, in the public-domain text used here from Theodora, describes the vertebral column as formed of 33 vertebræ: 7 cervical, 12 thoracic, 5 lumbar, 5 sacral, and 4 coccygeal.
- **[volta](days/d20.html#term-volta)** — Day 20, Elective · Poetry 2: caesura, enjambment, and the volta. The turn in a sonnet: the place where the argument or the figure shifts.
